Separate Chemistry 2

9.1.1.1 Flame Tests F+H ▼

Flame Tests

Spec 9.1C: Explain why the test for any ion must be unique

Spec 9.2C: Describe flame tests to identify the following ions in solids — Li⁺ (red), Na⁺ (yellow), K⁺ (lilac), Ca²⁺ (orange-red), Cu²⁺ (blue-green)

Flame tests are a simple qualitative method for identifying certain metal cations in solid samples. When a compound containing the ion is held in a hot Bunsen flame, the flame turns a characteristic colour that allows you to identify which metal ion is present.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
Qualitative analysisA practical method used to identify which ion(s) are present in a substance (it tells you what is there, not how much)
CationA positively charged ion, formed when an atom loses one or more electrons (e.g. Li⁺, Na⁺, K⁺, Ca²⁺, Cu²⁺)
Flame testA qualitative test in which a sample is heated in a Bunsen flame; the colour produced identifies the metal ion present
Unique testA test that gives a result for only one ion, so that no other ion can give the same observation and be confused with it

*Why the test for any ion must be unique (Spec 9.1C)*

If two different ions produced the same observation in a test, the test would be useless — you would not know which ion was actually present. A good qualitative test must give a distinctive, single result for one ion only. This is exactly why each of the five flame colours on the specification (red, yellow, lilac, orange-red, blue-green) is different. If lithium and copper both gave a red flame, you could not tell them apart from the flame test alone.

This is also why flame tests are sometimes combined with other tests (such as adding sodium hydroxide solution, which gives different coloured precipitates for different cations). A combination of tests gives you a unique fingerprint for the ion, even when one test on its own is ambiguous.

The Five Flame Colours You Must Learn

Metal IonSymbolFlame Colour
LithiumLi⁺Red (sometimes described as crimson)
SodiumNa⁺Yellow (sometimes described as orange-yellow)
PotassiumK⁺Lilac (sometimes described as pale purple)
CalciumCa²⁺Orange-red
CopperCu²⁺Blue-green (sometimes described as green-blue)

These five ions and their colours are named directly in the specification — you must learn them word-for-word. Examiners only credit the wording on the spec (and very close equivalents). Writing "purple" instead of "lilac" for potassium, or "green" instead of "blue-green" for copper, risks losing the mark.

*Why lithium and calcium can be confused*

Lithium gives a red flame and calcium gives an orange-red flame. These two colours look similar and are the most common cause of mis-identification. Reading the spec precisely — "red" for Li⁺, "orange-red" for Ca²⁺ — helps separate them, but in the lab you sometimes need a follow-up test (e.g. adding sodium hydroxide solution: Ca²⁺ gives a white precipitate; Li⁺ does not).

*Why the sodium flame can mask other colours*

Sodium produces an intense yellow flame. Sodium compounds are almost always present as contaminants on glassware or even on your fingers, which is why thorough cleaning of the wire (see procedure below) is so important. A small amount of sodium contamination can completely hide a fainter lilac potassium flame — this is the textbook reason for viewing potassium flame tests through cobalt blue glass, which filters out the yellow light. (You are not required to remember the cobalt-glass detail for Edexcel GCSE, but it explains why sodium contamination matters.)

The Practical Procedure

This is a core practical for Separate Chemistry. You should be able to describe it step by step:

  1. Take a clean nichrome or platinum wire mounted in a handle. Nichrome and platinum are used because they are unreactive and do not give a flame colour of their own.
  2. Dip the wire in concentrated hydrochloric acid (HCl) to clean it. Concentrated HCl reacts with any metal salt residues on the wire to form metal chlorides, which are volatile and burn off in the flame.
  3. Hold the wire in a hot, non-luminous (blue) Bunsen flame to confirm it is clean. A clean wire should produce no colour change in the flame. If a colour appears, repeat steps 2–3 until no colour is seen.
  4. Dip the cleaned wire back into the concentrated HCl, then into the solid sample. A small amount of solid will stick to the moistened wire.
  5. Hold the loaded wire in the edge of a non-luminous Bunsen flame (the hottest part is the tip of the blue inner cone). Observe the flame colour produced.
  6. Compare the colour to the known flame colours and identify the metal ion present.

*Why each step matters*

StepReason
Clean wire firstAny contaminant ion (especially Na⁺) will give a false colour
Use nichrome/platinumThese metals do not colour the flame themselves, so the colour seen is only from the sample
Use concentrated HClConverts residues to volatile chlorides which burn off; chlorides of test ions are also more volatile than other salts, so the colour appears more strongly
Use a non-luminous (blue) Bunsen flameThe yellow luminous flame would mask the colour produced by the ion; the non-luminous flame is also hotter, giving stronger emission
Use a solid sampleFlame tests on the Edexcel spec are described for solids — the solid is held on the wire so the ion is directly heated in the flame

Why Flame Tests Work — The Underlying Reason

When a metal ion is heated in a flame, the energy from the flame causes electrons in the ion to be excited — they jump from their normal (ground state) energy level to a higher energy level. These excited electrons are unstable, so they quickly fall back down to their original level. As they fall, they release the energy they absorbed, in the form of light.

The colour of the light depends on the size of the energy gap the electron falls through. Different metal ions have different arrangements of electrons in shells, so the energy gaps are different — and the colour of light emitted is different. That is why each metal ion gives a unique flame colour:

  • A lithium ion has a small set of allowed energy gaps that produce light at the red end of the visible spectrum.
  • A copper ion has different energy gaps that produce light in the blue-green region.

At GCSE you are not expected to give quantitative atomic-emission detail (no wavelengths, no specific energy-level diagrams), but you should be able to say:

> "When the metal ion is heated, its electrons absorb energy and are excited to higher energy levels. When the electrons fall back to lower levels, they release energy as light of a characteristic colour."

This explanation links beautifully to your understanding of electron shells from Topic 1 — and to flame emission spectroscopy as an example of an instrumental method (covered in topic 9.1.3).

Limitations of Flame Tests

Flame tests are simple and cheap, but they have important limitations that you should know:

  • Only metal cations are detected. Anions (e.g. Cl⁻, SO₄²⁻, CO₃²⁻) do not produce a flame colour, so flame tests on their own cannot fully identify a salt.
  • Mixtures are difficult. If two metal ions are present, the colour from one (especially sodium) can mask the colour from the other.
  • Some ions are not tested by this method. The Edexcel spec only lists five ions — many other metals also give flame colours, but you are only required to recognise these five.
  • Colour interpretation is subjective. Lithium "red" and calcium "orange-red" can be hard to distinguish reliably by eye.

For these reasons, modern laboratories use flame emission spectroscopy (an instrumental method), which detects the wavelengths of light emitted more precisely and can identify several ions in a mixture at once.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — 2019 June Examiner Report: On a 6-mark question about carrying out qualitative tests for cations, examiners reported that "Clearly, this area of the specification, 'qualitative tests for cations', is one which caused great difficulty for candidates, despite this being a Core practical… Many candidates were unable [to] recall the tests for metal ions let alone the results of the tests. The majority of responses which scored, did so by naming the flame test and a correct result… Very few candidates mentioned or described the use of the precipitation test with sodium hydroxide solution."
  • 1CH0/2F — 2019 June Examiner Report: Examiners noted that candidates often confused tests, "simply describing the tests and results for anions… or describing the reactions and observations for the reactions of the alkali metals with water."

The clear takeaway from examiners is that students lose marks by either (a) not learning the colours, (b) confusing the cation tests with anion tests, or (c) confusing flame tests with the reactivity-of-alkali-metals practical (which also gives flame colours but is a different experiment with a different purpose). Read the question carefully — if it asks about identifying an ion in a solid, it wants the flame test procedure, not "lithium reacts vigorously with water and floats."

How Flame Tests Appear in Exams

Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H)

Flame tests appear extremely frequently on Paper 2 in Separate Chemistry, typically in the mid-to-late sections of the paper. They often appear:

  • As matching / line-drawing questions ("Draw one straight line from each metal ion to its flame colour")
  • As part of larger 6-mark extended response questions identifying an unknown solid or liquid
  • As state, identify or describe questions in qualitative analysis sequences

*Specific examples (from examiner-cited papers):*

  • 1CH0/2F — 2023 June Q3(a)(ii): "Draw one straight line from each metal ion to its flame colour" — 3 marks.
  • 1CH0/2H — 2024 June Q8(c): 6-mark extended response identifying solid R and liquid S from a table of test results. Flame test observations were part of the evidence to be interpreted.

When flame-test results appear in a longer identification question, you are expected to (a) read the observation in the question, (b) match it to the correct ion, and (c) say which ion is present and how you know. The "and how you know" step (linking observation to ion) is usually where the explanation marks are.

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateName the flame colour or ion — no explanation needed1
IdentifyName the metal ion present, using the observation given1
DescribeOutline the procedure or the result, in sequential steps2–4
Draw one straight line from…Match each ion to its correct flame colour1 per correct match
ExplainGive a reason — e.g. why the test must be unique, or why the wire is cleaned first2–3

If you are asked to describe how to carry out a flame test, structure your answer around the practical steps: clean the wire in concentrated HCl, hold it in the Bunsen flame to check no colour appears, dip it in the solid sample, hold it in the non-luminous Bunsen flame, observe the colour. Each clear step is typically worth one mark.

Key Takeaways
  1. A flame test identifies a metal cation in a solid sample by heating it in a Bunsen flame and observing the colour produced.
  2. The five flame colours you must learn from the spec are: Li⁺ red, Na⁺ yellow, K⁺ lilac, Ca²⁺ orange-red, Cu²⁺ blue-green.
  3. The test for any ion must be unique — otherwise two different ions could give the same result and could not be distinguished.
  4. Procedure: clean a nichrome or platinum wire by dipping it in concentrated HCl and heating it in a Bunsen until the flame shows no colour; then dip in solid sample and hold in a non-luminous (blue) Bunsen flame.
  5. Nichrome and platinum are used because they are unreactive and do not colour the flame themselves.
  6. Concentrated HCl cleans the wire by converting residues to volatile chlorides; it also makes the test ion's chloride volatile, giving a stronger colour.
  7. The Bunsen flame must be non-luminous (blue) — a yellow luminous flame would mask the test colour and is cooler.
  8. Flame tests work because the metal ion's electrons are excited to higher energy levels by the heat of the flame, then release energy as light of a characteristic colour when they fall back down.
  9. Common confusion at exam: Li⁺ (red) and Ca²⁺ (orange-red) can look similar — read the spec wording carefully and use a follow-up test (e.g. NaOH (aq)) if needed.
  10. Flame tests detect cations only, not anions; modern labs use flame emission spectroscopy for greater accuracy and to detect mixtures.
  11. From examiner feedback, the most common errors are (a) not learning the colours, (b) confusing cation tests with anion tests, and (c) describing the reactions of alkali metals with water instead of the flame test.
  12. Flame tests are a core practical for Separate Chemistry — Paper 2 questions can ask you to describe the procedure, match ions to colours, or identify an unknown solid from a set of test observations.
9.1.2.1 Tests for Cations F+H ▼

Tests for Cations

Spec 9.3C: Describe tests to identify aluminium, calcium, copper(II), iron(II), iron(III) and ammonium ions in solids or solutions as appropriate, using sodium hydroxide solution.

This is a core practical topic in Separate Chemistry (1CH0) Paper 2. A single reagent — sodium hydroxide solution — is used to identify all six listed cations. Five of them are metal cations that form insoluble metal hydroxide precipitates when sodium hydroxide is added; the ammonium ion is the odd one out, releasing ammonia gas on warming rather than producing a precipitate.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
CationA positively charged ion, formed when an atom loses one or more electrons
PrecipitateAn insoluble solid formed when two solutions are mixed and react
Precipitation reactionA reaction in which two soluble reactants form an insoluble product (the precipitate)
Hydroxide ionOH⁻, the negative ion in sodium hydroxide solution that reacts with metal cations to form insoluble hydroxides
Soluble in excessDescribes a precipitate that dissolves again when more of the added reagent is mixed in

Why sodium hydroxide works as a single-reagent test

When you add sodium hydroxide solution, NaOH (aq), to a solution of a metal salt, the hydroxide ions (OH⁻) from the alkali combine with the metal cations to form a metal hydroxide. Most metal hydroxides are insoluble in water, so they appear as a coloured or white precipitate. Different metal ions give precipitates of different colours, which is why one reagent can distinguish between several cations. The sodium ions (Na⁺) and any spectator anions stay in solution and do not contribute to what you see.

For the ammonium ion (NH₄⁺), the chemistry is different: hydroxide ions react with the ammonium ion to release ammonia gas (NH₃). The reaction needs gentle warming to drive off the gas, and the gas is then identified using damp red litmus paper, which turns blue because ammonia is alkaline.

The Results Table — All Six Cations with Sodium Hydroxide

This table is the single most important piece of recall for this spec point. Learn the colours and the behaviour with excess NaOH (which distinguishes the two white precipitates).

CationObservation on adding NaOH (aq)Behaviour with excess NaOHConfirmatory note
Aluminium, Al³⁺White precipitateDissolves to give a colourless solutionThe "amphoteric" hydroxide — soluble in excess alkali
Calcium, Ca²⁺White precipitateInsoluble — precipitate remainsA flame test (brick-red flame) can confirm Ca²⁺ separately
Copper(II), Cu²⁺Blue precipitateInsoluble — precipitate remainsCu²⁺ solutions are themselves blue; precipitate is a deeper, gelatinous blue
Iron(II), Fe²⁺Green precipitateInsoluble — precipitate remainsSlowly turns brown on standing in air as Fe²⁺ oxidises to Fe³⁺
Iron(III), Fe³⁺Orange-brown (rust-coloured) precipitateInsoluble — precipitate remainsSometimes described as "red-brown" — both are accepted
Ammonium, NH₄⁺No precipitate — gas evolved on warmingN/A — no precipitate to dissolvePungent gas turns damp red litmus paper blue (ammonia is alkaline)

Distinguishing the Two White Precipitates — Al³⁺ vs Ca²⁺

Both aluminium and calcium ions give a white precipitate with a small amount of sodium hydroxide solution. To tell them apart, you must add excess NaOH and watch what happens:

  • Aluminium hydroxide, Al(OH)₃, is amphoteric — it reacts with both acids and alkalis. In excess sodium hydroxide it dissolves to form a colourless solution containing the aluminate ion. The white precipitate disappears entirely.
  • Calcium hydroxide, Ca(OH)₂, is not amphoteric. Adding more NaOH does nothing — the white precipitate stays put.

This is the most reliable way to distinguish them with a single reagent. If a flame test is available, Ca²⁺ also gives a characteristic brick-red flame, while Al³⁺ gives no flame colour.

Ionic Equations for the Metal Hydroxide Precipitations

Higher-tier candidates are routinely asked to support their answer with ionic equations. These show only the species that take part in forming the precipitate; the Na⁺ ions and any spectator anions (Cl⁻, SO₄²⁻, NO₃⁻) are left out.

*Aluminium:*

Al³⁺ (aq) + 3OH⁻ (aq) → Al(OH)₃ (s)

*Calcium:*

Ca²⁺ (aq) + 2OH⁻ (aq) → Ca(OH)₂ (s)

*Copper(II):*

Cu²⁺ (aq) + 2OH⁻ (aq) → Cu(OH)₂ (s)

*Iron(II):*

Fe²⁺ (aq) + 2OH⁻ (aq) → Fe(OH)₂ (s)

*Iron(III):*

Fe³⁺ (aq) + 3OH⁻ (aq) → Fe(OH)₃ (s)

*Ammonium (on warming):*

NH₄⁺ (aq) + OH⁻ (aq) → NH₃ (g) + H₂O (l)

Notice how the number of OH⁻ ions matches the charge on the metal cation — 2+ ions need two hydroxides, 3+ ions need three. Getting the balancing wrong is one of the easiest ways to lose marks on this question type.

How to Carry Out the Test — Practical Method

  1. Place a small volume (about 1 cm³) of the unknown solution into a clean test tube. If you have a solid sample, dissolve a small spatula-tip in distilled water first.
  2. Add sodium hydroxide solution dropwise, swirling gently between drops, and record the initial colour of any precipitate that forms.
  3. If a white precipitate forms, continue adding NaOH until you have a clear excess, and record whether it dissolves (Al³⁺) or remains (Ca²⁺).
  4. If no precipitate forms, warm the mixture gently (do not boil) and hold a piece of damp red litmus paper in the mouth of the tube. If the paper turns blue, the cation is ammonium, NH₄⁺.

Safety note: sodium hydroxide is corrosive — wear eye protection, and ammonia gas should not be inhaled directly.

Why Ammonium Is the Odd One Out

The ammonium ion, NH₄⁺, is not a metal cation — it is a polyatomic cation made of one nitrogen and four hydrogens. Because the bonding inside the ammonium ion is covalent, when it reacts with hydroxide ions it does not produce an insoluble hydroxide. Instead, the hydroxide takes a hydrogen ion from NH₄⁺, leaving neutral ammonia (NH₃), which is a gas at room temperature and escapes from solution on gentle warming. Damp red litmus paper turns blue because ammonia dissolves in the water on the paper to form an alkaline solution.

This is a very common source of confusion: a "test using sodium hydroxide" makes most students expect a precipitate, and they wrongly describe a white solid for NH₄⁺. Train yourself to remember: NH₄⁺ → gas, not a precipitate.

Worked Example — Identifying an Unknown Solid

A pale-green solid X dissolves in distilled water to give a pale-green solution. When sodium hydroxide solution is added dropwise, a green precipitate forms. On standing in air, the precipitate slowly turns brown. Identify the cation in X.

Step 1: The pale-green colour of the solution is a strong clue — Cu²⁺ solutions are blue, Fe³⁺ solutions are yellow-orange, and Fe²⁺ solutions are pale green.

Step 2: A green precipitate with NaOH (aq) matches iron(II) hydroxide, Fe(OH)₂.

Step 3: The slow colour change from green → brown on standing in air is caused by oxidation of Fe(OH)₂ to Fe(OH)₃ — this is itself a confirmatory test for Fe²⁺.

Conclusion: The cation in X is Fe²⁺.

Ionic equation for the test: Fe²⁺ (aq) + 2OH⁻ (aq) → Fe(OH)₂ (s)

Common Pitfalls — Things to Watch For

  • "White precipitate" without qualifying which one. Always say whether it dissolves in excess NaOH to distinguish Al³⁺ from Ca²⁺.
  • Mixing up Fe²⁺ and Fe³⁺ colours. Fe²⁺ → green; Fe³⁺ → orange-brown (or red-brown). The "II" feels lower and so does the green colour on the spectrum — a rough memory aid.
  • Forgetting to warm for the ammonium test. Without warming, NH₃ gas does not come out of solution fast enough to turn litmus paper.
  • Using dry litmus paper. It must be damp — ammonia dissolves in the water on the paper to form the alkali that turns it blue.
  • Confusing ammonium with aluminium because both names start with "Al…/Am…". They behave completely differently — aluminium gives a white precipitate soluble in excess NaOH; ammonium gives no precipitate at all but releases ammonia gas.
  • Writing molecular equations instead of ionic equations when the question asks for ionic. Strip out the spectator ions (Na⁺, Cl⁻, SO₄²⁻ etc.).

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — 2019 June Examiner Report: On a 6-mark question on qualitative tests for cations, examiners wrote: "Clearly, this area of the specification, 'qualitative tests for cations', is one which caused great difficulty for candidates, despite this being a Core practical… Many candidates were unable to recall the tests for metal ions let alone the results of the tests. The majority of responses which scored, did so by naming the flame test and a correct result… Very few candidates mentioned or described the use of the precipitation test with sodium hydroxide solution." The same report noted that some candidates wrongly "describe the tests and results for anions… or the reactions and observations for the reactions of the alkali metals with water."
  • 1CH0/2H — 2019 June Examiner Report: On a 6-mark question identifying three unknown ionic substances (K, L and M), examiners noted: "Knowledge of the tests was good. Candidates found discussing the additional test required to justify the presence of the ammonium ion and the sulfate ions the most difficult… a noticeable proportion of candidates appeared to be confused between ammonium and aluminium ions."

The takeaway from both reports is consistent: students lose marks by not recalling the precipitation test with NaOH at all, by confusing ammonium with aluminium, and by failing to justify their identification with the correct observation. Learn the table above so completely that you can reproduce it under exam conditions.

How Tests for Cations Appears in Exams

Paper: 1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H (Separate Chemistry only). Tests for cations is a core practical topic and a very common centrepiece of mid-to-late questions on Paper 2.

Position and type: Extremely common in Separate Chemistry papers, typically in the mid-to-late sections. Frequently the centrepiece of 6-mark extended-response identification questions where candidates must match observations to ions and justify their reasoning, often with equations on the Higher tier.

*Specific sitting cited in NotebookLM data:*

  • 1CH0/2H — 2024 June, Q8(c) (6 marks): A table of tests on solid R and liquid S — candidates had to identify them and support the answer with equations.

On Foundation tier, expect:

  • Single-cation identification from a stated observation (e.g. "A solution gives a blue precipitate with NaOH — name the cation").
  • Tables to complete linking cations to colours of precipitates.
  • Short-answer "describe the test for…" questions worth 2–3 marks.

On Higher tier, expect:

  • Extended-response 6-mark questions identifying two or three unknown substances from a given table of observations.
  • Ionic equations expected as supporting evidence.
  • Distinguishing Al³⁺ from Ca²⁺ using excess NaOH, sometimes with a follow-up flame test reasoning.
  • Justifying the presence of NH₄⁺ specifically (the most poorly answered part in the 2019 June 2H sitting).

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
DescribeA clear procedure: which reagent, what is observed, any further step needed2–3
IdentifyName the ion (and sometimes the full compound) consistent with the observations1–2
ExplainLink the observation to the underlying chemistry (e.g. amphoteric behaviour of Al(OH)₃)2–4
Give a test for…The reagent + the positive observation + (where needed) a confirmatory step2–3
Write an ionic equationBalanced, with state symbols, showing only the species that take part1–2

---

Key Takeaways
  1. One reagent, six cations: sodium hydroxide solution, NaOH (aq), is the single test reagent for Al³⁺, Ca²⁺, Cu²⁺, Fe²⁺, Fe³⁺ and NH₄⁺.
  2. Five metal cations form insoluble metal hydroxide precipitates with NaOH (aq); only the ammonium ion does not give a precipitate.
  3. Coloured precipitates: Cu²⁺ → blue, Fe²⁺ → green (turns brown on standing in air), Fe³⁺ → orange-brown.
  4. White precipitates: Al³⁺ and Ca²⁺ both give white precipitates — distinguish using excess NaOH: Al(OH)₃ dissolves to give a colourless solution (it is amphoteric); Ca(OH)₂ does not dissolve.
  5. Ammonium test: add NaOH and warm gently; ammonia gas is released, which turns damp red litmus paper blue.
  6. Ionic equations for metal hydroxide precipitations follow the pattern M^n+ + n OH⁻ → M(OH)ₙ — match the number of hydroxides to the charge on the cation.
  7. For NH₄⁺ specifically: NH₄⁺ + OH⁻ → NH₃ + H₂O — and the NH₃ must escape as gas, which is why warming is essential.
  8. Ammonium vs aluminium is a known examiner trap (per the 1CH0/2H June 2019 Examiner Report). Aluminium gives a white precipitate soluble in excess NaOH; ammonium gives no precipitate at all but releases ammonia gas.
  9. Damp red litmus, not dry — ammonia must dissolve in the water on the paper to act as an alkali and turn it blue.
  10. Justify your answer in extended-response identification questions: state the observation, name the cation, and (on Higher tier) write the ionic equation. This was the single biggest mark-loser in the 1CH0/2F June 2019 6-mark question.
  11. Core practical: examiners expect first-hand familiarity with the test procedure — this is one of the practicals that gets repeatedly assessed in Paper 2.
  12. Flame tests can supplement the NaOH test (especially for Ca²⁺ → brick-red flame, Cu²⁺ → blue-green flame), but NaOH precipitation is the spec-required core test for 9.3C.
9.1.3.1 Testing for Ammonia F+H ▼

Testing for Ammonia

Spec 9.4C: Describe the chemical test for ammonia.

Examined on Foundation and Higher tier in Paper 2 of the Separate Chemistry (1CH0) qualification. Combined Science (1SC0) candidates do not sit this content.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
AmmoniaA colourless gas with the formula NH₃; pungent smell; very soluble in water; weakly alkaline in solution
Damp red litmus paperIndicator paper, pre-moistened with water, that turns blue in the presence of an alkaline gas
Ammonium ionA positively charged polyatomic cation, NH₄⁺, found in ammonium salts such as ammonium chloride (NH₄Cl) and ammonium sulfate ((NH₄)₂SO₄)
Confirmatory testA second test used to verify the identity of an ion when an earlier test gives a result that more than one ion could produce

The test — step by step

The chemical test for ammonia gas (NH₃) is short and very specific. You should be able to describe it in two sentences:

  1. Hold a piece of damp red litmus paper in the mouth of the test tube containing the gas.
  2. If the gas is ammonia, the paper turns blue.

The paper must be damp, not dry. Litmus is a water-soluble pH indicator — it only changes colour when the substance being tested is dissolved in the water held in the paper. Dry litmus will not react with the gas because there is no water for the ammonia to dissolve into. This is one of the most common reasons candidates lose the mark: writing "red litmus paper turns blue" without including the word "damp" or "moist".

Why the test works

Ammonia is a weak base. When NH₃ molecules come into contact with the water film on damp litmus paper, a small fraction reacts with the water to form ammonium ions and hydroxide ions:

NH₃ (g) + H₂O (l) ⇌ NH₄⁺ (aq) + OH⁻ (aq)

The hydroxide ions raise the pH of the water on the paper above 7. Litmus indicator changes from red (acid/neutral side) to blue (alkaline side), and the paper visibly turns blue.

This is also why you can smell ammonia even at low concentrations — NH₃ dissolves readily in the moist lining of your nose, producing the characteristic pungent, sharp smell associated with kitchen cleaners, urine left to stand, and smelling salts. Do not deliberately inhale ammonia in the lab; waft a small amount of the gas towards your nose if you need to identify it by smell, and only do so when instructed.

What makes ammonia unique among common gases

Ammonia is the only common gas at GCSE level that is alkaline. Every other gas in the qualitative-analysis section is either neutral (hydrogen, oxygen) or acidic (carbon dioxide, sulfur dioxide, chlorine, hydrogen chloride). This is why the damp red litmus paper test is so reliable: nothing else in the standard syllabus will turn red litmus blue.

GasEffect on damp red litmus paper
Ammonia (NH₃)Turns blue
Hydrogen (H₂)No change (neutral)
Oxygen (O₂)No change (neutral)
Carbon dioxide (CO₂)Slight reddening or no change (weak acid in water)
Chlorine (Cl₂)Bleaches the paper white (decolourises)
Hydrogen chloride (HCl)Turns blue litmus red (acidic)

This selectivity is what makes the test diagnostic — if you see red litmus turn blue, ammonia is essentially the only sensible conclusion at this level.

How to generate ammonia gas in the lab — pairing with the ammonium-ion test

This is the most important practical context for the test, and it is the area where candidates most often lose marks. Ammonia gas does not usually appear in an exam as a bottled, ready-made gas — instead, it is generated from an ammonium salt during the test for the ammonium ion (NH₄⁺).

The procedure is:

  1. Add a small amount of the unknown solid or solution (suspected to contain NH₄⁺) to a test tube.
  2. Add a few cm³ of sodium hydroxide solution (NaOH).
  3. Warm the mixture gently.
  4. Hold damp red litmus paper in the mouth of the test tube.
  5. If the paper turns blue, ammonia gas has been released — confirming the original sample contained ammonium ions.

The chemistry behind this is a base displacement reaction. Sodium hydroxide is a stronger base than ammonia, so it displaces ammonia from its salt:

NH₄Cl (aq) + NaOH (aq) → NaCl (aq) + H₂O (l) + NH₃ (g)

Or in ionic form, applicable to any ammonium salt:

NH₄⁺ (aq) + OH⁻ (aq) → NH₃ (g) + H₂O (l)

You need to warm the mixture because the reaction is slow at room temperature and very little ammonia is released initially. Heat speeds up the reaction and drives the dissolved ammonia out of solution as a gas, where it can reach the litmus paper.

Distinguishing the test for ammonia from the test for ammonium ions

This is a subtle but important distinction that examiners look for.

Test for…What you start withReagent / procedurePositive result
Ammonia gas (NH₃)A sample of gasDamp red litmus paper held in the mouth of the tubeLitmus paper turns blue
Ammonium ion (NH₄⁺)A solid or solution containing the ionAdd NaOH (aq), warm, then hold damp red litmus paper at the mouth of the tubeLitmus paper turns blue (confirming NH₃ has been released, therefore NH₄⁺ was present)

A common error in 6-mark identification questions is to skip the "warm + NaOH" step and simply write "damp red litmus paper turns blue" as the test for ammonium ions — that loses marks because the litmus paper cannot detect the ion itself, only the ammonia gas it produces after reaction with hydroxide.

Ammonium vs aluminium — the classic trap

Both ammonium ions (NH₄⁺) and aluminium ions (Al³⁺) give a white precipitate when sodium hydroxide is added to their solutions. This is why a confirmatory test is needed: the white precipitate alone does not distinguish them.

  • For Al³⁺: adding excess NaOH causes the white precipitate (Al(OH)₃) to dissolve, giving a colourless solution. Aluminium hydroxide is amphoteric — it reacts with both acids and excess alkalis.
  • For NH₄⁺: there is no precipitate of "ammonium hydroxide" in the same sense; instead, warming the mixture releases ammonia gas, which turns damp red litmus paper blue.

When you write up the identification of NH₄⁺ in an exam, you must:

  1. Add NaOH (aq) — observation: may or may not give a precipitate depending on what else is in the sample.
  2. Warm the mixture.
  3. Test the gas given off with damp red litmus paper.
  4. State that it turns blue, confirming ammonia and therefore ammonium ions.

Omitting any of "warm", "damp", "red litmus paper", or "turns blue" risks losing marks.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2H — June 2019 Examiner Report: On a 6-mark question identifying three unknown ionic substances (K, L, and M), candidates' general "knowledge of the tests was good", but "discussing the additional test required to justify the presence of the ammonium ion and the sulfate ions" was the most difficult part. The report also noted that "a noticeable proportion of candidates appeared to be confused between ammonium and aluminium ions." The damp-red-litmus / NaOH-warm test is exactly the confirmatory test that distinguishes them.

This is the highest-value insight to take away from past papers: examiners are explicitly looking for candidates who can distinguish NH₄⁺ from Al³⁺ using the ammonia test, not just describe the colour change.

How Testing for Ammonia Appears in Exams

Paper 2 — Foundation (1CH0/2F)

  • Short-answer recall: "State the test for ammonia gas" or "Describe how you would show that a gas is ammonia" — typically 1–2 marks.
  • The 2 marks are usually split: 1 mark for "damp red litmus paper", 1 mark for "turns blue".
  • May also appear as a multiple-choice item asking which gas turns damp red litmus blue.

Paper 2 — Higher (1CH0/2H)

  • Often embedded inside a larger 5–6 mark qualitative analysis question in which candidates are given several unknown ionic substances and must describe the complete sequence of tests to identify both cations and anions.
  • The ammonia test typically forms the confirmatory test for ammonium ions: candidates need to describe adding NaOH, warming, and testing with damp red litmus paper.
  • Higher-tier questions often require an ionic equation: NH₄⁺ (aq) + OH⁻ (aq) → NH₃ (g) + H₂O (l) — sometimes with state symbols required for the final mark.

The June 2019 6-mark question is the canonical example: candidates needed to distinguish ammonium sulfate from other ionic substances, and the ammonia/NaOH/litmus test was the discriminator.

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateA short factual answer naming the reagent and result1–2
DescribeA sequence: reagent → procedure → observation2–3
IdentifyPick the gas/ion from a list of options based on a described result1
ExplainLink the colour change to the alkalinity of ammonia (less common at GCSE)2

Common mistakes to avoid

  • Forgetting "damp" — "Red litmus paper turns blue" without "damp" or "moist" is incomplete. The water on the paper is required for the indicator to respond.
  • Wrong colour of litmus — Blue litmus does not work here (it would already be blue). The test specifically uses red litmus, and the change is red → blue.
  • Skipping the warming step when testing NH₄⁺ — at room temperature very little ammonia is released; warming is essential to drive the gas off.
  • Confusing the ion with the gas — the litmus paper detects only the gas. Inside a solid or solution containing NH₄⁺, no ammonia is yet free; you have to react with NaOH and warm to generate it.
  • Confusing ammonium with aluminium — both give white precipitates with NaOH; only ammonium releases ammonia gas on warming.
Key Takeaways
  1. The test for ammonia gas is: hold damp red litmus paper in the mouth of the tube; if NH₃ is present, the paper turns blue.
  2. The paper must be damp because litmus only changes colour when the substance being tested can dissolve in the water film on the paper.
  3. The colour change happens because NH₃ is a weak base — it dissolves in the water on the paper, forming NH₄⁺ and OH⁻ ions and raising the pH above 7.
  4. Ammonia is the only common gas at GCSE that turns damp red litmus paper blue — this makes the test diagnostic.
  5. To test for the ammonium ion (NH₄⁺), add sodium hydroxide solution, warm gently, then hold damp red litmus paper in the mouth of the tube — if it turns blue, NH₄⁺ was present.
  6. The ionic equation for the reaction generating ammonia is: NH₄⁺ (aq) + OH⁻ (aq) → NH₃ (g) + H₂O (l). Warming is needed to drive off the gas.
  7. Ammonium (NH₄⁺) and aluminium (Al³⁺) both give white precipitates with NaOH — the ammonia/warm/litmus test is the confirmatory test that distinguishes them.
  8. In Paper 2 Higher, this content typically appears inside a 5–6 mark qualitative analysis question; the June 2019 paper highlighted ammonium/aluminium confusion as a common error.
  9. Use the command word as a guide: "State" wants the reagent and result (1–2 marks); "Describe" wants the full procedure including warming (2–3 marks).
  10. The most common missed marks are: forgetting "damp", forgetting to warm with NaOH, and writing "red litmus paper" without specifying the colour change red → blue.
9.1.4.1 Tests for Anions F+H ▼

Tests for Anions

Spec 9.5C: Describe tests to identify carbonate, sulfate, and halide ions in solids or solution.

Foundation + Higher · Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only · Paper 2

Qualitative analysis is about identifying which ions are present in an unknown ionic compound. Cation tests (flame tests, sodium hydroxide tests) identify the positive ion; anion tests identify the negative ion. You need to know three anion tests in detail: carbonate, sulfate, and halides. Each test produces a clear, observable result — a gas, a coloured precipitate, or a colour change — and each test has a specific acidification step that you must explain.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
AnionA negatively charged ion, formed when a non-metal atom gains one or more electrons
Qualitative analysisChemical tests used to identify which ions are present in a substance (as opposed to quantitative analysis, which measures how much)
PrecipitateAn insoluble solid formed when two soluble solutions are mixed
EffervescenceThe release of gas bubbles from a liquid during a chemical reaction
LimewaterA dilute solution of calcium hydroxide, Ca(OH)₂ (aq) — used to test for carbon dioxide
AcidificationAdding a dilute acid before the main test reagent to remove interfering ions

The Three Anion Tests — Summary Table

AnionTest ReagentsPositive ResultConfirms
Carbonate, CO₃²⁻Add dilute hydrochloric acid; pass any gas through limewaterEffervescence; limewater turns cloudy/milkyCO₂ evolved → carbonate present
Sulfate, SO₄²⁻Acidify with dilute hydrochloric acid; then add barium chloride solutionWhite precipitateBaSO₄ → sulfate present
Chloride, Cl⁻Acidify with dilute nitric acid; then add silver nitrate solutionWhite precipitateAgCl → chloride present
Bromide, Br⁻Acidify with dilute nitric acid; then add silver nitrate solutionCream precipitateAgBr → bromide present
Iodide, I⁻Acidify with dilute nitric acid; then add silver nitrate solutionYellow precipitateAgI → iodide present

Test 1 — Carbonate Ion (CO₃²⁻)

*Method:*

  1. Place a small amount of the solid (or solution) in a test tube
  2. Add a few drops of dilute hydrochloric acid (or dilute nitric acid)
  3. If bubbles are produced, collect the gas and pass it through limewater (Ca(OH)₂ (aq))
  4. If the limewater turns cloudy/milky, the gas is carbon dioxide — confirming a carbonate

*The chemistry behind the test*

Carbonates react with dilute acids to release carbon dioxide gas. For a generic metal carbonate MCO₃:

MCO₃ (s) + 2HCl (aq) → MCl₂ (aq) + H₂O (l) + CO₂ (g)

Ionic equation for the gas-producing step:

CO₃²⁻ (aq) + 2H⁺ (aq) → H₂O (l) + CO₂ (g)

The carbon dioxide is then identified by the limewater test:

Ca(OH)₂ (aq) + CO₂ (g) → CaCO₃ (s) + H₂O (l)

The cloudiness is a fine suspension of insoluble calcium carbonate.

*Why effervescence alone is not enough*

Effervescence only tells you that a gas has been released — not which gas. Several reactions can produce bubbles (e.g. a reactive metal with acid releases hydrogen). The limewater step is what confirms the gas is CO₂ and therefore that a carbonate was present. If a question asks you to "describe the test" for a carbonate, you must include both stages — adding acid AND passing the gas through limewater — or you will lose a mark.

Test 2 — Sulfate Ion (SO₄²⁻)

*Method:*

  1. Dissolve the solid in water (if not already in solution)
  2. Add a few drops of dilute hydrochloric acid to acidify the solution
  3. Add a few drops of barium chloride solution, BaCl₂ (aq)
  4. A white precipitate confirms the presence of sulfate ions

*The chemistry behind the test*

Barium ions react with sulfate ions to form barium sulfate, which is highly insoluble and appears as a fine white precipitate:

Ba²⁺ (aq) + SO₄²⁻ (aq) → BaSO₄ (s)

Full balanced equation (using sodium sulfate as the example):

BaCl₂ (aq) + Na₂SO₄ (aq) → BaSO₄ (s) + 2NaCl (aq)

*Why acidify with dilute HCl first?*

This is one of the highest-value "explain" points in the topic. The acidification step removes any carbonate ions that may be present in the unknown solution. Carbonate would otherwise produce a false positive, because barium carbonate (BaCO₃) is also a white insoluble precipitate. Without acidification, you could not tell whether the white precipitate was BaSO₄ (real sulfate) or BaCO₃ (carbonate interference).

Adding dilute hydrochloric acid converts any carbonate to CO₂ gas, which leaves the solution:

CO₃²⁻ (aq) + 2H⁺ (aq) → H₂O (l) + CO₂ (g)

Once the carbonate is gone, any white precipitate that forms when barium chloride is added must be BaSO₄.

*Why HCl rather than another acid?*

Dilute hydrochloric acid is chosen because it does not introduce any ion that would interfere with the sulfate test — the only spectator ion added is Cl⁻, which does not react with Ba²⁺. If sulfuric acid were used, it would itself contain sulfate ions and ruin the test. If nitric acid were used, no harm would be done — but HCl is the specification choice for the sulfate test, so use it.

Test 3 — Halide Ions (Cl⁻, Br⁻, I⁻)

*Method:*

  1. Dissolve the solid in water (if not already in solution)
  2. Add a few drops of dilute nitric acid to acidify the solution
  3. Add a few drops of silver nitrate solution, AgNO₃ (aq)
  4. Observe the colour of the precipitate:

- White precipitate → chloride (Cl⁻) - Cream precipitate → bromide (Br⁻) - Yellow precipitate → iodide (I⁻)

*The chemistry behind the test*

Silver ions react with halide ions to form insoluble silver halides:

Ag⁺ (aq) + Cl⁻ (aq) → AgCl (s) — white

Ag⁺ (aq) + Br⁻ (aq) → AgBr (s) — cream

Ag⁺ (aq) + I⁻ (aq) → AgI (s) — yellow

Full balanced equation (using sodium chloride as the example):

AgNO₃ (aq) + NaCl (aq) → AgCl (s) + NaNO₃ (aq)

*The colour ladder — a classic 1-mark question*

The three silver halide precipitates form a colour ladder that you must learn precisely:

HalidePrecipitate FormulaColour
Chloride, Cl⁻AgClWhite
Bromide, Br⁻AgBrCream
Iodide, I⁻AgIYellow

A common mistake is to call the bromide precipitate "off-white" or "pale yellow" — neither will score. The accepted descriptor is cream. The colour deepens going down the group: white → cream → yellow. If you cannot distinguish white from cream confidently, knowing this ordering helps — bromide always sits between chloride and iodide in shade.

*Why acidify with dilute HNO₃ first?*

The reason is identical in principle to the sulfate test: to remove interfering ions before the halide reagent is added. Specifically, the dilute nitric acid removes any carbonate ions in the solution. If carbonate were present, it would react with silver nitrate to form silver carbonate (Ag₂CO₃) — which is also a pale-coloured precipitate and would give a misleading positive result. Acidification converts carbonate into CO₂ gas:

CO₃²⁻ (aq) + 2H⁺ (aq) → H₂O (l) + CO₂ (g)

Once the carbonate is gone, any precipitate formed on adding silver nitrate must be a silver halide.

*Why HNO₃ rather than HCl for the halide test?*

This is the critical pairing to learn. You cannot use hydrochloric acid here because HCl contains chloride ions — those chloride ions would themselves react with silver nitrate to form a white AgCl precipitate, giving a false positive for chloride every time. Dilute nitric acid is used because the nitrate ion does not react with silver ions to form an insoluble compound (AgNO₃ is soluble), so it cannot cause a false positive.

Why the Acidification Step Matters — The Big Picture

The acidification step is the single most important "explain" point in this topic. The logic is the same in both the sulfate test and the halide test:

  1. Carbonate ions might be present in the unknown
  2. Carbonate would react with the test reagent (Ba²⁺ or Ag⁺) to form a white-ish precipitate
  3. This would give a false positive — you would not know whether you had sulfate/halide or carbonate
  4. Adding dilute acid first converts any carbonate to CO₂ gas, which leaves the solution
  5. Now any precipitate that forms when the test reagent is added must be the real positive result

The choice of acid is also important and follows a clear rule: never use an acid whose anion would itself give a positive result with the test reagent.

TestAcid UsedWhy That Acid
SulfateDilute HClChloride doesn't form a precipitate with Ba²⁺
HalidesDilute HNO₃Nitrate doesn't form a precipitate with Ag⁺

You should never use dilute H₂SO₄ for the sulfate test (it contains the very ion you're testing for) and you should never use dilute HCl for the halide test (it would give a false positive for chloride).

Order of Testing — Practical Sequence

If you are given an unknown ionic solution and asked to identify the anion, a logical order is:

  1. Carbonate first — add dilute acid and look for effervescence. If no fizzing, no carbonate.
  2. Sulfate next — to a fresh sample, add dilute HCl then BaCl₂. A white precipitate = sulfate.
  3. Halide last — to a fresh sample, add dilute HNO₃ then AgNO₃. Observe the colour of any precipitate.

Always use a fresh sample for each test — otherwise residues from the first test will interfere with later ones.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — 2019 June Examiner Report: On a 6-mark question testing qualitative tests for cations, candidates often went off-topic by "simply describing the tests and results for anions" — indicating that anion tests are familiar enough to candidates to be confidently recalled, but that students must read the question carefully and answer the cation/anion that is actually asked.
  • 1CH0/2H — 2019 June Examiner Report: On a 6-mark question identifying three unknown ionic substances (K, L, and M), examiners reported that "Candidates found discussing the additional test required to justify the presence of the ammonium ion and the sulfate ions the most difficult." — emphasising that for sulfate, the acidification step is often the part candidates miss.

How Tests for Anions Appears in Exams

*Paper 2 (Separate Chemistry only):*

This SP is examined exclusively on Separate Chemistry papers (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H), in the mid-to-late section of the paper. It is very frequently bundled into larger identification questions, often worth 6 marks, where:

  • The first half of the evidence is a cation test (e.g. flame test, NaOH test)
  • The second half is an anion test (e.g. BaCl₂ for sulfate, AgNO₃ for halides)

*Specific sitting cited:*

  • 1CH0/2H, 2024 June, Q8(c): 6-mark identification of solid R and liquid S — anion tests (silver nitrate for halides, barium chloride for sulfate) feature among the test-result rows that candidates must interpret.

Standalone anion-test questions also appear as 2- or 3-mark "describe the test for the X ion" items, which require you to state the reagent(s), the procedure, and the observed positive result.

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
DescribeReagent + procedure + observation (positive result)2–3
IdentifyName the ion present, based on given test results1
StateA short factual answer — colour of precipitate, name of gas, etc.1
ExplainThe reason behind the procedure (e.g. why acidify first)2
Key Takeaways
  1. The carbonate test is: add dilute acid → effervescence → pass gas through limewater → cloudy/milky confirms CO₂.
  2. The sulfate test is: acidify with dilute HCl, then add barium chloride → white precipitate of BaSO₄ confirms sulfate. Ionic equation: Ba²⁺ + SO₄²⁻ → BaSO₄.
  3. The halide test is: acidify with dilute nitric acid, then add silver nitrate → coloured precipitate confirms the halide.
  4. The silver halide colour ladder: AgCl is white, AgBr is cream, AgI is yellow — learn these descriptors exactly; "off-white" and "pale yellow" for bromide are not accepted.
  5. Acidification removes carbonate — without it, carbonate would form a white precipitate with Ba²⁺ or Ag⁺ and give a false positive.
  6. Use HCl for the sulfate test (chloride doesn't interfere with Ba²⁺), and HNO₃ for the halide test (nitrate doesn't interfere with Ag⁺).
  7. Never use H₂SO₄ for the sulfate test (it contains sulfate) and never use HCl for the halide test (it contains chloride — false positive).
  8. For the carbonate test, effervescence alone is not enough — you must include the limewater step to confirm the gas is CO₂.
  9. Use a fresh sample for each anion test to avoid residues from earlier tests interfering with results.
  10. Anion tests usually appear in 6-mark identification questions on Paper 2, paired with cation tests — read carefully so you answer the correct half of the question.
  11. The ionic equation for the carbonate-acid reaction is: CO₃²⁻ + 2H⁺ → H₂O + CO₂.
  12. Examiners specifically flagged the acidification step in the sulfate test as the part candidates most often forget to mention when justifying their identification.
9.1.5.1 Core Practical: Identifying Ions F+H ▼

Core Practical: Identifying Ions

Spec 9.6C / 9.7C: Identify the ions in unknown salts, using the tests for the specified cations and anions in 9.2C–9.5C, and using the results of the tests above.

This Core Practical is the synthesis SP of Topic 9.1 — it does not introduce new tests, but expects you to combine the tests from 9.1.1.1 (flame tests), 9.1.2.1 (NaOH for cations), 9.1.3.1 (halides, sulfates, carbonates) and 9.1.4.1 (ammonium) into a systematic procedure for identifying the ions in an unknown ionic compound.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
Qualitative analysisA practical technique that identifies which ions are present in a sample, but not how much of each is present
CationA positively charged ion — formed when an atom loses one or more electrons
AnionA negatively charged ion — formed when an atom (or group of atoms) gains one or more electrons
PrecipitateAn insoluble solid formed when two soluble reagents react in solution
Unknown saltAn ionic compound (cation + anion) whose identity must be deduced from test results

The systematic order of tests

When a single sample of unknown salt is presented, the order in which you run tests matters — both because some tests interfere with later tests, and because the flame colour is a quick, non-destructive screen before you commit a sample to wet chemistry.

StepTestEvidence For
1Flame test on a small amount of the solidLithium, sodium, potassium, calcium, copper(II) cations
2Dissolve a fresh sample in distilled water, then add a few drops of sodium hydroxide solutionAluminium, calcium, copper(II), iron(II), iron(III), ammonium cations (by precipitate colour)
3If a white precipitate forms with NaOH, warm gently and test the gas with damp red litmus paperConfirms NH₄⁺ (litmus turns blue) vs Al³⁺ (no gas)
4On a fresh sample, run the anion tests in turn: carbonate (acid + limewater), sulfate (acid + barium chloride), halide (acid + silver nitrate)Carbonate, sulfate, chloride, bromide, iodide anions

*Why test for cations first*

The flame test only needs a clean nichrome wire dipped in concentrated HCl and a tiny scrape of the solid — it does not consume the sample and it does not put a foreign ion into the mixture. Wet tests for the anion all add HCl, HNO₃ or BaCl₂ to the solution, which contaminate it with chloride or sulfate from the reagent. If you ran the anion test first you would have no clean solution left for the cation test.

*Why use a fresh sample for each wet test*

Once you have added BaCl₂ to a portion, the solution now contains Cl⁻ from the reagent — so testing the same portion for halides with AgNO₃ would always give a false positive (white precipitate of AgCl). Always start a new portion of dissolved salt for each anion test.

Choice of acid: why HCl for some tests and HNO₃ for others

The acid added at the start of each anion test is not just to acidify — it is chosen specifically to avoid interfering with the test reagent that follows.

TestAcid usedWhy this acid (and not the others)
CarbonateDilute HCl (or dilute HNO₃)Reacts with CO₃²⁻ to release CO₂, which turns limewater milky
SulfateDilute HClRemoves carbonate impurity. HNO₃ would also work, but HCl is the standard reagent. Cannot use H₂SO₄ — it would itself provide SO₄²⁻ ions, giving a false positive
Halides (Cl⁻, Br⁻, I⁻)Dilute HNO₃Removes carbonate impurity without adding chloride. Cannot use HCl — it would add Cl⁻ to the mixture, giving a false positive with AgNO₃. Cannot use H₂SO₄ — concentrated H₂SO₄ oxidises Br⁻ and I⁻ to bromine and iodine, destroying the ions you are trying to test for

The reason for adding acid in the first place is to react with any carbonate present in the salt or in the water. Carbonate would otherwise give a white precipitate with both BaCl₂ (BaCO₃) and AgNO₃ (Ag₂CO₃), masking a true sulfate or halide result.

Worked example 1 — identifying iron(II) sulfate

A student is given a pale green crystalline solid and runs the following tests.

TestObservation
Flame testNo distinctive colour seen
Solid dissolved in distilled water, NaOH addedGreen precipitate forms
Fresh sample dissolved in water, dilute HCl added, then BaCl₂ addedWhite precipitate forms
Fresh sample dissolved in water, dilute HNO₃ added, then AgNO₃ addedNo precipitate forms

*Deductions:*

  • No flame colour rules out lithium (red), sodium (yellow), potassium (lilac), calcium (orange-red) and copper(II) (blue-green). This is consistent with a transition metal that has no characteristic flame colour, or a cation tested better by NaOH.
  • Green precipitate with NaOH → iron(II) (Fe²⁺). The chemistry: Fe²⁺ (aq) + 2OH⁻ (aq) → Fe(OH)₂ (s).
  • White precipitate with acidified BaCl₂ → sulfate (SO₄²⁻). The chemistry: Ba²⁺ (aq) + SO₄²⁻ (aq) → BaSO₄ (s).
  • No precipitate with acidified AgNO₃ → no halide present, which is consistent with sulfate being the anion.

Identity: iron(II) sulfate, FeSO₄.

The pale green colour of the original solid is itself a clue (Fe²⁺ compounds are commonly pale green), but in the exam you must always justify your answer with test results, not with colour alone.

Worked example 2 — identifying copper(II) chloride

A student is given a blue-green crystalline solid.

TestObservation
Flame testBlue-green flame
Solid dissolved in distilled water, NaOH addedBlue precipitate forms
Fresh sample dissolved in water, dilute HNO₃ added, then AgNO₃ addedWhite precipitate forms
White precipitate from above tested with dilute and concentrated ammoniaDissolves in dilute ammonia

*Deductions:*

  • Blue-green flame → copper(II) (Cu²⁺).
  • Blue precipitate with NaOH → confirms Cu²⁺. The chemistry: Cu²⁺ (aq) + 2OH⁻ (aq) → Cu(OH)₂ (s).
  • White precipitate with acidified AgNO₃ → halide present. Dissolves in dilute ammonia → chloride (Cl⁻). The chemistry: Ag⁺ (aq) + Cl⁻ (aq) → AgCl (s).

Identity: copper(II) chloride, CuCl₂.

Note how the flame test and the NaOH test agree — both point to Cu²⁺. In a 6-mark question, examiners look for this kind of cross-checking, because relying on a single positive result is unsafe (e.g. impurities can mimic flame colours).

Handling ambiguities and mixtures

*Ammonium vs aluminium*

Both NH₄⁺ and Al³⁺ give a white precipitate with NaOH at room temperature (or, in the ammonium case, no precipitate but a colourless solution — the only way to distinguish is to gently warm the mixture). On warming:

  • Ammonium: NH₃ gas is released. Hold damp red litmus paper at the mouth of the test tube — it turns blue. The chemistry: NH₄⁺ (aq) + OH⁻ (aq) → NH₃ (g) + H₂O (l).
  • Aluminium: no gas released. The white Al(OH)₃ precipitate also dissolves in excess NaOH (forming a colourless aluminate solution), whereas calcium hydroxide does not.

*Calcium vs aluminium*

Both can give a white precipitate with NaOH. Distinguish with the flame test (calcium gives an orange-red flame; aluminium gives no characteristic flame colour) or by adding excess NaOH (Al(OH)₃ dissolves; Ca(OH)₂ does not).

*Mixtures of halides*

If both Cl⁻ and Br⁻ are present, the AgNO₃ test will give a precipitate that is a mixture of white AgCl and cream AgBr — the apparent colour may look "off-white" or "very pale cream". Use the ammonia confirmation:

  • AgCl dissolves in dilute ammonia
  • AgBr dissolves only in concentrated ammonia
  • AgI does not dissolve in concentrated ammonia

*Mixtures of cations*

If both Fe²⁺ and Fe³⁺ are present, the NaOH precipitate will appear as a mixture of green and orange-brown solids, often described as "muddy" or "brown-green". This is rare in Foundation-tier questions but appears occasionally at Higher tier.

Risks and safety considerations

HazardRiskControl
Silver nitrate solutionStains skin and clothing dark brown/black on contact (light-sensitive reaction)Wear gloves and a lab coat; wipe spills immediately
Dilute HCl, HNO₃, NaOHIrritant to skin and eyesWear safety goggles; rinse spills with water
Concentrated ammoniaToxic vapour; respiratory irritantUse in a fume cupboard or well-ventilated area; keep stoppered when not in use
Limewater (calcium hydroxide solution)Irritant to eyesGoggles
Heating salts in a flameHot apparatus; risk of burnsAllow nichrome wire to cool before redipping; tie back long hair
Barium compoundsToxic if ingested (soluble barium salts)Wash hands after the practical; do not pipette by mouth

*Why H₂SO₄ is not used with halide tests*

Sulfuric acid is a stronger oxidising acid than HCl or HNO₃. Concentrated H₂SO₄ will oxidise bromide ions to bromine (orange-brown fumes) and iodide ions to iodine (purple vapour and a brown solution). This destroys the very ions you are trying to test for, so HNO₃ is used instead.

Worked example 3 — identifying an unknown sample R

A student tests an unknown white solid R.

TestObservation
Flame test on RYellow flame
Dissolve R in distilled water, add NaOH and warm gentlyPungent gas released that turns damp red litmus paper blue
Dissolve fresh R in water, add dilute HNO₃ then AgNO₃No precipitate
Dissolve fresh R in water, add dilute HCl then BaCl₂White precipitate forms

*Deductions:*

  • Yellow flame → sodium (Na⁺) is present.
  • Pungent gas turning damp red litmus blue → ammonium (NH₄⁺) is also present.
  • This means R contains two cations.
  • No precipitate with AgNO₃ → no halide.
  • White precipitate with acidified BaCl₂ → sulfate (SO₄²⁻) is the anion.

Identity: sodium ammonium sulfate (a mixed salt containing both Na⁺ and NH₄⁺ cations with SO₄²⁻). Two cations sharing one anion is unusual but possible — and the test sequence reveals it because flame and NaOH gas tests are independent and both come back positive.

This shows why a table of test results plus a clear deduction chain is the structure examiners expect in 6-mark identify questions.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — June 2019 Examiner Report: On a 6-mark question about carrying out qualitative tests for cations, examiners commented that "Clearly, this area of the specification, 'qualitative tests for cations', is one which caused great difficulty for candidates, despite this being a Core practical." The implication: this is a Core Practical that is frequently under-prepared, even at Foundation tier.
  • Because 9.1.5.1 is the wrapper Core Practical, the candidate-error commentary recorded against the individual cation/anion test SPs (9.1.1.1–9.1.4.1) applies here in combination. The most common failure modes are: confusing flame colours (especially calcium orange-red vs lithium red), misnaming precipitate colours from NaOH (e.g. writing "blue" for Fe²⁺ instead of green), and choosing the wrong acid for the anion test.

How Core Practical: Identifying Ions appears in exams

This SP is the showpiece extended-response question of Topic 9.1. It typically appears as a 6-mark part-question on Paper 2 (1CH0/2F or 1CH0/2H), in which a table of test results is given for two or three unknown ionic substances and you must identify each one and justify the identification with reference to the observations and/or equations.

*Canonical past-paper appearances:*

  • 1CH0/2H — June 2024, Q8(c): a table of tests on a solid R and a liquid S. Candidates had to identify both and support each answer with ionic equations (6 marks).
  • 1CH0/2H — June 2019: a 6-mark extended-response question on three unknown ionic substances K, L and M. This is the canonical past-paper appearance for this Core Practical — examiners noted it caused great difficulty for candidates.

The standard structure of such a question:

  1. A short stem giving the unknown samples (labelled by letter — R, S, K, L, M, etc.)
  2. A table of test results with rows = tests carried out, columns = each unknown sample
  3. A single command sentence: "Identify the ions in each substance. Justify your answer with reference to the observations [and/or write balanced equations for the precipitation reactions]."

Command words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
IdentifyName the cation and anion present1 mark per ion (or per substance)
DescribeOutline what you would observe — colours, gases, precipitates2–4
ExplainLink the observation to the chemistry (which ion produced which result)3–6
JustifyGive the test result that confirms each identification — observation plus the ion it indicatesFull-question (often 6)
SuggestPropose a test that has not been pre-given, when the results allow more than one possibility1–2

How to score full marks on the 6-mark synthesis question

  1. Read the whole table first before writing anything — don't pick the first row and commit to a cation, then realise a later row contradicts it.
  2. Identify each ion with one piece of evidence, written as "observation → ion". For example: "Green precipitate with NaOH → Fe²⁺".
  3. Match cation to anion to give the full salt name (e.g., iron(II) sulfate, not just "iron(II)" and "sulfate" separately).
  4. Add balanced ionic equations where the question asks for them — every Higher-tier 6-mark identify question expects at least one equation. Standard examples to memorise: Cu²⁺ + 2OH⁻ → Cu(OH)₂; Ba²⁺ + SO₄²⁻ → BaSO₄; Ag⁺ + Cl⁻ → AgCl.
  5. Cross-check with a second test where possible — if both the flame test and the NaOH test point to Cu²⁺, say so. This earns the higher communication mark on a levels-of-response question.
Key Takeaways
  1. This Core Practical synthesises the four ion-test SPs (9.1.1.1 flame tests, 9.1.2.1 NaOH for cations, 9.1.3.1 halide/sulfate/carbonate tests, 9.1.4.1 ammonium test) into one systematic procedure.
  2. The standard order of tests is: flame test → NaOH for cations → warm-with-NaOH for ammonium → anion tests on fresh sample portions.
  3. Always use a fresh sample of the unknown solution for each wet test — adding BaCl₂ contaminates the mixture with chloride, and adding AgNO₃ would then give a false positive.
  4. HCl is used to acidify the sulfate test (it cannot use H₂SO₄, which would itself provide SO₄²⁻ ions); HNO₃ is used to acidify the halide test (HCl would add Cl⁻; H₂SO₄ would oxidise Br⁻ and I⁻).
  5. Ammonium vs aluminium — both can appear to give a white precipitate or no precipitate with NaOH; distinguish by warming and testing the gas with damp red litmus paper (ammonia turns it blue; aluminium gives no gas).
  6. Halide mixtures are resolved by dissolving the silver halide precipitate in ammonia: AgCl dissolves in dilute ammonia; AgBr dissolves only in concentrated ammonia; AgI does not dissolve in concentrated ammonia.
  7. Silver nitrate is the most hazardous reagent in this practical — it stains skin and clothing dark brown on contact, so wear gloves and a lab coat.
  8. The exam appearance is consistently a 6-mark extended-response question on Paper 2 in which a table of test results must be interpreted to identify two or three unknown ionic substances — 1CH0/2H June 2024 Q8(c) and 1CH0/2H June 2019 are the canonical past-paper examples.
  9. Justify identifications with observations — every named ion must be tied to a specific row of the table (e.g. "blue precipitate with NaOH → Cu²⁺"), and at Higher tier balanced ionic equations are expected.
  10. The June 2019 1CH0/2F examiner report flagged that this Core Practical causes great difficulty for candidates despite being a Core Practical — so preparing the test results, colours and reagent choices thoroughly is a high-value revision priority.
  11. Cross-checking improves reliability — when flame test and NaOH test agree (e.g. both indicate Cu²⁺), state the agreement to score the communication mark on a 6-mark question.
  12. The flame test is the quick, non-destructive screen that should always go first — it uses a tiny scrape of solid and does not contaminate the sample for any later wet test.
9.1.6.1 Advantages of Instrumental Methods F+H ▼

Advantages of Instrumental Methods

Spec point 9.8C: Describe that instrumental methods of analysis are available and that these may improve sensitivity, accuracy and speed of tests.

Tier: Foundation and Higher · Qualification: Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only · Paper 2.

The Edexcel specification is unusually explicit on this spec point: no knowledge of the instrument or how it works is required. The exam will never ask you to label the inside of a flame photometer, draw a diagram of a mass spectrometer, or describe the optics of a UV-visible spectrophotometer. Instead, what you must know is why instrumental methods are used in modern analytical chemistry in preference to traditional chemical tests — and that "why" comes down to three benefits: sensitivity, accuracy, and speed. Learn the three advantages by name, learn what each one means, and learn at least one concrete example of how an instrumental method beats a traditional test on each measure.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
Instrumental methodAn analytical technique that uses a machine (an "instrument") to detect, identify, or measure the concentration of a substance, rather than relying on a chemical reaction observed by eye
Traditional chemical testAn analytical method that depends on a visible chemical change — colour change, precipitate, gas evolved, flame colour — observed by the human eye
SensitivityThe smallest amount or lowest concentration of a substance that a method can reliably detect
AccuracyHow close a measured value is to the true value of the quantity being measured
SpeedHow quickly a single sample, or a large batch of samples, can be analysed

Why Instrumental Methods Are Used

*The three named advantages: sensitivity, accuracy and speed*

These three words are the exam content. If a question asks you to "state three advantages of instrumental methods over traditional chemical tests", these are the three words that score the marks. Each one needs a short justification you can write in one sentence.

*Sensitivity — detecting tiny amounts*

An instrument can detect ions or molecules at concentrations far below the level at which a human eye can see a colour, a precipitate, or a flame colour. A flame photometer, for example, can detect a metal ion such as sodium or potassium at parts-per-million (or even parts-per-billion) levels in a sample. A traditional flame test on the same dilute solution would simply show no flame colour at all — there is not enough metal present to give a visible response. This matters because real-world samples in environmental monitoring, food testing, and clinical analysis are often very dilute. A traditional test cannot analyse them; an instrument can.

*Accuracy — getting the right answer*

Traditional chemical tests rely on a human observer to judge colour. Is that flame "lilac" (potassium) or "red" (lithium)? Is the precipitate "blue" (copper(II)) or "green" (iron(II))? Two students looking at the same flame can disagree. An instrument removes the human eye from the measurement. A flame photometer reads the wavelength and intensity of the light emitted on a calibrated scale, returning a numerical value. The number is reproducible — the same sample analysed again gives the same reading — and the calibration is traceable to known standards. This makes instrumental results much more accurate and far less dependent on the skill of the analyst.

*Speed — analysing many samples quickly*

A flame test takes a few seconds for a single ion, but recording the colour, comparing it to a reference, and writing up the result for many samples is slow. An instrument such as a flame photometer can analyse a sample in seconds and is often connected to a computer that records the result automatically. For laboratories that need to process hundreds of samples per day (water companies, hospital pathology labs, food-safety labs), the time saved by automation is decisive.

Comparison — Instrumental Methods vs Traditional Chemical Tests

FeatureTraditional chemical testInstrumental methodWhy this matters
SensitivityDetects only if there is enough substance to give a visible colour, precipitate, or flameDetects very low concentrations (ppm or lower) using sensitive electronic detectorsReal environmental and clinical samples are often too dilute for traditional tests
AccuracyDepends on the observer's judgement of colour or precipitateCalibrated against standards; gives a numerical readingRemoves human error; result is reproducible
SpeedMinutes per sample; slower for batchesSeconds per sample; can be automated for batch analysisAllows large numbers of samples to be processed quickly
Sample size requiredLarger volume needed so changes are visibleVery small sample volume is sufficientUseful when only a tiny sample is available (e.g. forensic, medical)
Identification of mixturesA single flame test gives one dominant colour and can hide other ionsCan resolve and quantify multiple ions in the same sampleA mixture of sodium and potassium ions can be separately quantified by flame photometry, but a single flame test sees only the bright yellow of sodium masking the lilac of potassium
OutputQualitative — "is it present, yes or no"Quantitative — gives a concentration valueAllows the analyst to report "how much", not just "is it there"
Skill requiredTrained chemist needed to interpret colours and precipitatesTechnician can run pre-programmed instrumentLowers operator skill requirement once the method is set up
CostCheap glassware and reagentsExpensive instrument with maintenance and calibration costsThe one disadvantage — instruments are costly to buy and maintain

Concrete Examples — How an Instrument Beats a Traditional Test

*Example 1 — Sensitivity: flame test vs flame photometry*

A flame test held in a Bunsen flame requires enough metal ion in the sample for the eye to see a colour. For sodium this is straightforward — the bright yellow is visible at fairly low levels — but for many metal ions a dilute solution will give no visible flame colour at all. A flame photometer, by contrast, measures the intensity of light emitted at a specific wavelength using a photoelectric detector. It can detect sodium, potassium, lithium and other metal ions at concentrations of parts per million — well below the eye's detection limit. Environmental testing of river water for sodium contamination, or clinical testing of blood for low potassium, would be impossible with a traditional flame test but is routine with flame photometry.

*Example 2 — Accuracy: distinguishing similar flame colours*

Lithium burns crimson red; strontium burns a similar (but deeper) red. A trained chemist can usually tell them apart, but the human eye is not a precise wavelength detector and the colours can easily be confused, especially if the sample is contaminated or impure. A flame photometer measures the exact wavelength of the emitted light against a calibration standard, so lithium (around 671 nm) and strontium (around 461 nm) are unambiguously distinguished. The instrument turns a subjective colour judgement into an objective wavelength measurement.

*Example 3 — Resolving mixtures*

If a solution contains both sodium and potassium ions, a single flame test is unhelpful. The intense yellow of sodium dominates the flame and masks the much paler lilac of potassium — even though the potassium is genuinely there. A flame photometer can measure the sodium signal at its wavelength and the potassium signal at its own (separate) wavelength independently, and report a concentration for each ion. Two different ions in one sample, two independent quantitative results — something a flame test cannot deliver.

*Example 4 — Speed: water-quality testing*

A water company testing river samples for sodium and potassium might receive hundreds of samples per day. Running individual flame tests would consume hours of a trained chemist's time per sample and produce only qualitative answers. A flame photometer, once calibrated each morning against standard solutions, can run a sample in seconds and feed the result directly to a computer. The same workload that would occupy a chemist for a full day can be cleared in under an hour by an automated instrument.

The "Disadvantage" — Cost

You may also be asked about a disadvantage of instrumental methods. The expected answer is cost: the instruments are expensive to buy, expensive to maintain, and require trained operators to set up and calibrate. Traditional chemical tests, by contrast, need only standard laboratory glassware, common reagents, and a Bunsen burner. For occasional one-off tests in a school or small lab, traditional tests remain practical and cheap; the advantages of instrumental methods only become decisive when the lab is processing many samples or needs quantitative data.

A second disadvantage sometimes credited is that an instrument may need to be calibrated before use — a calibration step adds time and requires standard reference solutions of accurately known concentration.

What the Spec Does and Does Not Require

The Edexcel specification on this spec point is deliberately narrow. You are expected to know:

  • That instrumental methods exist and are used in modern analytical chemistry
  • That they offer three named advantages — sensitivity, accuracy and speed
  • That you can give an example for each advantage (flame photometry vs flame test is the standard worked example, since flame photometry is the named instrument in spec point 9.1.6.2)

You are not expected to know:

  • The internal workings of any instrument
  • Diagrams of optical paths, detectors or sample introduction systems
  • Names of techniques outside flame photometry (mass spectrometry, NMR, UV-visible spectroscopy and so on are not on the Edexcel GCSE Chemistry specification)
  • The physics of how the light is detected, dispersed, or measured

This is a deliberate spec design: the syllabus wants students to understand the role and value of instruments in chemistry rather than to memorise instrument schematics. Keep your answers focused on the three named advantages, with a one-sentence justification for each.

Examiner Report Insights

No examiner-report data available for this spec point in the current notebook. The Topic 9.1 instrumental-methods examiner-report material in the notebook targets flame photometry specifically — see the sister spec point 9.1.6.2 Flame Photometry for examiner commentary on flame-photometer calibration and concentration-determination questions.

How Advantages of Instrumental Methods Appears in Exams

This spec point typically appears on Paper 2 of the Separate Chemistry award (1CH0/2F or 1CH0/2H) as part of the late "qualitative analysis" question. The two formats are:

*Format 1 — Standalone short-answer recall (1–2 marks)*

A short part-question asking the student to state advantages of instrumental methods over traditional chemical tests. Typical command words are "State", "Give" or "Describe". For a 2-mark question you would name two of sensitivity / accuracy / speed and give a one-sentence justification of each — or name all three for the recall mark and add a short comparison sentence for the second mark.

*Format 2 — Lead-in to a flame-photometry question*

This spec point often appears as part (a) of a multi-part question where part (b) goes on to test the named instrumental method, flame photometry (spec point 9.1.6.2). Part (a) establishes the comparative advantages over a flame test; part (b) then asks the student to use a calibration curve to read off a concentration. Example pairing: 1CH0/2F November 2021 Q4, where the opening parts establish the comparative advantages before later parts set a flame-photometer calibration task.

*Mark tariff guide:*

Question typeTypical marksWhat's needed for full marks
State one advantage1One of: sensitivity, accuracy, speed
State two/three advantages2–3Each advantage named and briefly explained or exemplified
Describe an advantage with an example2Name the advantage and give a worked example (e.g. "can detect ions at lower concentration than a flame test")
State an advantage and a disadvantage2One named advantage plus "expensive" / "requires calibration"

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateA short factual answer; just name the advantage1
GiveA short example or named answer1
DescribeThe advantage plus a short justification or example2
ExplainA reasoned link between the advantage and why it matters or how it works in practice2–3

*Exam-writing tips for full marks:*

  • Never write "instrumental methods are better" without specifying how they are better. The mark is for the named advantage (sensitivity / accuracy / speed), not a generic value judgement.
  • Pair each advantage with a comparator. "Instrumental methods are more sensitive" is weak; "instrumental methods are more sensitive than traditional chemical tests because they can detect ions at concentrations of parts per million, which are too dilute to give a visible flame colour" is strong.
  • If asked for a disadvantage, write "they are expensive" or "they require calibration" — not "they are difficult to use" (the spec deliberately says you don't need to know how they work).
  • Watch the command word. "State" wants just the named advantage; "Describe" wants the advantage and a one-line example; "Explain" wants the cause-and-effect chain.
Key Takeaways
  1. Instrumental methods of analysis use a machine to detect or measure a substance, replacing tests that rely on the human eye observing a colour, precipitate or flame.
  2. The three named advantages of instrumental methods on the Edexcel GCSE specification are sensitivity, accuracy and speed — learn all three by name.
