  3. Sensitivity: instruments can detect substances at much lower concentrations (e.g. parts per million) than traditional tests, which need enough substance to give a visible response.
  4. Accuracy: instruments give numerical, calibrated readings, so the result does not depend on the observer's judgement of colour and is reproducible.
  5. Speed: instruments analyse samples in seconds and can be automated for large batches, where traditional tests would take minutes each.
  6. Instrumental methods can resolve mixtures of ions (e.g. distinguish sodium and potassium in the same sample) where a single flame test would be masked by the brighter colour.
  7. The main disadvantage is cost — instruments are expensive to buy and maintain — and a secondary disadvantage is that they typically require calibration against standard solutions before use.
  8. The Edexcel specification explicitly states that no knowledge of the instrument or how it works is required — focus on the why-it's-better content, not on instrument internals or diagrams.
  9. The named instrumental method on the Edexcel GCSE Chemistry specification is flame photometry (spec point 9.1.6.2), and the standard comparator is the traditional flame test for metal ions.
  10. This spec point appears on Paper 2 of the Separate Chemistry award (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H), typically as a short recall part (1–2 marks) or as the lead-in to a multi-part flame-photometry question.
  11. For full marks, pair every named advantage with a one-line justification or example — "more sensitive" alone is weaker than "more sensitive, because the instrument can detect ions at parts-per-million levels that would be invisible in a flame test".
  12. Do not write "instrumental methods are better" as a generic statement — examiners reward the specific named advantage and a concrete reason, not a value judgement.
9.1.6.2 Flame Photometry F+H ▼

Flame Photometry

Spec 9.9C: Evaluate data from a flame photometer (a) to determine the concentration of ions in dilute solution using a calibration curve, and (b) to identify metal ions by comparing the data with reference data. No knowledge of the instrument or how it works is required.

The specification is unusually explicit here: you do not need to know how the instrument works mechanically — only how to read and use the data it produces. This is a data-handling spec point at heart, sitting in the instrumental-methods subtopic alongside chromatography. Foundation and Higher candidates are both examined on it, and it appears only on Paper 2 of the Separate Chemistry course (1CH0).

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
Flame photometerAn instrument that measures the intensity of light emitted at specific wavelengths when metal ions are excited in a flame
Emission spectrumThe pattern of wavelengths (and their intensities) of light given out by a sample when its ions are excited; each metal ion produces a characteristic pattern
Calibration curveA graph of measured light intensity (y-axis) against known concentration of an ion (x-axis), built from standard solutions and used to read off the concentration of an unknown
Reference dataA pre-recorded table or set of spectra showing the wavelengths and intensities produced by known metal ions, used to identify the ions in an unknown sample
Standard solutionA solution of known concentration of a particular ion, used to plot points on the calibration curve

What a Flame Photometer Measures

When a solution containing metal ions is sprayed into a flame, the ions absorb thermal energy and their electrons are promoted to higher energy levels. As the electrons fall back, they release that energy as light. Each metal ion releases light at its own characteristic wavelengths — this is what produces the familiar flame-test colours (lilac for potassium, yellow for sodium, red for lithium, and so on).

A flame photometer does two things a simple flame test cannot:

  1. It separates the emitted light by wavelength, so it can tell which ions are present even in a mixture where one colour would normally mask another.
  2. It measures the intensity of the light at each wavelength — and intensity is proportional to the amount of that ion in solution, which lets you calculate concentration.

The output is therefore not a colour-by-eye observation but a numerical light-intensity reading at one or more specific wavelengths.

Why Flame Photometry Beats a Simple Flame Test

FeatureFlame test (qualitative)Flame photometer (instrumental)
OutputA flame colour observed by eyeA numerical light-intensity value at a specific wavelength
Mixtures of ionsOne strong colour (e.g. sodium yellow) masks weaker onesEach ion's wavelength is detected separately, so mixtures can be resolved
ConcentrationNot measured — only "ion present" or "not present"Concentration is read off a calibration curve
AccuracySubjective; depends on the observerObjective, repeatable, far more sensitive
Very dilute samplesColour may be too faint to seeSensitive enough to detect ions at low concentrations

This is why flame photometry is described as a quantitative instrumental method, while the flame test is only qualitative.

Using a Calibration Curve to Find Concentration

The standard procedure (which you may be asked to evaluate from given data) is:

  1. Prepare a series of standard solutions — for example, solutions of potassium chloride at known concentrations of 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5 g/dm³.
  2. Place each standard solution into the flame photometer in turn and record the light-intensity reading for each.
  3. Plot a graph of light intensity (y-axis) against concentration of the ion (x-axis) and draw a best-fit line through the points — this is the calibration curve.
  4. Place the unknown sample into the photometer and record its light intensity.
  5. Find that intensity on the y-axis, draw a horizontal line across to the calibration curve, then drop a vertical line down to the x-axis. The value on the x-axis is the concentration of the ion in the unknown solution.

The calibration curve is normally drawn as a straight line through the origin — zero ion = zero intensity, and intensity rises in direct proportion to concentration in the dilute range the technique is used for.

Worked Example — Reading a Calibration Curve

A student is given the following calibration data for potassium ions (K⁺):

Concentration of K⁺ / g dm⁻³Light intensity (arbitrary units)
00
1.012
2.024
3.036
4.048
5.060

When plotted, these points produce a straight line through the origin with a gradient of 12 units per g dm⁻³.

An unknown sample is then placed into the photometer and gives a light-intensity reading of 42 units at the wavelength characteristic of potassium.

To find the concentration:

  • Locate 42 on the y-axis (light intensity).
  • Move across to the calibration line.
  • Drop down to the x-axis.
  • Read off the concentration.

42 ÷ 12 = 3.5 g dm⁻³ of K⁺ in the unknown sample.

You should always:

  • Quote the concentration with the correct unit (g/dm³, mol/dm³ or mg/dm³ depending on the y-axis label of the question's graph).
  • Read graph values to the nearest small division — don't round to the nearest whole number unless the question tells you to.
  • If the unknown's intensity falls outside the range of standards, state that the sample must be diluted (or fresh standards prepared at higher concentration) — extrapolating past the plotted standards is unsafe.

Using Reference Data to Identify Metal Ions in a Mixture

Different metal ions emit light at different characteristic wavelengths. For example (illustrative values you would be told in a question — not values to memorise):

Metal ionApproximate emission wavelength / nmVisible colour produced
Lithium, Li⁺~670Crimson red
Sodium, Na⁺~589Yellow / orange
Potassium, K⁺~766Lilac
Calcium, Ca²⁺~620Orange-red
Copper, Cu²⁺~515Blue-green

A flame photometer scanning a sample produces a trace with peaks at each wavelength where a metal ion is emitting. To identify what's in an unknown:

  1. Note the wavelengths at which peaks appear in the unknown's spectrum.
  2. Compare these wavelengths against a table or chart of reference data for known metal ions.
  3. A peak at a reference-data wavelength means that ion is present in the sample.
  4. The height (intensity) of each peak can be compared to a calibration curve for that ion to give its concentration.

This is the key reason flame photometry can do something a simple flame test cannot: a sample containing both sodium and potassium would show its sodium yellow only when looked at by eye (the brighter Na⁺ swamps the lilac K⁺), but a photometer reads each wavelength independently — so it produces a peak at ~589 nm and a separate peak at ~766 nm, confirming both ions are present and giving the concentration of each.

Worked Example — Identifying Ions from a Spectrum

A photometer trace of an unknown solution shows three peaks: one at ~589 nm, one at ~620 nm and one at ~766 nm. Using the reference data above:

  • 589 nm matches sodium (Na⁺).
  • 620 nm matches calcium (Ca²⁺).
  • 766 nm matches potassium (K⁺).

The sample contains a mixture of sodium, calcium and potassium ions. If a calibration curve is also provided for each ion, the height of each peak can then be read off to give each ion's concentration.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2H — June 2019 Examiner Report: "The most common score on this question was 0. Candidates found it hard to describe how it could be shown the potassium and sodium ions were present in the sample... Although it had been stated that a flame test had already been carried out on the sample, many candidates then described how to carry out the flame test. Of those that knew that a flame photometer should be used, only around half of those then knew how this would show that the two ions were present."

The pattern is striking and it should shape how you revise this spec point. When a question gives a scenario in which a flame test has already been done and asks how the ions could now be confirmed (especially in a mixture), the examiners are testing whether you know to switch techniques to a flame photometer and that you can explain what it does next. The two things you must therefore be able to do are:

  1. Name the technique (flame photometer / flame photometry) — not "do a flame test".
  2. Explain how it shows both ions are present — by giving peaks at the characteristic wavelengths of each ion, which can be matched against reference data.

If you only do step 1, you get half the marks; if you describe a flame test instead of the photometer, you get zero.

Mathematical Skills

*4a — Translate information between graphical and numeric form.*

You must be fluent in reading values off a calibration curve and, where the question provides a gradient or a linear relationship, calculating concentration from intensity directly.

The general rule the calibration curve embodies is:

`` intensity = gradient × concentration ``

Rearranged:

`` concentration = intensity ÷ gradient ``

Step-by-step procedure for any calibration-curve question:

  1. Identify what is on each axis (intensity vs concentration) and note the units on the concentration axis.
  2. If standards data is given as a table, plot the points or check that a drawn line is a good best fit.
  3. Read the unknown's intensity on the y-axis.
  4. Draw across horizontally to the line, then down to the x-axis.
  5. State the concentration with correct units.

You may also be asked to compare an unknown's peak pattern with reference data — this is a translation between a graphical spectrum and a numerical table of wavelengths. Both directions can be tested.

How Flame Photometry Appears in Exams

Flame photometry sits on Paper 2 of the Separate Chemistry course (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H) and tends to appear late in the paper as part of an instrumental-methods or qualitative-analysis question.

*Foundation tier (1CH0/2F):*

  • Calibration-graph reading: given a curve and an intensity, read off the concentration of the unknown.
  • 1CH0/2F November 2021 Q4(b)(ii): Flame-photometer calibration graph — find the concentration of copper ions (2 marks).
  • Identification questions: given a peak's wavelength and a reference table, state which ion is present.

*Higher tier (1CH0/2H):*

  • The same calibration-graph and reference-data skills are tested but often with an added comparison element (e.g. why the photometer can distinguish two ions where a flame test cannot, or evaluating which technique to recommend).
  • 1CH0/2H June 2019: A question requiring candidates to describe how to show both potassium and sodium ions were present in a mixture (after a flame test had already been carried out) — this is the question on which the most common score was 0 (see Examiner Report Insights above).

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
DetermineRead a value off a calibration curve — show the lines or working on the graph2
CalculateUse a gradient or linear relationship to find concentration numerically2–3
IdentifyMatch a peak's wavelength to a reference-data table to name the ion1
CompareContrast flame photometry with another technique (e.g. flame test) — both advantages and limitations2–3
DescribeOutline how the photometer's output shows particular ions are present in a sample2–4

*Read the question carefully — flame test versus flame photometer*

If a question stem already says a flame test has been carried out, the examiners are very likely setting up a follow-on instrumental method. Answers that describe the flame test again will score zero on that follow-on. Switch to the photometer: peaks at characteristic wavelengths matched against reference data.

*Show your graph-reading lines*

When asked to determine a concentration from a calibration curve, draw the horizontal line from your intensity reading across to the curve and the vertical line down to the concentration axis on the graph itself. This makes your method visible to the examiner and protects the method mark if your final number is slightly off.

---

Key Takeaways
  1. A flame photometer measures the intensity of light emitted at characteristic wavelengths when metal ions are excited in a flame.
  2. The Edexcel specification states no knowledge of how the instrument works is required — you are only assessed on reading and evaluating its data.
  3. Each metal ion emits light at its own characteristic wavelength, so peaks in a spectrum can be matched against reference data to identify which ions are present.
  4. A calibration curve is a graph of light intensity (y-axis) against known ion concentration (x-axis), built from standard solutions of known concentration.
  5. To find the concentration of an unknown: read its intensity on the y-axis, go across to the calibration line, then down to the x-axis — that x-value is the concentration.
  6. The calibration line is typically straight and passes through the origin (zero ion → zero intensity), with intensity directly proportional to concentration.
  7. Flame photometry resolves mixtures of ions that a simple flame test cannot — e.g. sodium and potassium together — because each ion's wavelength is measured separately.
  8. Flame photometry is more accurate, more sensitive, faster, and gives a numerical concentration — whereas a flame test gives only a colour observed by eye.
  9. If a question says a flame test has already been done and asks how the ions are confirmed, the answer is use a flame photometer — not "do the flame test again". This was the most common mistake on 1CH0/2H June 2019, where the most common score was 0.
  10. To explain how a flame photometer confirms both ions in a mixture: it produces peaks at the characteristic wavelengths of each ion, which can be compared with reference data to confirm each ion's identity (and to a calibration curve to give concentration).
  11. Always quote concentrations with the correct units (g/dm³, mol/dm³ or mg/dm³ — check the y-axis label) and read graph values to the nearest small division rather than rounding aggressively.
  12. On graph-reading questions, draw the horizontal and vertical lines on the calibration graph itself so the examiner can see your method even if the final number is slightly out.
9.2.1.1 Alkanes F+H ▼

Alkanes

Spec 9.10C: Recall the formulae of molecules of the alkanes, methane, ethane, propane and butane, and draw the structures of these molecules, showing all covalent bonds.

Spec 9.11C: Explain why the alkanes are saturated hydrocarbons.

*Foundation + Higher tier · Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only · Paper 2*

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
HydrocarbonA compound made of carbon and hydrogen atoms only — no other elements
AlkaneA saturated hydrocarbon containing only single covalent bonds between carbon atoms
SaturatedContains only single covalent bonds between carbon atoms; cannot add more hydrogen atoms
General formulaA formula that describes every member of a homologous series; for alkanes this is CₙH₂ₙ₊₂
Displayed formulaA 2D drawing showing every atom and every covalent bond explicitly
Homologous seriesA family of organic compounds with the same general formula, similar chemical properties, and a difference of CH₂ between consecutive members

*Why the term "hydrocarbon" matters so much*

A hydrocarbon contains only two elements: carbon and hydrogen. If a molecule contains any other element — oxygen, nitrogen, chlorine — it is not a hydrocarbon, even if it has carbon and hydrogen in it. Ethanol (C₂H₅OH) contains carbon and hydrogen, but it also contains oxygen, so it is not a hydrocarbon. This is the most-confused term in Section 9, and getting it precise is worth easy marks.

The word "hydrocarbon" also describes a compound, not a mixture. Each molecule of methane, ethane, propane and butane is a single compound. Crude oil is a mixture of hydrocarbons — that distinction also catches students out.

The General Formula of Alkanes

The general formula for an alkane is:

CₙH₂ₙ₊₂

where n is the number of carbon atoms in the molecule. Plug n into the formula and the molecular formula falls out:

nMoleculeFormula calculationMolecular formula
1MethaneC₁H₂(1)+2 = C₁H₄CH₄
2EthaneC₂H₂(2)+2 = C₂H₆C₂H₆
3PropaneC₃H₂(3)+2 = C₃H₈C₃H₈
4ButaneC₄H₂(4)+2 = C₄H₁₀C₄H₁₀

The pattern CH₂ between each member (methane → ethane gains CH₂; ethane → propane gains CH₂; etc.) is what makes the alkanes a homologous series. You can use the general formula to write the molecular formula of any alkane — pentane (n = 5) is C₅H₁₂, hexane (n = 6) is C₆H₁₄, and so on.

The Four Named Alkanes — Displayed Structures

You must be able to draw a displayed formula for each of the four named alkanes — methane, ethane, propane, butane — showing every C–H bond and every C–C bond as a single line.

*Methane — CH₄*

One carbon atom bonded to four hydrogen atoms:

`` H | H — C — H | H ``

Four C–H bonds; no C–C bonds. The simplest possible alkane.

*Ethane — C₂H₆*

Two carbon atoms joined by a single C–C bond, each carbon also bonded to three hydrogen atoms:

`` H H | | H — C — C — H | | H H ``

One C–C bond and six C–H bonds — seven bonds in total.

*Propane — C₃H₈*

Three carbon atoms in a chain, each carbon completing its four bonds with hydrogen atoms. The two end carbons each have three H atoms; the middle carbon has two H atoms:

`` H H H | | | H — C — C — C — H | | | H H H ``

Two C–C bonds and eight C–H bonds.

*Butane — C₄H₁₀*

Four carbon atoms in a chain. The two end carbons each carry three H atoms; the two middle carbons each carry two H atoms:

`` H H H H | | | | H — C — C — C — C — H | | | | H H H H ``

Three C–C bonds and ten C–H bonds.

*The rule that ties every drawing together*

Every carbon atom in every alkane forms exactly four bonds. If you have drawn a carbon with only three lines coming off it, you have forgotten a hydrogen. If you have drawn a carbon with five lines, you have made a mistake — carbon cannot have five bonds. Count the lines round each carbon as a final check before moving on.

Why Alkanes Are Saturated Hydrocarbons

Spec point 9.11C requires you to explain why alkanes are saturated. A full mark-scoring explanation has two parts:

  1. Alkanes contain only single covalent bonds between carbon atoms (no C=C double bonds, no C≡C triple bonds).
  2. Therefore, each carbon atom already has the maximum number of hydrogen atoms it can hold — the molecule cannot add any more hydrogen atoms to it.

The word "saturated" is borrowed from everyday English — a saturated sponge is full of water and cannot soak up any more. A saturated hydrocarbon is full of hydrogen and cannot bond to any more.

*Why this matters chemically*

Because every carbon already has four single bonds, alkanes are relatively unreactive compared to alkenes. There is no double bond for hydrogen, halogens or bromine water to add across. This is exactly why alkanes do not decolourise bromine water but alkenes do — a contrast you will be tested on repeatedly in Section 9.

Comparison: Saturated vs Unsaturated Hydrocarbons

FeatureSaturated (alkane)Unsaturated (alkene)
Bonds between carbonsSingle bonds only (C–C)At least one C=C double bond
General formulaCₙH₂ₙ₊₂CₙH₂ₙ
Hydrogen atoms per carbonMaximum possibleLess than maximum
Bromine water testNo reaction — orange colour staysDecolourised — turns colourless
ReactivityRelatively unreactiveMore reactive (the C=C is a reactive site)
First memberMethane (CH₄)Ethene (C₂H₄)

Worked Example — Working Out an Alkane Formula

Question: Pentane is the alkane with five carbon atoms. Use the general formula CₙH₂ₙ₊₂ to work out its molecular formula and the number of bonds in one molecule.

*Step 1 — Substitute n = 5 into the general formula:*

CₙH₂ₙ₊₂ = C₅H(2×5)+2 = C₅H₁₂

*Step 2 — Count the C–C bonds:*

A chain of 5 carbons has (5 − 1) = 4 C–C bonds.

*Step 3 — Count the C–H bonds:*

Each carbon has 4 bonds in total. The two end carbons use 1 bond for the C–C and 3 for C–H (3 × 2 = 6 C–H bonds). The three middle carbons each use 2 bonds for C–C and 2 for C–H (2 × 3 = 6 C–H bonds). Total = 12 C–H bonds, which matches the 12 hydrogens in C₅H₁₂.

Step 4 — Total bonds: 4 + 12 = 16 bonds in one molecule of pentane.

This same method works for any alkane in a straight chain.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — June 2019 Examiner Report: "Few candidates were able to recall the term hydrocarbon correctly. The vast majority of responses focused on why the molecule was unsaturated by correctly stating the presence of the double bond... A common error was to confuse atoms and molecules, ie hydrocarbon containing hydrogen molecules and carbon molecules."

The takeaway is sharp: when a question asks for the meaning of hydrocarbon, you must write that it is a compound containing only carbon and hydrogen atoms — not "molecules" of hydrogen and carbon, and not anything about double bonds (that is a separate idea about saturation). The 2019 cohort lost marks because they conflated "hydrocarbon" with "unsaturated", and because they wrote "hydrogen molecules and carbon molecules" instead of "hydrogen atoms and carbon atoms".

Examiner Tips & Tricks

  • Always write "atoms", not "molecules", when describing what a hydrocarbon contains. A hydrocarbon contains carbon atoms and hydrogen atoms bonded together. Writing "hydrogen molecules and carbon molecules" — the exact error flagged in the 1CH0/2F June 2019 Examiner Report — will not score.
  • Don't confuse "hydrocarbon" with "unsaturated". "Hydrocarbon" means "made of carbon and hydrogen only". "Unsaturated" means "contains a C=C double bond". These are two separate ideas and you must answer the question that is actually being asked. The 2019 cohort lost marks specifically by jumping to "double bond" when the question asked what hydrocarbon meant.
  • Count the bonds on every carbon. Every carbon atom must have exactly four bonds in your displayed structure. The single most common drawing error is a carbon with only three lines coming off it (a missing H).
  • Draw every C–H bond explicitly. In a displayed formula, condensed shortcuts like "CH₃" are not acceptable. Each hydrogen needs its own line to the carbon it is bonded to.
  • Use the general formula as a check. After drawing your structure, count the atoms and confirm they match CₙH₂ₙ₊₂. If your "propane" has 7 hydrogens, you have made a mistake — propane is C₃H₈.
  • For the 2-mark "explain why alkanes are saturated" question, score both marks by saying (1) only single bonds between carbons, and (2) so no more hydrogen atoms can be added / each carbon already has the maximum number of bonds. One half of that without the other is usually only worth one mark.

How Alkanes Appears in Exams

This spec point appears on 1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H only (Separate Chemistry Paper 2). It does not appear on Combined Science (1SC0).

*Typical positions and types*

  • Mid-paper, often appearing as a draw-the-structure or write-the-formula task worth 1–2 marks.
  • Or as the "name and explain saturated" lead-in to a larger comparison question about alkanes and alkenes, where the alkane content opens the question and the alkene reactions follow.
  • 1CH0/2F June 2023 Q7(b)(i): "Complete Figure 13 to show the structure of one molecule of propane and the formula of butane" — worth 2 marks.

*Common command words for this SP*

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
Recall / StateGive the molecular formula of a named alkane1
DrawThe displayed structure showing every atom and every bond1–2
CompleteFill in missing atoms or bonds on a partial structure1–2
ExplainReasoned answer — typically "explain why alkanes are saturated"2
Key Takeaways
  1. A hydrocarbon is a compound containing only carbon and hydrogen atoms — no other elements. Get this definition word-perfect: the 1CH0/2F June 2019 Examiner Report flagged "hydrocarbon" as the most-confused term in Section 9.
  2. An alkane is a saturated hydrocarbon — only single covalent bonds between carbons, with each carbon at its maximum hydrogen count.
  3. The general formula for alkanes is CₙH₂ₙ₊₂. Substitute the number of carbons in for n to get the molecular formula.
  4. The four named alkanes are: methane CH₄, ethane C₂H₆, propane C₃H₈, butane C₄H₁₀. Learn these molecular formulae by heart.
  5. In a displayed formula you must draw every C–C bond and every C–H bond as a separate line. Every carbon must have exactly four bonds.
  6. Why alkanes are saturated (2-mark answer): (1) only single bonds between carbon atoms, so (2) each carbon already has the maximum number of hydrogen atoms and the molecule cannot add any more.
  7. Each consecutive alkane differs from the last by CH₂ — this is the homologous series pattern.
  8. Because alkanes have no C=C double bonds, they are relatively unreactive and do not decolourise bromine water (a key contrast with alkenes).
  9. Watch out for the atoms vs molecules trap: hydrocarbons contain hydrogen atoms and carbon atoms, not "hydrogen molecules and carbon molecules" — this exact error was flagged in 1CH0/2F June 2019.
  10. Hydrocarbon ≠ unsaturated. These two terms are tested separately. Answer the question that is actually asked.
  11. Use the general formula to check your drawing: if your displayed propane has 7 hydrogens instead of 8, you have forgotten one.
9.2.2.1 Alkenes F+H ▼

Alkenes

Spec 9.12C: Recall the formulae of molecules of the alkenes, ethene, propene, butene, and draw the structures of these molecules, showing all covalent bonds (but-1-ene and but-2-ene only).

Spec 9.13C: Explain why the alkenes are unsaturated hydrocarbons, describing that their molecules contain the functional group C=C.

Foundation + Higher tier · Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only · Paper 2.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
HydrocarbonA compound made of only the elements hydrogen and carbon — no other atoms
AlkeneA hydrocarbon containing at least one carbon–carbon double bond (C=C); the homologous series with general formula CₙH₂ₙ
UnsaturatedA molecule that contains one or more carbon–carbon double (or triple) bonds, so it can react with more atoms by addition across the multiple bond
SaturatedA molecule that contains only single bonds between carbon atoms, so it cannot add any more atoms without breaking a bond
Functional groupThe atom or group of atoms that gives a molecule its characteristic chemical reactions — for alkenes, this is the C=C double bond
General formulaAn algebraic expression that gives the molecular formula of every member of a homologous series — for alkenes, CₙH₂ₙ
Displayed formulaA 2D structural drawing showing every atom and every covalent bond explicitly

*Why the term "hydrocarbon" is precise — and often misremembered*

A hydrocarbon contains only carbon and hydrogen atoms; nothing else. A molecule that also contains oxygen (like ethanol, C₂H₅OH) or nitrogen is not a hydrocarbon, even if the rest of the structure looks alkene-like. This sounds obvious, but the term is regularly confused in exams. The 1CH0/2F June 2019 Examiner Report flagged that "Few candidates were able to recall the term hydrocarbon correctly" and noted that "A common error was to confuse atoms and molecules, ie hydrocarbon containing hydrogen molecules and carbon molecules". The fix is to learn the term as a one-line definition: a compound made of hydrogen and carbon atoms only — and use the word atoms, not molecules.

The General Formula CₙH₂ₙ

The general formula for the alkene homologous series is CₙH₂ₙ, where n is the number of carbon atoms.

n (carbons)Molecular formulaName
2C₂H₄Ethene
3C₃H₆Propene
4C₄H₈Butene (but-1-ene or but-2-ene)

Notice there is no alkene with n = 1. Methene (CH₂) does not exist as a stable molecule, because a single carbon cannot form a C=C double bond with itself. The series therefore starts at ethene (C₂H₄).

*Why alkenes have the formula CₙH₂ₙ and alkanes have CₙH₂ₙ₊₂*

In a fully saturated alkane, each carbon forms four single bonds — using two of those bonds to link to neighbouring carbons (or one if it's a chain end) and the rest to bond with hydrogen atoms. When two carbon atoms are joined by a double bond instead of two separate single bonds, two of the "slots" that would otherwise have held hydrogens are now used to make the second bond between the two carbons. The molecule therefore has two fewer hydrogen atoms than the corresponding alkane — which is exactly what CₙH₂ₙ describes compared to CₙH₂ₙ₊₂.

The Three Named Alkenes — Displayed Structures

You must be able to draw all three molecules showing every covalent bond explicitly.

*Ethene — C₂H₄*

`` H H \ / C = C / \ H H ``

Two carbon atoms joined by a C=C double bond; each carbon has two single bonds to hydrogen. Total: 1 C=C double bond, 4 C–H single bonds.

*Propene — C₃H₆*

`` H H H \ \ / C — C = C / \ H H | H ``

(Cleaner layout:)

`` H H H \ \ / H — C — C = C | \ H H ``

Three carbon atoms in a chain. The C=C double bond is between carbons 1 and 2 (counting from the end nearest the double bond). The third carbon (the –CH₃ group) carries three hydrogens. Total: 1 C=C, 1 C–C single bond, 6 C–H single bonds.

*But-1-ene — C₄H₈*

The C=C double bond is between carbons 1 and 2 — at the end of the chain.

`` H H H H \ \ / / H — C — C — C = C | | H H ``

(Re-laid for clarity, reading left-to-right C4 → C3 → C2 = C1:)

`` CH₃ — CH₂ — CH = CH₂ ``

Total: 1 C=C, 2 C–C single bonds, 8 C–H single bonds.

*But-2-ene — C₄H₈*

The C=C double bond is between carbons 2 and 3 — in the middle of the chain.

`` CH₃ — CH = CH — CH₃ ``

Displayed:

`` H H H H \ \ \ / H — C — C = C — C — H | | H H ``

Total: 1 C=C, 2 C–C single bonds, 8 C–H single bonds.

But-1-ene and but-2-ene are structural isomers: same molecular formula (C₄H₈), different position of the C=C double bond. The Edexcel GCSE specification names both explicitly — you can be asked to draw either.

A note on scope: The spec wording (but-1-ene and but-2-ene only) means you are not required to know any other butene isomers (such as methylpropene or cyclobutane derivatives) at GCSE. Stay inside the two isomers named.

Why Alkenes Are Unsaturated

An alkene contains a C=C double bond. This double bond is the functional group that defines the entire homologous series and gives alkenes their characteristic reactivity.

The molecule is described as unsaturated because the carbon atoms in the C=C bond are using one of their four available bonds for the second bond between them — instead of bonding to a hydrogen (or another atom). This means each carbon in the C=C bond is bonded to only three other atoms, not four.

Because the C=C bond can break open to form two new single bonds, an alkene can react with another small molecule (such as Br₂, H₂, or H₂O) by addition across the double bond. The two carbons each pick up one new atom or group, and the C=C becomes a C–C single bond. After this addition, every carbon is bonded to four other atoms — the molecule is now fully saturated.

*The chain of logic students must produce in an explain question:*

  1. Alkenes contain a C=C double bond (the functional group).
  2. Each carbon in the C=C is currently bonded to only 3 other atoms, not 4.
  3. The C=C can break open to form two single bonds.
  4. This means the molecule can add extra atoms across the double bond.
  5. Therefore the alkene is unsaturated — it is not "fully bonded" with hydrogens like an alkane is.

Alkanes vs Alkenes — Comparison Table

FeatureAlkanesAlkenes
General formulaCₙH₂ₙ₊₂CₙH₂ₙ
First memberMethane CH₄ (n = 1)Ethene C₂H₄ (n = 2)
C–C bond typeSingle bonds only (C–C)At least one double bond (C=C)
Functional groupNone — saturated chainC=C
Saturated or unsaturatedSaturatedUnsaturated
Hydrogens vs corresponding hydrocarbonMaximum number for that many carbons2 fewer hydrogens than the alkane with the same number of carbons
Reaction with bromine waterNo reaction (stays orange)Decolourises bromine water (turns colourless) — test for unsaturation
Ease of further reactionUnreactive; only undergoes substitution under UV lightReactive; readily undergoes addition reactions
CombustionBurns in O₂ → CO₂ + H₂OBurns in O₂ → CO₂ + H₂O (often with a smokier flame due to higher C:H ratio)

The reactivity difference is the most important consequence: the C=C double bond is the reason alkenes can be turned into useful products (polymers, alcohols, halogenoalkanes) while alkanes are largely just used as fuels.

Forward Link — Reaction with Bromine (SP 9.2.2.2)

The C=C functional group lets alkenes react with bromine in an addition reaction. When bromine water (orange) is shaken with an alkene, the Br₂ adds across the double bond — one Br to each carbon — and the orange colour disappears, leaving a colourless solution. Alkanes do not do this, so the bromine water test is used to distinguish alkenes from alkanes.

The reaction equation and full mechanism are covered in 9.2.2.2 Reaction of Alkenes with Bromine. For this spec point you only need to recognise that:

  • The C=C is what makes the reaction possible.
  • The product is a dibromoalkane (saturated).
  • Decolourisation of bromine water confirms the presence of a C=C.

A Note on Combustion Products (linked to 9.2.3.1)

Although combustion of alkenes is properly examined under 9.2.3.1 Combustion of Hydrocarbons, the 1CH0/2H June 2019 Examiner Report flagged a misconception worth flagging here too, because it stems from confusion about what an alkene is. On the complete-combustion-of-ethene equation, the report stated: "Where candidates lost marks, it was often as they gave the formula of oxygen as O rather than O₂. Others lost marks as they were not aware that the products of the complete combustion were carbon dioxide and water with a common misconception being that ethanoic acid would be produced."

The ethanoic acid answer comes from candidates treating ethene (C₂H₄) as if it were ethanol (C₂H₅OH) — i.e., confusing alkenes with alcohols. If you know that an alkene is a hydrocarbon (carbon and hydrogen only — no oxygen in the molecule), you know it can only burn to CO₂ + H₂O, just like every other hydrocarbon. The oxygen in the products comes from O₂ in the air, not from the alkene itself.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — June 2019 Examiner Report: On explaining the terms 'hydrocarbon' and 'unsaturated': "Few candidates were able to recall the term hydrocarbon correctly. The vast majority of responses focused on why the molecule was unsaturated by correctly stating the presence of the double bond... A common error was to confuse atoms and molecules, ie hydrocarbon containing hydrogen molecules and carbon molecules." Lesson: the unsaturated mark is usually scored (mention the C=C), but the hydrocarbon mark requires the precise statement that the compound contains hydrogen and carbon atoms only.
  • 1CH0/2H — June 2019 Examiner Report: On the complete combustion of ethene: "Where candidates lost marks, it was often as they gave the formula of oxygen as O rather than O₂. Others lost marks as they were not aware that the products of the complete combustion were carbon dioxide and water with a common misconception being that ethanoic acid would be produced." (Strictly a 9.2.3.1 issue but reveals a wider misunderstanding of what an alkene is — candidates were treating ethene like an alcohol.)

Mathematical Skills

5b — Translate information between diagrammatic and numerical forms. You must be able to:

  • Convert a molecular formula (e.g., C₃H₆) into a displayed formula with every covalent bond drawn.
  • Convert a displayed structure back into the molecular formula.
  • Apply the general formula CₙH₂ₙ to predict the molecular formula of any alkene given the number of carbons.

*Procedure to draw a displayed alkene:*

  1. Count the number of carbon atoms (n) from the molecular formula (CₙH₂ₙ).
  2. Draw n carbon atoms in a row.
  3. Place the C=C double bond between the correct pair of carbons (position 1 for terminal alkenes like but-1-ene; position 2 for but-2-ene).
  4. Each carbon in the C=C double bond will have two remaining bonds — fill those with H atoms or chain continuations.
  5. Each carbon not in the C=C double bond will have four single bonds in total — fill the remaining slots with H atoms or chain continuations.
  6. Count your hydrogens: they should total 2n. If they don't, you've made an error.

How Alkenes Appears in Exams

Paper 2 (1CH0/2F + 1CH0/2H) — Separate Chemistry only. Typically tested in the middle-to-late part of the paper alongside other organic chemistry content (hydrocarbons, polymers, alcohols).

*Typical question types:*

  • Draw the displayed formula of ethene, propene, but-1-ene or but-2-ene — must show every covalent bond, including the C=C explicitly with two parallel lines.
  • Recall the molecular formula of a named alkene (or vice versa).
  • State what is meant by hydrocarbon and unsaturated — both terms appear together as a 2-mark explain pair.
  • Explain why alkenes are unsaturated, referencing the C=C double bond and the ability to react further.
  • Compare alkanes and alkenes (formula, bonding, reaction with bromine water).
  • Identify a hydrocarbon as an alkene from its molecular formula (does it fit CₙH₂ₙ?).

*Specific cited appearance:*

  • 1CH0/2H June 2024 Q10(a)(ii) (2 marks): "Complete the balanced equation for the reaction of hydrocarbon Z, C₄H₈, with bromine" — relies on identifying C₄H₈ as an alkene first (it fits CₙH₂ₙ with n = 4) before writing the addition product. The link between this spec point and 9.2.2.2 is constantly tested.

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
RecallReproduce the molecular formula of a named alkene from memory1
DrawDisplayed structure with every covalent bond shown — including the C=C as two parallel lines1–2
StateShort factual answer — e.g., the general formula, the functional group, or a definition1
ExplainReason linking cause (C=C double bond) to effect (unsaturated, capable of addition)2–3
DescribeA short prose answer — e.g., describe the structure of an alkene molecule1–2
Key Takeaways
  1. Alkenes are a homologous series of unsaturated hydrocarbons with general formula CₙH₂ₙ.
  2. A hydrocarbon is a compound containing only hydrogen and carbon atoms — the word atoms (not molecules) matters in the definition.
  3. The three named alkenes for Edexcel GCSE are ethene C₂H₄, propene C₃H₆, and butene C₄H₈ — for butene, only but-1-ene and but-2-ene are required.
  4. The functional group of every alkene is the C=C double bond — this is what makes the molecule reactive.
  5. Alkenes are unsaturated because the C=C double bond can break open and add extra atoms (such as Br₂, H₂, or H₂O) across it.
  6. Alkenes have two fewer hydrogens than the corresponding alkane — compare CₙH₂ₙ (alkene) with CₙH₂ₙ₊₂ (alkane).
  7. But-1-ene (CH₃–CH₂–CH=CH₂) and but-2-ene (CH₃–CH=CH–CH₃) are structural isomers of C₄H₈ — same molecular formula, different position of the C=C.
  8. When drawing a displayed formula, every covalent bond must be shown — including both lines of the C=C, all C–C single bonds, and every C–H bond. The total number of hydrogens must equal 2n.
  9. Alkenes decolourise bromine water (orange → colourless) because the C=C adds Br₂ across the double bond — this is the chemical test that distinguishes alkenes from alkanes (covered in 9.2.2.2 Reaction of Alkenes with Bromine).
  10. Alkanes have only single C–C bonds and are saturated and unreactive by comparison — they do not react with bromine water.
  11. The June 2019 1CH0/2F report shows the unsaturated mark is usually scored but the hydrocarbon mark is often lost — learn the precise wording.
  12. Alkenes are hydrocarbons — they contain no oxygen. On complete combustion they form CO₂ and H₂O only (never ethanoic acid or any other oxygenated product).
9.2.2.2 Bromine & Alkenes F+H ▼

Bromine & Alkenes

Spec 9.14C: Recall the addition reaction of ethene with bromine, showing the structures of reactants and products, and extend this to other alkenes

Spec 9.15C: Explain how bromine water is used to distinguish between alkanes and alkenes

This spec point links two related ideas: (1) the addition reaction between an alkene and bromine, and (2) the use of bromine water as a chemical test to tell alkanes and alkenes apart. Both depend on the same underlying chemistry — the C=C double bond opens up so that two new bonds can form across it.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
Addition reactionA reaction in which two reactants join to form a single product, with no atoms left over
SaturatedA hydrocarbon that contains only single C–C and C–H bonds; cannot undergo addition reactions
UnsaturatedA hydrocarbon that contains at least one C=C double bond; can undergo addition reactions
Bromine waterA dilute solution of bromine (Br₂) in water; an orange/brown solution used to test for alkenes
DecolouriseTo remove the colour from a coloured solution — here, orange/brown bromine water becomes colourless when shaken with an alkene

The Addition Reaction of Ethene with Bromine

When ethene is shaken with bromine, the two molecules combine to form a single product: 1,2-dibromoethane. The C=C double bond opens up, and one bromine atom attaches to each of the two carbon atoms that were previously double-bonded.

*Balanced equation:*

CH₂=CH₂ + Br₂ → CH₂BrCH₂Br

*Displayed structures of reactants and products:*

`` H H H H \ / | | C = C + Br—Br → H—C — C—H / \ | | H H Br Br ``

Reactant (ethene) — note the C=C double bond and the four hydrogen atoms. Bromine (Br–Br) is shown as a single covalent bond between two bromine atoms.

Product (1,2-dibromoethane) — note that the double bond has become a single bond, and the two new C–Br bonds appear on adjacent carbon atoms (carbon 1 and carbon 2 — hence the name 1,2-dibromoethane).

*Why this is an addition reaction*

No atoms are lost. Every atom in ethene and bromine ends up in the product — there is no other molecule formed. This is the defining feature of addition: two reactants → one product, with 100% atom economy.

Extending to Other Alkenes

Any molecule containing a C=C double bond reacts with bromine in the same way. The Br₂ molecule adds across the double bond, putting one Br on each of the two carbons that used to share the double bond.

*Propene + bromine:*

CH₃CH=CH₂ + Br₂ → CH₃CHBrCH₂Br

The product is 1,2-dibromopropane. The "1,2" tells you which carbons the bromine atoms are attached to — carbon 1 and carbon 2 (counted from the end nearer the original double bond).

*Displayed structure of 1,2-dibromopropane:*

`` H H H | | | H—C — C — C—H | | | H Br Br ``

*But-2-ene + bromine:*

CH₃CH=CHCH₃ + Br₂ → CH₃CHBrCHBrCH₃

The product is 2,3-dibromobutane. Once again, the two bromine atoms add across the original double bond — onto carbons 2 and 3 of the butane chain.

*The general pattern*

For any alkene with formula CₙH₂ₙ:

CₙH₂ₙ + Br₂ → CₙH₂ₙBr₂

The number of bromine atoms added is always two, no matter how long the carbon chain — because each Br₂ molecule supplies exactly two Br atoms, and these go onto the two carbons of the (single) double bond.

The Bromine Water Test — Distinguishing Alkanes from Alkenes

Bromine water is dilute bromine dissolved in water. It has a distinctive orange/brown colour. When you shake an unknown hydrocarbon with bromine water in a test tube, you can tell whether it is an alkane or an alkene by what happens to the colour.

HydrocarbonWhat happens to bromine waterWhy
Alkene (e.g. ethene, propene)Orange/brown → colourlessThe C=C double bond adds Br₂ across it, forming a colourless dibromoalkane
Alkane (e.g. ethane, propane)Stays orange/brown (no change)Alkanes are saturated — no C=C bond, so no addition reaction can take place

*The crucial pair of observations*

A complete answer to "How does bromine water distinguish between alkanes and alkenes?" needs both halves:

  1. Alkene: bromine water changes from orange/brown to colourless
  2. Alkane: bromine water stays orange/brown (no change)

Stating only what happens with the alkene — without saying what happens (or doesn't happen) with the alkane — is incomplete.

Why the Test Works — the Chemistry Behind the Colour Change

Bromine (Br₂) is orange/brown in solution. When it reacts with an alkene by addition, it becomes a dibromoalkane (e.g. 1,2-dibromoethane), which is colourless. So the orange/brown colour disappears because the bromine has been chemically used up — there is no Br₂ left in the solution.

Alkanes do not react with bromine water at room temperature because they are saturated: every carbon is already bonded to four other atoms via single bonds. There is no C=C double bond to open up, so Br₂ stays in solution and the orange/brown colour stays.

This contrast — reactive C=C versus unreactive C–C — is the chemical reason alkenes are described as "more reactive" than alkanes. The C=C double bond is a region of high electron density that bromine readily attacks.

Colourless vs Clear — a Critical Distinction

The product solution after an alkene reacts with bromine water is colourless — meaning it has no colour. It is not correct to describe it as "clear". "Clear" means transparent (you can see through it) — but both orange/brown bromine water and a glass of dilute orange squash are also clear, so "clear" tells you nothing about colour.

  • Colourless = has no colour (e.g. water, the dibromoalkane solution)
  • Clear = transparent, not cloudy (independent of colour)

A solution can be clear and coloured (orange bromine water), clear and colourless (water), or cloudy and coloured (a precipitate suspended in dye). When describing the bromine water test, the required word is colourless.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — 2019 June Examiner Report: "A common misconception seen by examiners was that alkanes are more reactive than alkenes so the alkane must be the one that goes colourless." The reality is the opposite — it is the alkene that decolourises bromine water (because its C=C bond is reactive), while the alkane is unreactive and leaves bromine water unchanged.
  • 1CH0/2H — 2019 June Examiner Report: "Some were able to get the correct change but failed to state the original colour of the bromine water. Some candidates are still incorrectly referring to the colour change with ethene as clear rather than colourless... candidates would benefit with more guidance as to what is required by different command words so that if asked to describe what they would see, they should not then be trying to explain those observations." This gives three explicit lessons: (a) always state the starting colour (orange/brown), not just the end colour; (b) use colourless, not "clear"; (c) if the command word is "describe what you would see", give the observation only — do not explain the chemistry behind it.
  • 1CH0/2H — 2021 November Examiner Report: "It was disappointing to see the poor response to what is a straightforward question about using bromine water to distinguish between an alkene and an alkane. Few candidates scored full marks." Despite the topic being well within Foundation- and Higher-tier scope, candidates are not picking up easy marks here. The fix is to memorise the paired observation (orange/brown → colourless for the alkene; stays orange/brown for the alkane) and use the precise colour names.

Mathematical Skills

*Mathematical Skill 5b** — *Translate information between diagrammatic and numerical forms.

This is the skill called on when you have to draw the displayed formula of the product of an addition reaction. From the molecular equation:

C₂H₄ + Br₂ → C₂H₄Br₂

You must translate the molecular formula into a displayed structure showing every covalent bond:

`` H H H H | | | | C = C + Br—Br → C — C | | | | H H Br Br ``

*Key rules when drawing the product:*

  • The C=C double bond becomes a C–C single bond
  • One Br atom goes on each of the two carbons that used to be double-bonded
  • All original C–H bonds are kept
  • All bonds (including those to H) must be shown as straight lines — no shortcuts

How Bromine & Alkenes Appears in Exams

This spec point is regularly tested on Paper 2 of the Separate Chemistry award. Questions tend to fall into two categories.

*Category 1 — Equation completion / structure drawing*

You are given an alkene (with or without its structure shown) and asked to write the balanced equation for its reaction with bromine, or draw the displayed structure of the product.

  • Example: 1CH0/2H — 2024 June, Q10(a)(ii): "Complete the balanced equation for the reaction of hydrocarbon Z, C₄H₈, with bromine." (2 marks)

*Category 2 — Observation / "describe what you would see"*

You are given two unknown hydrocarbons (one alkane, one alkene) and asked how bromine water could be used to distinguish them. The required answer is the pair of observations (orange/brown → colourless for the alkene; no change for the alkane). Watch for the command word — if it says "describe what you would see", do not explain the chemistry; if it says "explain", you must also include the C=C / saturated reasoning.

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
Complete the equationFill in missing reactants, products, balancing numbers, or state symbols1–2
Describe what you would seeThe observation only — colour change (with starting and ending colours), no explanation1–2
ExplainObservation plus the reason — link the colour change to the C=C double bond and addition reaction2–3
StateA short factual answer (e.g. the name of the product)1
DrawA complete displayed formula — every atom and every bond shown1–2
Key Takeaways
  1. Alkenes react with bromine in an addition reaction: the C=C double bond opens up and one Br atom attaches to each of the two carbons.
  2. The product of ethene + bromine is 1,2-dibromoethane: CH₂=CH₂ + Br₂ → CH₂BrCH₂Br.
  3. The same pattern applies to other alkenes: propene + Br₂ → 1,2-dibromopropane; but-2-ene + Br₂ → 2,3-dibromobutane.
  4. Bromine water is an orange/brown solution of bromine dissolved in water — learn this colour exactly.
  5. An alkene turns bromine water from orange/brown to colourless (the bromine is used up in the addition reaction).
  6. An alkane does not react with bromine water — it stays orange/brown (alkanes are saturated, no C=C bond available).
  7. The correct word for the final solution is colourless — not "clear". Clear just means transparent; colourless means no colour at all.
  8. A full answer to the bromine water test needs the pair of observations: what happens with the alkene and what happens (or doesn't happen) with the alkane.
  9. Always state the starting colour (orange/brown) of bromine water as well as the final colour — examiners penalise answers that skip the original colour.
  10. If the command word is describe what you would see, give the observation only; if it is explain, also link the colour change to the C=C double bond opening up to allow addition.
  11. The general pattern for any alkene: CₙH₂ₙ + Br₂ → CₙH₂ₙBr₂ — two Br atoms added across the original double bond.
  12. The reactivity of alkenes (compared with the unreactivity of alkanes) is the central reason alkenes are useful starting materials in industry — for both addition reactions and addition polymerisation.
9.2.3.1 Combustion of Hydrocarbons F+H ▼

Combustion of Hydrocarbons

Spec 9.16C: Describe how the complete combustion of alkanes and alkenes involves the oxidation of the hydrocarbons to produce carbon dioxide and water.

Foundation + Higher tier · 1CH0 (Separate Chemistry) only · Paper 2

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
HydrocarbonA compound containing only hydrogen and carbon atoms
Complete combustionThe reaction of a fuel with sufficient (excess) oxygen so that all carbon is converted to carbon dioxide and all hydrogen to water
OxidationThe addition of oxygen to a substance (one of three Edexcel-accepted definitions; loss of electrons and increase in oxidation number are the others)
ExothermicA reaction that transfers energy to the surroundings, so the surroundings get hotter
AlkaneA saturated hydrocarbon with the general formula CₙH₂ₙ₊₂ — only single C–C bonds
AlkeneAn unsaturated hydrocarbon with the general formula CₙH₂ₙ — contains at least one C=C double bond

What complete combustion actually is

When a hydrocarbon burns in excess oxygen, every carbon atom in the molecule ends up bonded to oxygen as carbon dioxide, and every hydrogen atom ends up bonded to oxygen as water. There are only ever two products: carbon dioxide (CO₂) and water (H₂O). No other products are formed when combustion is complete — no carbon monoxide, no soot, and (importantly) no ethanoic acid, methanol, or any other partially oxidised organic species.

Three things happen at the same time during complete combustion:

  1. The hydrocarbon is oxidised. Oxygen atoms are added to both the carbon and the hydrogen in the molecule. This is oxidation by the simplest GCSE definition — addition of oxygen.
  2. Energy is released. Combustion reactions are strongly exothermic. The bonds formed in CO₂ and H₂O are stronger overall than the C–C, C–H, and O=O bonds broken in the reactants, so energy is transferred to the surroundings as heat (and light, as a flame).
  3. Oxygen is reduced. The O=O double bond in molecular oxygen is broken and oxygen atoms end up bonded to carbon and hydrogen. Although the spec wording focuses on the hydrocarbon being oxidised, the oxygen is simultaneously reduced — this is a redox reaction.

Why hydrocarbons are useful fuels

This is the chemistry that underlies the entire global fossil-fuel economy. The energy released per gram of hydrocarbon burned is very high, the products are gases (so they leave the engine or burner easily), and the only products of complete combustion are non-toxic. Alkanes from crude oil — methane in natural gas, propane and butane in LPG, octane in petrol, longer chains in diesel and kerosene — are all used because their complete combustion is highly exothermic and the products are CO₂ and H₂O.

The catch is that complete combustion only happens if there is enough oxygen. In a real engine, oxygen supply is often limited, and incomplete combustion produces carbon monoxide (a toxic gas) and soot (carbon particulates). That contrast is covered in 9.2.4.1; the focus of this SP is the complete reaction.

Balancing combustion equations — a reliable strategy

Combustion equations are balanced in the same order every time. Doing it any other way wastes time and tends to produce errors. The order is:

  1. Balance carbon first. Count the carbons in the hydrocarbon and put that number in front of CO₂.
  2. Balance hydrogen next. Count the hydrogens in the hydrocarbon, divide by 2, and put that number in front of H₂O.
  3. Balance oxygen last. Count the oxygens on the right-hand side (2 per CO₂ + 1 per H₂O) and divide by 2 to get the O₂ coefficient. If the answer is a fraction, multiply every coefficient by 2 to clear it.

The reason oxygen is left until last is that the only oxygen on the left-hand side is the O₂ being balanced. Carbon and hydrogen only have one source each on the left (the hydrocarbon), so you can fix the right-hand side coefficients first and then work backwards to find how much O₂ you need.

*Worked example — complete combustion of ethene (C₂H₄)*

This is the canonical Edexcel example because it is the equation flagged in the June 2019 1CH0/2H examiner report.

Reactants: C₂H₄ + O₂. Products: CO₂ + H₂O.

Step 1 — Balance C. Ethene has 2 carbons, so 2 CO₂ on the right: > C₂H₄ + O₂ → 2CO₂ + H₂O

Step 2 — Balance H. Ethene has 4 hydrogens, so 4/2 = 2 H₂O on the right: > C₂H₄ + O₂ → 2CO₂ + 2H₂O

Step 3 — Balance O. Right-hand side now has (2 × 2) + (2 × 1) = 6 oxygen atoms. Divide by 2 to get the O₂ coefficient: 6 ÷ 2 = 3. > C₂H₄ + 3O₂ → 2CO₂ + 2H₂O

This equation already has whole numbers, so no further scaling is needed. The mole ratio is 1 : 3 : 2 : 2 — one mole of ethene reacts with three moles of oxygen to give two moles of carbon dioxide and two moles of water.

*Worked example — complete combustion of propene (C₃H₆)*

Step 1 — Balance C. Three carbons → 3 CO₂: > C₃H₆ + O₂ → 3CO₂ + H₂O

Step 2 — Balance H. Six hydrogens → 6/2 = 3 H₂O: > C₃H₆ + O₂ → 3CO₂ + 3H₂O

Step 3 — Balance O. Right-hand side now has (3 × 2) + (3 × 1) = 9 oxygen atoms. 9 ÷ 2 = 4.5, which is a fraction. > C₃H₆ + 4.5O₂ → 3CO₂ + 3H₂O

Multiply everything by 2 to clear the fraction: > 2C₃H₆ + 9O₂ → 6CO₂ + 6H₂O

Mole ratio: 2 : 9 : 6 : 6.

Combustion equations for the alkanes

AlkaneFormulaBalanced equation for complete combustion
MethaneCH₄CH₄ + 2O₂ → CO₂ + 2H₂O
EthaneC₂H₆2C₂H₆ + 7O₂ → 4CO₂ + 6H₂O
PropaneC₃H₈C₃H₈ + 5O₂ → 3CO₂ + 4H₂O
ButaneC₄H₁₀2C₄H₁₀ + 13O₂ → 8CO₂ + 10H₂O

Methane is the simplest case and worth memorising — it is the main component of natural gas, so it is the equation that appears most often in real exam contexts. Ethane and butane both end up with fractional O₂ coefficients before scaling, which is why their balanced equations begin with a 2.

Combustion equations for the alkenes

AlkeneFormulaBalanced equation for complete combustion
EtheneC₂H₄C₂H₄ + 3O₂ → 2CO₂ + 2H₂O
PropeneC₃H₆2C₃H₆ + 9O₂ → 6CO₂ + 6H₂O
ButeneC₄H₈C₄H₈ + 6O₂ → 4CO₂ + 4H₂O

Notice that alkenes need slightly less oxygen per carbon atom than the corresponding alkane — for example, ethane needs 3.5 O₂ per molecule but ethene needs only 3 O₂. This is because alkenes have fewer hydrogens per carbon than alkanes (CₙH₂ₙ vs CₙH₂ₙ₊₂), so there is less H₂O to form and therefore fewer oxygen atoms required overall.

State symbols (Higher tier expectation)

At Higher tier you may be asked to include state symbols. Under the conditions in which combustion happens (high temperatures, in a flame):

  • Hydrocarbons that are gases at room temperature (methane, ethane, propane, butane, ethene, propene, butene) → (g)
  • Oxygen → (g)
  • Carbon dioxide → (g)
  • Water → (g) — at flame temperatures water is a gas (steam), not a liquid

A typical fully-state-symboled equation:

> C₂H₄ (g) + 3O₂ (g) → 2CO₂ (g) + 2H₂O (g)

If the question specifies water collected and cooled at room temperature, (l) for water becomes acceptable — read the question.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2H — June 2019 Examiner Report (complete combustion of ethene): "Where candidates lost marks, it was often as they gave the formula of oxygen as O rather than O₂. Others lost marks as they were not aware that the products of the complete combustion were carbon dioxide and water with a common misconception being that ethanoic acid would be produced."

Three separate errors are flagged in this single comment, and all three are worth treating as named hazards:

  1. Writing O instead of O₂. Oxygen exists as a diatomic molecule. Writing the reactant as O (atomic oxygen) is treated as a formula error and loses the mark even if the rest of the equation is balanced around it.
  2. Inventing the wrong product. "Ethanoic acid" (CH₃COOH) is what you get from the partial oxidation of ethanol — not from the combustion of ethene. Students who half-remembered an organic oxidation reaction from elsewhere in the spec wrote ethanoic acid here. The only products of complete combustion are CO₂ and H₂O.
  3. Missing a product entirely. Some students wrote just CO₂ or just H₂O. Both products must appear, in the correct ratio, with the equation balanced.

How Combustion of Hydrocarbons Appears in Exams

*Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H) — mid-to-late questions in the Separate Chemistry organic section.*

  • Balanced-equation writing. The most common task. Candidates are given a hydrocarbon and asked to write the balanced equation for its complete combustion. Higher-tier versions usually require state symbols.
  • Short-explain questions. "Describe what is meant by complete combustion" or "Explain why complete combustion of a hydrocarbon releases energy" — typically 2–3 marks. The expected answer references CO₂ and H₂O as the only products, oxidation of the hydrocarbon, and the exothermic energy release.
  • Canonical past-paper appearance. The complete combustion of ethene was examined in 1CH0/2H, June 2019, which is the source of the candidate-error pattern flagged above. This question is the reference point for the topic: students are expected to be able to write C₂H₄ + 3O₂ → 2CO₂ + 2H₂O with confidence.
  • Cross-topic links. Combustion appears alongside questions on cracking (long alkane → shorter alkane + alkene) and on incomplete combustion (CO and soot as alternative products when oxygen is limited).

Command Words for this SP

Command wordWhat it requiresTypical mark tariff
WriteThe balanced symbol equation for the combustion reaction, with correct formulae and integer coefficients2–3
CompleteFill in missing species or coefficients in a partially-given equation1–2
BalanceAdd coefficients to an equation with correct formulae already given1
DescribeA short prose explanation of what complete combustion is (products, oxygen requirement, oxidation, energy release)2–3

Mathematical Skills

1c — use ratios, fractions and percentages. Balancing combustion equations is fundamentally a ratio exercise. The four coefficients in C₂H₄ + 3O₂ → 2CO₂ + 2H₂O give the 1 : 3 : 2 : 2 mole ratio. If a follow-up question asks how many moles of CO₂ are produced from a given number of moles of ethene, you scale the ratio:

> moles of CO₂ = moles of C₂H₄ × (2 ÷ 1) = 2 × moles of C₂H₄

Fractional coefficients during balancing (e.g. 4.5 O₂ for propene) get cleared by multiplying every coefficient by the smallest whole number that removes the fraction — almost always × 2.

Key Takeaways
  1. Complete combustion of a hydrocarbon is its reaction with excess oxygen to give only carbon dioxide and water — there are no other products.
  2. Complete combustion is exothermic — it transfers energy to the surroundings — and is the reason hydrocarbons are used as fuels.
  3. The hydrocarbon is oxidised (oxygen is added to both the carbon and the hydrogen); oxygen is simultaneously reduced — it is a redox reaction.
  4. Balance combustion equations in the order C → H → O. If the O₂ coefficient comes out as a fraction, multiply every coefficient by 2 to clear it.
  5. Methane: CH₄ + 2O₂ → CO₂ + 2H₂O.
  6. Ethene (canonical Edexcel equation): C₂H₄ + 3O₂ → 2CO₂ + 2H₂O — mole ratio 1 : 3 : 2 : 2.
  7. Propene: 2C₃H₆ + 9O₂ → 6CO₂ + 6H₂O. Butene: C₄H₈ + 6O₂ → 4CO₂ + 4H₂O.
  8. Oxygen in an equation must always be written as O₂, never as O — writing O (atomic oxygen) was a specific source of lost marks in 1CH0/2H June 2019.
  9. The products of complete combustion are only CO₂ and H₂O — not ethanoic acid or any other partially oxidised species. The June 2019 ethanoic-acid misconception was flagged by examiners.
  10. At Higher tier, state symbols may be required — under combustion conditions all species are gases, so (g) is normally used throughout, including for water as steam.
  11. Alkenes need slightly less oxygen per molecule than the corresponding alkane (e.g. C₂H₄ needs 3 O₂; C₂H₆ needs 3.5 O₂) because they have fewer hydrogens to convert to water.
  12. Memorise the four alkane and three alkene equations in the tables above — they are high-frequency Paper 2 content and account for the majority of combustion-balancing marks at GCSE.
9.3.1.1 Polymers F+H ▼

Polymers

Spec point 9.17C — Recall that a polymer is a substance of high average relative molecular mass made up of small repeating units. Foundation and Higher tier, Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only, Paper 2.

This spec point introduces the definition of a polymer. It is the gateway concept for the whole of Topic 9.3 — every later piece of polymer chemistry (addition polymerisation, condensation polymerisation, natural polymers, polymer properties) builds on the idea that a polymer is one long molecule formed by joining many identical small units together. Get the definition watertight here and the rest of the topic becomes much easier.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
PolymerA substance of high average relative molecular mass made up of small repeating units
MonomerA small molecule that can join with many others (of the same or different type) to form a polymer
Repeating unitThe smallest section of a polymer chain that, when repeated n times, gives the full polymer structure
PolymerisationThe chemical reaction in which many monomer molecules join together to form a polymer
MacromoleculeAny very large molecule formed from many smaller subunits — polymers are one type of macromolecule
Degree of polymerisation (n)The number of repeating units in a single polymer chain

What a polymer actually is

A polymer is a long-chain molecule built by chemically bonding together a large number of small molecules called monomers. The word comes from the Greek poly- meaning "many" and -mer meaning "part". Because so many monomer units join into one molecule, the resulting chain has a very high relative molecular mass — typically tens of thousands to several million, compared with relative molecular masses of around 28–200 for the monomers themselves.

Three features sit at the heart of the spec-point definition, and you must include all three in any "what is a polymer" answer:

  1. It is a substance (a chemical compound, not a mixture).
  2. Its molecules have a high average relative molecular mass.
  3. The structure is built from small repeating units.

The word "average" matters. Real polymer samples are not all one length — different chains in the same lump of polymer contain different numbers of repeating units, so the relative molecular mass is quoted as an average over the sample. This is different from a small molecule such as water or ethene, where every molecule has the same Mr.

Why "high average relative molecular mass" is in the definition

The high relative molecular mass is what gives polymers their characteristic physical properties:

  • Solid at room temperature — the chains are too heavy and too entangled to flow easily.
  • Strong and often flexible — many points of contact between chains create many intermolecular forces, even if each individual force is weak.
  • Often insoluble in water — the chains are too large to be solvated.
  • Mouldable on heating (for thermoplastics) — the chains slide past one another but do not break apart into small molecules.

A short chain of just three or four ethene units would not behave like a useful plastic; you need thousands of monomer units joined end-to-end before the bulk material starts to feel like poly(ethene). The definition therefore highlights the high average relative molecular mass specifically — it is the size of the chain that delivers the macroscopic behaviour.

Why "small repeating units" is in the definition

If you look at a polymer chain, you will see the same chemical pattern over and over again. That pattern is the repeating unit, and it is what the polymer "remembers" of its starting monomer. For example, when ethene (CH₂=CH₂) polymerises to make poly(ethene), the C=C double bond opens and each ethene unit becomes a –CH₂–CH₂– section of the chain. The repeating unit is therefore –(CH₂–CH₂)–, drawn inside square brackets with an n outside the bracket:

`` ⎡ H H ⎤ ⎢ | | ⎥ — ⎢ —C — C—⎥ — ⎢ | | ⎥ ⎣ H H ⎦ n ``

The n is essential: it tells you that this short bracketed section repeats n times along the chain, where n is a large whole number. Dropping the n is one of the most penalised errors at GCSE — see the examiner reports below.

Building up a polymer chain

A useful mental picture is a string of paperclips. Each paperclip is a monomer, and the finished chain is the polymer. Three points follow from this:

  • One type of monomer → one type of repeating unit. Poly(ethene) is built from ethene only, so the repeating unit is just the ethene fragment –CH₂–CH₂–.
  • Two types of monomer (in some polymers) → a more complex repeating unit. Condensation polymers like nylon and polyesters use two different monomers; the repeating unit then contains a piece of each.
  • The "average" is necessary because not every chain is the same length. Real samples have a distribution of chain lengths.

How a polymer chain relates to its monomer mass

The relative molecular mass of a polymer chain is approximately:

`` Mr(polymer) = n × Mr(monomer repeating unit) ``

Worked example — poly(ethene):

The repeating unit –CH₂–CH₂– contains 2 carbons (2 × 12 = 24) and 4 hydrogens (4 × 1 = 4), giving an Mr of 28 per repeating unit. If a particular poly(ethene) sample has n ≈ 1000 repeating units per chain, then:

`` Mr(polymer) ≈ 1000 × 28 = 28 000 ``

That is about a thousand times the Mr of any single ethene molecule, which is exactly the kind of "high average relative molecular mass" the spec point refers to.

A second worked example is given in the Mathematical Skills section below.

Comparing monomers and polymers

FeatureMonomerPolymer
SizeSmall moleculeLong-chain molecule (macromolecule)
Relative molecular massLow (typically 28–200)Very high (often 10 000 – 1 000 000+)
State at room temperatureOften gas or liquid (e.g. ethene is a gas)Usually solid
VolatilityHigh (low boiling point)Very low (high boiling/decomposition point)
Number of units in one moleculeOne unitMany repeating units (n is large)
ReactivityOften contains a C=C double bond or a functional group able to react furtherThe repeating-unit backbone is usually unreactive (single bonds only, for addition polymers)

Common errors with the definition

When answering a "what is a polymer?" question, avoid these slips:

  • Saying "lots of monomers joined together" without mentioning high Mr or repeating units. The spec definition has three parts; an answer missing any of them is incomplete.
  • Calling a polymer "a mixture of monomers" — it is a single substance, not a mixture, and it is built from monomers that have reacted, not just been mixed.
  • Forgetting the word "average" when describing the relative molecular mass — different chains in a sample have different lengths.
  • Drawing a repeating unit without the n subscript outside the brackets — this is a frequent loss of marks (see Examiner Report Insights).
  • Writing the polymer name with bits missing, e.g. "polyfluoroethene" instead of "poly(tetrafluoroethene)" — the prefix and the brackets are part of the IUPAC name.

Naming polymers

Most addition polymers are named by adding "poly(...)" in front of the monomer name in brackets:

MonomerPolymer
ethenepoly(ethene)
propenepoly(propene)
chloroethenepoly(chloroethene)
tetrafluoroethenepoly(tetrafluoroethene)
phenylethenepoly(phenylethene)

Keep the brackets and keep every part of the monomer name. "tetra" and the "(...)" punctuation both count — examiners specifically flag these as common omissions.

Examiner Report Insights

The definition itself is short, but examiners care about precise terminology across the whole of Topic 9.3. The two main warnings transferable to this spec point are:

  • 1CH0/2F — 2019 June Examiner Report: "missing out the letter 'n' following the square brackets in the repeating unit for poly(ethene); missing out the word 'tetra' from the name of the polymer." Translation for this spec point: when you write a repeating unit or name a polymer, every character matters. Lose the n and you lose the mark.
  • 1CH0/2H — 2021 November Examiner Report: "Polymer formation from monomers is generally well understood, although some candidates lack the appropriate literacy skills in explaining the process." Translation: candidates know roughly what is happening, but score badly when they cannot put it into clear, technical English. Practise writing the definition out word-for-word so you can produce it under time pressure.

Mathematical Skills

The relevant mathematical skill for this spec point is ratios/fractions (skill 1c): scaling the relative formula mass of a repeating unit up by n to give the Mr of a whole chain.

Formula:

`` Mr(polymer chain) = n × Mr(repeating unit) ``

Worked procedure (from a real Edexcel-style data point):

  1. Identify the repeating unit and write its molecular formula.
  2. Calculate its Mr from atomic masses.
  3. Multiply by n, the degree of polymerisation given in the question.
  4. Round sensibly (usually to 3 sig figs).

Worked example using NLM data — 1CH0/2F June 2019 Q3(d) referenced n = 24 600 and a repeating-unit-related mass of 42.0:

`` Mr(polymer chain) = 24 600 × 42.0 = 1 033 200 ≈ 1.03 × 10⁶ ``

So a single chain of this polymer has a relative molecular mass of about one million. That order of magnitude (10⁵–10⁶) is typical for commercial plastics and is exactly the "high average relative molecular mass" the spec definition is talking about.

How Polymers Appears in Exams

NLM data for this spec point:

  • Position and type: The definition of a polymer typically anchors the opening part of a longer polymer question on Paper 2 — students are usually asked to state what a polymer is for 1–2 marks before the question moves on to drawing repeating units, identifying monomers from polymer structures, or distinguishing addition from condensation polymerisation.
  • Both tiers: The "Recall" wording in the spec means a basic definition is fair game at Foundation as well as Higher. Higher candidates may be asked the same definition but in the context of a comparison (e.g. with a small-molecule covalent substance) or as part of an extended-response question.
  • Linked content: Once the definition is stated, the question normally proceeds into addition polymerisation (Topic 9.3.2) or condensation polymerisation (Topic 9.3.3), so spec point 9.3.1.1 is rarely seen in isolation — it lives at the start of a multi-part stem.

For a worked appearance in a maths-skills role, see the 1CH0/2F June 2019 Q3(d) example above: a relative-formula-mass calculation involving n × 42.0 with n = 24 600.

Command Words table

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateA short factual answer with no explanation — give the definition cleanly1
RecallIdentical in scope to "state"; reproduce the spec-defined fact1
ExplainReasoning that links cause and effect, e.g. why polymers have high Mr or why they are solids at room temperature2–4
DescribeA short prose answer covering the main points, e.g. describing polymer formation from monomers2–3

NLM-confirmed command words for this spec point: State, Recall, Explain.

Key Takeaways
  1. A polymer is a substance of high average relative molecular mass made up of small repeating units — get all three parts of that definition into any "what is a polymer" answer.
  2. Polymers are made by joining many monomer molecules together in a chemical reaction called polymerisation.
  3. The repeating unit is the short pattern that appears over and over along the polymer chain; it is what the polymer "inherits" from its monomer.
  4. The repeating unit is written inside square brackets with an n outside the bracket to show that it repeats n times — dropping the n loses marks (1CH0/2F June 2019 examiner report).
  5. The relative molecular mass of a polymer chain is approximately n × Mr(repeating unit), and can reach 10⁴ to 10⁶ for real plastics.
  6. The word "average" appears in the definition because real polymer samples contain chains of slightly different lengths.
  7. Polymers are single substances, not mixtures — the monomers have reacted, not just been mixed.
  8. Polymer names follow the pattern poly(monomer name) — keep every part of the name including any "tetra" prefix and the brackets (1CH0/2F June 2019 examiner report).
  9. The high relative molecular mass explains polymer properties: solids at room temperature, often strong and mouldable, low volatility, often insoluble in water.
  10. Spec point 9.3.1.1 is Foundation + Higher and is examined only on Paper 2 of 1CH0 (Separate Chemistry); it usually anchors the start of a longer polymer question rather than appearing as a stand-alone item.
  11. Candidates "generally understand" polymer formation but lose marks through weak technical literacy (1CH0/2H November 2021 examiner report) — practise saying the definition out loud until the wording is automatic.
  12. This spec point is the foundation for the rest of Topic 9.3 — get the definition watertight here and addition polymers (9.3.2), condensation polymers (9.3.3), and natural polymers (9.3.5) all become easier.
9.3.2.1 Formation of Polythene F+H ▼

Formation of Polythene

Spec 9.18C — Foundation + Higher tier, Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only, Paper 2. Conditions and mechanisms are **not** required at GCSE.

Poly(ethene) — commonly called polythene — is the simplest and most familiar addition polymer. Around the world it is the polymer used to make plastic bags, squeezy bottles, cling film, drink-bottle caps, and food packaging. The fact that one of the most useful materials of the modern world is built from the smallest alkene (ethene, C₂H₄) is a powerful demonstration of how a tiny change in chemistry — opening one double bond — turns a flammable gas into a solid plastic.

This spec point is purely descriptive: you need to be able to explain in words and in diagrams how many ethene molecules combine to form a long-chain molecule, name the product, and draw the repeating unit. You are not required at GCSE to give the catalyst, the temperature, the pressure, or the radical mechanism.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
MonomerA small molecule, containing a C=C double bond, that can join with many identical molecules to form a polymer
PolymerA very large molecule (a macromolecule) made by joining together many small monomer molecules
Addition polymerisationA reaction in which many monomer molecules containing C=C double bonds join together to form a single long-chain polymer molecule, with no other product formed
