Repeating unitThe small section of a polymer chain that, when repeated many times, makes up the whole polymer
Poly(ethene)The addition polymer formed when many ethene (C₂H₄) molecules join together; also called polythene or polyethylene
nThe (very large) number of monomer units that join together to form one polymer molecule

What ethene looks like and why it can polymerise

Ethene is the simplest alkene. Its molecular formula is C₂H₄ and its displayed structure shows two carbon atoms joined by a double bond, with two hydrogen atoms on each carbon:

`` H H \\ // C=C / \\ H H ``

The C=C double bond is the functional group of an alkene and it is the key to addition polymerisation. A double bond is made of two shared pairs of electrons between the same two carbon atoms. One of those pairs is much more reactive than a single C–C bond: it can open up so that each carbon has a "spare" bonding position that can be used to join to a neighbouring molecule. When this happens to thousands of ethene molecules at once, they link end-to-end into a single long chain.

Because alkanes (such as ethane, C₂H₆) only have single C–C bonds, they cannot polymerise in this way. This is why polymer chemistry uses alkenes — and why cracking (which makes alkenes from longer alkane molecules in crude oil) is so industrially important. The ethene used to make poly(ethene) almost all comes from steam cracking of hydrocarbon fractions from crude oil.

How the polymerisation happens (qualitatively)

In addition polymerisation:

  1. Many ethene molecules come together — typically several thousand.
  2. The C=C double bond in each ethene molecule opens up to leave a single C–C bond plus two new bonding positions, one on each carbon.
  3. Those new bonding positions link to the neighbouring ethene molecules.
  4. The result is a single long chain in which every C is joined to its neighbour by a single bond, and every C still has its two hydrogens.
  5. No other product is formed. The whole monomer is built into the polymer — that's why this is called addition polymerisation (compare with condensation polymerisation, where small molecules such as water are released).

Because every atom in the monomer ends up in the polymer, the polymer has exactly the same empirical formula as the monomer. This is a useful check when you draw your answer: count the atoms in the repeating unit; the ratio must match C₂H₄.

Drawing the equation for the formation of poly(ethene)

The standard GCSE way to show the formation of poly(ethene) is:

`` H H ┌ H H ┐ \\ // | | | | n C=C ────► | — C — C — | / \\ | | | | H H └ H H ┘ ₙ ``

In writing:

n CH₂=CH₂ → −(−CH₂−CH₂−)−ₙ

There are four crucial features of this diagram that students lose marks on:

  • The n in front of the monomer. This tells the examiner that a large number of ethene molecules are reacting, not just one. Without the "n" you have not shown polymerisation.
  • The n outside the bracket on the right. This shows that the repeating unit is repeated many times in the chain. The "n" after the bracket and the "n" before the monomer must match.
  • The square brackets (or round brackets) around the repeating unit. They define what is repeated.
  • The two extension bonds sticking out of the left and right of the repeating unit. These represent the bonds to the next repeating units in the chain. Without them you have drawn a single molecule, not a polymer.

The single bond between the two carbons (where the double bond used to be) is a single line. Inside the repeating unit the C=C double bond of ethene has gone — it is now a C–C single bond. This is the visual signature that addition polymerisation has happened.

Why the polymer is called poly(ethene)

The IUPAC system for naming addition polymers is:

*poly( monomer name )*

So ethene → poly(ethene), propene → poly(propene), chloroethene → poly(chloroethene), tetrafluoroethene → poly(tetrafluoroethene). The brackets matter: they remind you that the whole monomer name sits inside, and they prevent ambiguity for monomers whose names contain numbers or substituents. The older industrial name polyethylene (and the everyday name polythene) refer to exactly the same substance — these alternative names are accepted in many specifications but Edexcel uses the IUPAC name poly(ethene) in mark schemes.

Properties of poly(ethene) and why it is useful

Poly(ethene) is a long, flexible, saturated hydrocarbon chain. The bulk properties that make it useful follow directly from that chain structure:

  • It is a solid at room temperature because the long chains are tangled together and the intermolecular forces between many CH₂ groups along a 10 000-carbon chain add up to a lot, even though each individual force is weak.
  • It is flexible because the chains can slide past one another.
  • It is unreactive because there are no C=C double bonds or other reactive groups left after polymerisation — it is essentially a giant alkane.
  • It is electrically insulating and waterproof, which is why it is used for cable insulation, bags, bottles and food wrapping.
  • It is non-biodegradable, which is why waste poly(ethene) is a major environmental problem (covered in 9.3.2.4).

Worked example — finding the relative formula mass of poly(ethene)

A typical Higher-style calculation links the monomer's relative formula mass (Mᵣ) to the polymer's Mᵣ via the number of repeating units, n.

Question: A sample of poly(ethene) is made from 24 600 ethene molecules joining together. Calculate the relative formula mass of one molecule of this poly(ethene).

*Step 1 — Mᵣ of ethene:*

Mᵣ(C₂H₄) = (2 × 12) + (4 × 1) = 24 + 4 = 28

Wait — check the monomer's Mᵣ: the spec talks about repeating units of −CH₂−CH₂−, which is also C₂H₄ in atom count, so Mᵣ of the repeating unit = 28 as well. This is what we expect — every atom from the monomer ends up in the polymer.

*Step 2 — multiply by n:*

Mᵣ(poly(ethene)) = n × Mᵣ(repeating unit) = 24 600 × 28

24 600 × 28 = 688 800

*Answer:** Mᵣ of the poly(ethene) molecule = **688 800*

The general formula you'll keep using is:

`` Mr(polymer) = n × Mr(repeating unit) ``

Note that for poly(ethene) the repeating-unit Mᵣ equals the monomer Mᵣ (because no atoms are lost). This is a general feature of all addition polymers and is worth remembering: it is what distinguishes addition from condensation polymerisation.

Mathematical Skills

Edexcel maps this spec point to two mathematical skills:

  • 5b — Translate information between diagrammatic and numerical forms. You must be able to look at a drawn poly(ethene) repeating unit and write the molecular formula C₂H₄, and vice versa. You will also be expected to look at an addition polymer's structure and draw the monomer (and the other way round). This is the single highest-yield skill in this topic.
  • 1c — Use ratios and fractions. Used when you scale from monomer Mᵣ to polymer Mᵣ (as in the worked example above), or from polymer mass to number of monomers.

A useful procedure for the polymer-Mᵣ style calculation:

  1. Identify the monomer and write its molecular formula.
  2. Calculate Mᵣ of the monomer using the periodic table values.
  3. Multiply by the number of repeating units, n, given in the question.
  4. State the answer (no units — Mᵣ is a ratio).

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — June 2019 Examiner Report: When candidates were asked to draw repeating units and monomers for addition polymers, "the most commonly scored mark... was invariably for drawing the correct monomer for poly(chloroethene)." The most common errors reported were "missing out the letter 'n' following the square brackets in the repeating unit for poly(ethene); missing out the word 'tetra' from the name of the polymer." The take-home for poly(ethene) specifically: the "n" outside the square brackets is examined, and losing it is one of the most common mark losses on this whole topic.
  • 1CH0/2H — November 2021 Examiner Report: "Polymer formation from monomers is generally well understood, although some candidates lack the appropriate literacy skills in explaining the process. Better candidates produced good diagrams." The lesson: a clear, fully-labelled diagram (with the "n", brackets and extension bonds) is often easier to score on than a written description, and the best candidates back up their words with a diagram.

How Formation of Polythene Appears in Exams

This spec point appears on Paper 2 of the Separate Chemistry qualification (1CH0). It is not assessed on Combined Science.

  • Position and type: Polymer questions are found throughout Paper 2 and very commonly take the form of a table-completion task, where you must complete missing cells with a drawn monomer, a drawn repeating unit, or a polymer name. Typical tariff is 2–3 marks for completing the relevant row(s).
  • Command words used: "Draw", "State", and "Explain". "Draw" is by far the most common for this SP — you are usually drawing either the monomer (ethene) given the repeating unit, or the repeating unit given the monomer.
  • Worked appearance: 1CH0/2F June 2019 Q3(a) — candidates had to complete a table giving the structure of poly(ethene), the structure of chloroethene, and the name of poly(tetrafluoroethene). Worth 3 marks in total. The same paper, Q3(d), used the calculation 24 600 × 42.0 to find the Mᵣ of a polymer from the Mᵣ of the monomer — exactly the style of calculation in the worked example above.

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
DrawA clear, fully-labelled structural diagram. For a polymer, you must include the "n", brackets and extension bonds; for a monomer, you must include the C=C double bond1–2
StateA short factual answer with no explanation (e.g. the name of the polymer, or the type of polymerisation)1
ExplainA reasoned sentence that links cause and effect (e.g. why ethene can polymerise but ethane cannot)2–3

Common student mistakes to avoid

  • Drawing the double bond still in place in the repeating unit. The C=C of ethene has been used up in joining the chains — inside the repeating unit it is now a single C–C bond.
  • Forgetting the two extension bonds out of the sides of the repeating unit. Without them you have not drawn a section of polymer chain.
  • Missing the n before the monomer or after the bracket.
  • Writing the polymer name without brackets — Edexcel mark schemes specifically use "poly(ethene)" with the brackets.
  • Forgetting that all of the monomer ends up in the polymer — there is no small molecule (such as water) produced in addition polymerisation.
  • Confusing addition polymerisation (this SP) with condensation polymerisation (not on this spec point but introduced elsewhere).
Key Takeaways
  1. Ethene (C₂H₄) is the monomer used to make poly(ethene); it contains a C=C double bond that opens up during polymerisation.
  2. Addition polymerisation joins many ethene molecules into a single long chain with no other product formed.
  3. The product is called poly(ethene) — the IUPAC name, with brackets. "Polythene" and "polyethylene" mean the same substance.
  4. The equation is n CH₂=CH₂ → −(−CH₂−CH₂−)−ₙ, where n is a large number (typically thousands).
  5. The repeating unit is −CH₂−CH₂− inside square (or round) brackets, with two extension bonds and a subscript n.
  6. Inside the repeating unit the C=C of ethene has become a C–C single bond — drawing the double bond still in place is a common mark-loser.
  7. Every atom of the monomer ends up in the polymer, so Mᵣ(repeating unit) = Mᵣ(monomer) and Mᵣ(polymer) = n × Mᵣ(monomer).
  8. Conditions and mechanisms are not required at GCSE — do not waste time writing about catalysts, temperatures or radicals.
  9. Ethene comes industrially from cracking long-chain alkanes from crude oil.
  10. Poly(ethene) is widely used for bags, bottles, films and cable insulation because it is solid at room temperature, flexible, unreactive, waterproof and an electrical insulator.
  11. This SP is Separate Chemistry only (1CH0) and is examined on Paper 2 at both Foundation and Higher tier.
  12. Examiner reports flag the missing "n" outside the bracket and missing brackets in polymer names as the most common errors — a clearly drawn diagram with all labels in place is the safest way to score.
9.3.2.2 Other Addition Polymers F+H ▼

Other Addition Polymers

Syllabus 9.19C — Describe how other addition polymers can be made by combining together other monomer molecules containing C=C, to include poly(propene), poly(chloroethene) (PVC) and poly(tetrafluoroethene) (PTFE) (conditions and mechanisms not required).

This spec point extends the addition-polymerisation idea from ethene to three further named monomers. Foundation and Higher candidates need to recognise the monomer, draw the repeating unit, and name the polymer correctly. The conditions (high pressure, catalyst) and the radical mechanism are explicitly not examined here.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
Addition polymerA long-chain molecule made when many small monomer units, each containing a C=C double bond, join together with no other product formed
MonomerA small molecule containing a C=C double bond that can be linked to many others to form an addition polymer
Repeating unitThe shortest section of a polymer chain that, repeated n times, gives the whole polymer; drawn with a pair of "trailing" bonds extending through square brackets and a subscript n outside
Poly(propene)The addition polymer formed from propene (CH₂=CH–CH₃); has a methyl (–CH₃) branch on every other carbon of the backbone
Poly(chloroethene), PVCThe addition polymer formed from chloroethene (CH₂=CHCl); has a chlorine atom on every other carbon
Poly(tetrafluoroethene), PTFEThe addition polymer formed from tetrafluoroethene (CF₂=CF₂); every backbone carbon carries two fluorine atoms

How any addition polymer is built

A monomer must contain a C=C double bond. Under high pressure and with a suitable catalyst (not assessable here), the double bond breaks open and the carbons use those freed electrons to bond to the next monomer in the chain. The process repeats thousands of times to give a saturated, long-chain polymer.

Key features that hold for every addition polymer:

  • The polymer backbone is fully single-bonded — every C=C in the monomers has been used up.
  • No small molecule is lost (no water, no HCl, no anything). The atoms in n monomers are all present in the polymer; this is why mass is conserved across the reaction.
  • The repeating unit has the same molecular formula as the monomer, just drawn as a single-bonded fragment with two trailing bonds extending out of the square brackets.
  • A subscript n outside the brackets tells you the unit repeats many (typically thousands of) times.

Drawing the three named monomers and repeating units

Each monomer is built from the ethene template (CH₂=CH₂) by swapping one or more hydrogens for a different group. The trick is to identify which hydrogens have been replaced, keep the C=C in place when drawing the monomer, and then redraw the same carbon skeleton with the double bond opened out and trailing bonds added when drawing the repeating unit.

*Poly(propene) — monomer is propene*

Propene is ethene with one hydrogen on a carbon swapped for a methyl (–CH₃) group:

Monomer: CH₂=CH–CH₃

Repeating unit (displayed): a two-carbon backbone where one carbon carries two hydrogens and the other carries one hydrogen and one –CH₃, with trailing bonds extending out of the brackets:

`` H H | | –[ C – C ]– | | n H CH₃ ``

*Poly(chloroethene) — monomer is chloroethene*

Chloroethene is ethene with one hydrogen replaced by a chlorine atom:

Monomer: CH₂=CHCl

Repeating unit:

`` H H | | –[ C – C ]– | | n H Cl ``

*Poly(tetrafluoroethene) — monomer is tetrafluoroethene*

Tetrafluoroethene is ethene with all four hydrogens replaced by fluorine atoms:

Monomer: CF₂=CF₂

Repeating unit:

`` F F | | –[ C – C ]– | | n F F ``

Comparison table — the three "other" addition polymers

PolymerMonomer formulaRepeating unit (simplified)Distinguishing feature
Poly(propene)CH₂=CH–CH₃–[CH₂–CH(CH₃)]– nA methyl branch on alternate carbons
Poly(chloroethene) (PVC)CH₂=CHCl–[CH₂–CHCl]– nOne chlorine atom on alternate carbons
Poly(tetrafluoroethene) (PTFE)CF₂=CF₂–[CF₂–CF₂]– nAll four backbone hydrogens replaced by fluorine

Why the structures look the way they do

Why every repeating unit shows two carbons, not one. The monomer is built around a C=C bond. When that double bond opens, both of those carbons end up in the polymer, joined by a single bond. So even though the monomer often looks compact, the repeating unit always shows a two-carbon backbone inside the brackets.

Why the trailing bonds extend through the brackets. Those bonds are the points where this unit links to the next one along the chain. Forgetting to draw them is a very common error — it leaves the molecule looking like an isolated alkane, not a section of a polymer. Lines must stick out through the square brackets on both ends.

Why n matters. The n outside the brackets is what makes the structure a polymer rather than a single repeating unit. Without it, what you have drawn is just a small molecule, not a long-chain macromolecule. Missing the n is one of the most frequently penalised errors at this level (see Examiner Report Insights below).

Why naming with poly( ) matters. Polymer names are written as poly(monomer). The brackets around the monomer name are part of the polymer's name — leaving them off, or dropping the "tetra" from "poly(tetrafluoroethene)", costs the naming mark.

Worked example — building the repeating unit of PVC from chloroethene

Question: Chloroethene has the formula CH₂=CHCl. Draw the repeating unit of poly(chloroethene).

Step 1 — Identify the C=C bond. Chloroethene has one C=C bond. Both carbons of that bond will end up in the repeating unit.

Step 2 — Open out the double bond. Replace the C=C with a single C–C bond. Each of those two carbons now has one "spare" bond that will connect outwards to the next monomer along the chain.

Step 3 — Keep all the other atoms exactly where they were. The left-hand carbon was CH₂ — it stays CH₂. The right-hand carbon was CHCl — it stays CHCl. Nothing is lost.

Step 4 — Add trailing bonds and brackets. Draw square brackets around the two-carbon unit. Extend a bond out of the left side of the bracket (off the left carbon) and out of the right side of the bracket (off the right carbon).

Step 5 — Add the subscript n. Write a small n just outside the bottom-right of the closing bracket to show that the unit repeats many times.

Final structure: –[CH₂–CHCl]– n (with the displayed-formula version showing all four bonds on each carbon if asked for a full displayed structure).

Examiner Report Insights

Use the citation format 1CH0/[paper][tier] — [Year] [Sitting] Examiner Report.

1CH0/2F — June 2019 Examiner Report: "The most commonly scored mark... was invariably for drawing the correct monomer for poly(chloroethene)." Foundation candidates were generally able to convert from the polymer name to the monomer with the C=C bond in place, which is encouraging — but the report flags two repeated errors:

  • "missing out the letter 'n' following the square brackets in the repeating unit for poly(ethene)" — the same trap applies to the "other" addition polymers in this spec point. If you draw a beautiful repeating unit but forget the n, you have not drawn a polymer.
  • "missing out the word 'tetra' from the name of the polymer." — Candidates wrote "polyfluoroethene" instead of poly(tetrafluoroethene). The "tetra" prefix is part of the systematic monomer name (four fluorines on the C=C) and must be preserved when the polymer is named.

Together these tell you exactly which two marks examiners reliably see lost on this spec point: the n on the repeating unit, and the "tetra" prefix in the PTFE name.

How Other Addition Polymers Appears in Exams

This spec point sits firmly on Paper 2 (Sections 6–9 + Section 1) and is Separate Chemistry only — it is not part of the Combined Science 1SC0 paper. Both Foundation and Higher candidates are eligible.

Paper 1CH0/2F (Foundation): Appears as a 2–3 mark, table-completion-style question that mixes drawing structures and naming polymers. Candidates fill in cells of a table that already gives some of the information (e.g. the polymer name) and must supply the missing monomer structure or repeating unit.

  • 1CH0/2F June 2019 Q3(a) (3 marks) — candidates completed a table requiring: the structure of poly(ethene), the structure of chloroethene (i.e. the monomer of PVC), and the name of poly(tetrafluoroethene). Command words: Draw, State, Name.

Paper 1CH0/2H (Higher): Same skill set as Foundation, but typically integrated into a longer organic-chemistry question that may also probe the uses of the polymer or compare its properties with another material. The drawing skill itself is identical — the carbon skeleton, side groups, trailing bonds and n are all examined.

*Typical question shapes for this SP:*

  • "Draw the repeating unit of poly(propene)" → 1–2 marks (1 for the two-carbon backbone with the correct side group; 1 for trailing bonds and n)
  • "State the monomer used to make PVC" → 1 mark (CH₂=CHCl or chloroethene, with the C=C bond shown)
  • "Name the polymer made from CF₂=CF₂" → 1 mark (poly(tetrafluoroethene) — both "tetra" and the brackets required)
  • "Complete the table" → 2–3 marks across rows giving combinations of monomer, repeating unit and polymer name

Command Words Used in This SP

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
DrawA displayed or partially displayed structure with bonds shown explicitly, including the C=C in the monomer or the trailing bonds and n in the repeating unit1–2
StateA short factual answer — a name, a formula, or a single structure with no explanation1
NameThe IUPAC-style name of the polymer, written as poly(monomer) with the brackets and any prefix (e.g. "tetra") preserved1

Mathematical Skills

Skill 5b — Translate information between diagrammatic and numerical forms. Although there are no arithmetic calculations attached to 9.19C, the spec point sits squarely on the maths skill of moving between 2D displayed structures and the corresponding monomer/repeating-unit notation. You must be able to:

  • Read a monomer drawn with a C=C bond and deduce the displayed structure of the repeating unit
  • Read a repeating unit drawn with trailing bonds and n and deduce the displayed structure of the monomer, putting the C=C bond back in
  • Convert between structural shorthand (e.g. CH₂=CHCl) and fully displayed structures showing every bond and atom

Procedure for translating a monomer to its repeating unit:

  1. Identify the C=C double bond in the monomer.
  2. Replace the C=C with a C–C single bond.
  3. Keep every other atom and bond exactly as it was.
  4. Draw two trailing bonds — one extending off each end carbon, through the square brackets.
  5. Add a small n outside the closing bracket.

Procedure for translating a repeating unit to its monomer:

  1. Identify the two-carbon backbone inside the brackets.
  2. Remove the trailing bonds and the n.
  3. Reinstate the C=C double bond between the two backbone carbons.
  4. Keep every side group (H, CH₃, Cl, F) exactly where it was.
Key Takeaways
  1. Addition polymers form when many monomers, each containing a C=C double bond, join end-to-end with no other product formed — all atoms in the monomers end up in the polymer.
  2. The three "other" addition polymers named in 9.19C are poly(propene), poly(chloroethene) (PVC) and poly(tetrafluoroethene) (PTFE), made from propene, chloroethene and tetrafluoroethene respectively.
  3. The monomer of poly(propene) is CH₂=CH–CH₃; its repeating unit is –[CH₂–CH(CH₃)]– n — a methyl group on every alternate carbon.
  4. The monomer of PVC is CH₂=CHCl (chloroethene); its repeating unit is –[CH₂–CHCl]– n — one chlorine on every alternate carbon.
  5. The monomer of PTFE is CF₂=CF₂ (tetrafluoroethene); its repeating unit is –[CF₂–CF₂]– n — all four hydrogens of ethene replaced by fluorine.
  6. The repeating unit is always two carbons wide because it comes from opening out the single C=C bond in the monomer.
  7. You must draw trailing bonds extending through the square brackets on both sides — they show where the unit links to the next one along the chain.
  8. The subscript n outside the brackets is what makes the structure a polymer; missing it is a frequently penalised error (June 2019 Foundation examiner report).
  9. Polymer names are written as poly(monomer) — the brackets and any prefix (e.g. "tetra" in poly(tetrafluoroethene)) are part of the name and must not be dropped.
  10. Conditions and mechanisms (high pressure, radicals, initiation steps) are explicitly not required by 9.19C — focus on structures and names only.
  11. This SP is Separate Chemistry only (1CH0) and appears on Paper 2 at both Foundation and Higher tier; on 2F it has appeared as a 3-mark table-completion question (June 2019 Q3(a)).
  12. Command words used here are Draw, State and Name — short factual marks awarded for accuracy of structure and naming, not for extended explanation.
9.3.2.3 Deducing Structures F+H ▼

Deducing Structures

Syllabus reference: 9.20C — Deduce the structure of a monomer from the structure of an addition polymer and vice versa. Foundation + Higher tier, Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only, Paper 2.

This spec point is purely a drawing skill. You will be given one structure (either the monomer or a section of the addition polymer) and asked to draw the other. The chemistry behind it is simple — addition polymerisation breaks one of the two bonds in the carbon–carbon double bond (C=C) of every monomer and uses those electrons to join thousands of monomers together into a long saturated chain — but the marks are won and lost on how you draw the answer, not on the underlying theory.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
MonomerA small molecule (containing a C=C double bond, for addition polymerisation) that can join with many identical molecules to form a polymer
PolymerA very long molecule built from many small repeating units joined end-to-end
Addition polymerA polymer formed when many monomers containing a C=C double bond join together with no other product (no atoms are lost)
Repeating unitThe short section of a polymer chain that is repeated many times along its length; shown inside square brackets with the subscript n
Continuation bondsThe two short lines extending out of the square brackets of a repeating unit, showing that the chain continues in both directions
nA subscript outside the bracket of a repeating unit indicating that the unit is repeated a large (but unspecified) number of times

How addition polymerisation changes the structure

The single rule you need to apply over and over again is this: in the monomer, two carbon atoms are joined by a double bond (C=C). In the polymer, the same two carbon atoms are joined by a single bond (C–C), and the two carbons now form part of the main chain that extends outwards on both sides.

Everything else stays the same:

  • The number of carbon atoms in the repeating unit equals the number of carbons in the monomer (for the monomers on this spec, that's always 2).
  • The side groups (H, Cl, F, CH₃, etc.) stay attached to exactly the same carbon they were on in the monomer.
  • Nothing is added and nothing is removed — the molecular formula of the repeating unit is identical to the molecular formula of the monomer.

That last point is what makes it an addition polymerisation: one molecule is formed from many, with no by-product like water or HCl.

*Why the C=C must be drawn as C=C in the monomer*

When deducing a monomer from a given polymer, the most common mistake is to draw the monomer with a single bond. If the monomer had a single bond, it could not polymerise — there would be no spare pair of electrons to share with the next monomer. You must always put a double bond between the two carbons that form the backbone of the repeating unit in the polymer.

*Why the polymer is drawn with brackets, continuation bonds and an "n"*

A real polymer chain contains thousands of repeat units. Drawing the whole chain is impossible, so the conventions are:

  1. Square brackets [ ] around the repeating unit — they mark out exactly which atoms make up the unit being repeated.
  2. Two short continuation bonds sticking out of the left and right sides of the brackets — these show that the chain extends in both directions.
  3. A subscript n just outside the right-hand bracket — this means "this unit is repeated a large number of times".

Omitting any of these three is a common reason marks are dropped.

Worked examples

*Example 1 — From monomer to polymer: ethene → poly(ethene)*

The monomer ethene is CH₂=CH₂. The C=C breaks open and the carbons become part of the main chain:

  • Monomer: CH₂=CH₂
  • Repeating unit: –[CH₂–CH₂]– with subscript n

Drawn fully out:

`` H H H H \ / | | C = C → —[ —C — C— ]— / \ | | n H H H H ``

Every carbon in the chain has two hydrogens attached. The bond between the two carbons inside the bracket is a single bond. The two bonds out of the bracket are the continuation bonds — they show the chain carries on either side.

*Example 2 — From monomer to polymer: chloroethene → poly(chloroethene)*

Chloroethene is CH₂=CHCl. One carbon has two hydrogens; the other has one hydrogen and one chlorine. Open the C=C, keep the substituents where they were:

  • Repeating unit: –[CH₂–CHCl]– with subscript n

`` H H H H \ / | | C = C → —[ —C — C— ]— / \ | | n H Cl H Cl ``

The Cl stays on the same carbon as in the monomer. The other carbon still has its two hydrogens.

*Example 3 — From monomer to polymer: tetrafluoroethene → poly(tetrafluoroethene)*

Tetrafluoroethene is CF₂=CF₂. Both carbons carry two fluorines.

  • Repeating unit: –[CF₂–CF₂]– with subscript n

`` F F F F \ / | | C = C → —[ —C — C— ]— / \ | | n F F F F ``

Note the polymer name: poly(tetrafluoroethene). The "tetra" is essential — without it the name implies only one fluorine.

*Example 4 — From polymer to monomer (reverse direction): given a repeating unit, draw the monomer*

The reverse procedure is just as important. To go from a repeating unit back to a monomer:

  1. Identify the two carbon atoms that form the backbone of the repeat unit (inside the brackets).
  2. Remove the brackets, the continuation bonds, and the subscript n.
  3. Change the single bond between those two carbons to a double bond.
  4. Keep every substituent (H, Cl, F, CH₃, …) attached to exactly the same carbon as before.

For example, if you are shown the polymer below:

`` CH₃ H | | —[ —C — C— ]— | | n H H ``

…the monomer must be CH₂=CHCH₃ — propene. The two carbons are joined by a C=C, one carbon has two hydrogens, and the other has one hydrogen plus one methyl group. The molecular formula is the same as the repeating unit (C₃H₆).

Mathematical Skills

Mathematical Skill 5b — Translate information between diagrammatic and numerical forms is required throughout this spec point. You are not doing numerical calculations, but you are translating between two diagrammatic representations (the 2D displayed structure of a monomer and the 2D displayed structure of a polymer repeating unit). The "numerical" aspects to watch for:

  • The number of atoms in the monomer must equal the number of atoms in the repeating unit (because no atoms are added or removed).
  • The number of carbons in the main chain of the repeating unit equals the number of carbons in the monomer (always 2 for the monomers on this spec).
  • The subscript n has no numerical value — it just signals "many" — but it must be present.

A quick atom-balance check is the fastest way to spot a mistake: count the H, C, Cl or F atoms on both sides of your translation and they must match exactly. If they don't, redraw.

Common drawing mistakes to avoid

These show up repeatedly in answers to monomer/polymer drawing questions:

  • Drawing a double bond inside the repeating unit. The repeating unit always has a single C–C bond between the two backbone carbons. The double bond only exists in the monomer.
  • Drawing a single bond in the monomer. The monomer must have a C=C double bond — without it, it cannot polymerise.
  • Omitting the continuation bonds. The two short lines extending out of the square brackets are required.
  • Omitting the subscript "n". "n" outside the bracket signals the unit is repeated many times. Without it, the diagram is just a small molecule, not a polymer.
  • Misplacing substituents. A side group (e.g. Cl, CH₃) must stay on the same carbon as it was in the monomer. Don't slide it onto the other carbon.
  • Missing hydrogens. Every carbon must show four bonds total. If a carbon has one Cl and one C–C, it must also have two H atoms drawn in.
  • Forgetting prefixes in polymer names — "tetra" in poly(tetrafluoroethene) being the obvious example.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — June 2019 Examiner Report: On drawing repeating units and monomers, "The most commonly scored mark... was invariably for drawing the correct monomer for poly(chloroethene)." The same report flagged two recurring error categories: "missing out the letter 'n' following the square brackets in the repeating unit for poly(ethene); missing out the word 'tetra' from the name of the polymer."

The take-aways for you are concrete:

  1. The monomer-from-polymer direction (e.g. drawing chloroethene given poly(chloroethene)) is achievable for most candidates if you remember to put a C=C in the monomer.
  2. The two named pitfalls are easy to lose marks on but equally easy to avoid: include the n after the bracket, and write tetrafluoroethene in full when naming the polymer.

How Deducing Structures Appears in Exams

This SP only appears on Paper 2 (Sections 6–9 are Paper 2 content; this is a Separate-Chemistry SP, so Combined Science candidates do not see it).

  • Position & type: Distributed throughout Paper 2. The dominant question type is a table that pairs polymer structures with monomer structures (or vice versa). One row gives you the structure, the next asks you to deduce and draw the partner; another row may give you a name and ask for the structure.
  • Command words: Draw, Deduce, State — see the table below.
  • Example — 1CH0/2F June 2019 Q3(a): A table containing the structure of poly(ethene), the structure of chloroethene, and the name "poly(tetrafluoroethene)" — candidates had to deduce the missing entries (3 marks).

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
DrawProduce a 2D displayed structure. Show every bond and every atom on each carbon. For the repeating unit: brackets, continuation bonds, and the subscript n. For a monomer: a clear C=C double bond.1 per correct structure
DeduceWork out the answer using information given (e.g. work out the monomer from the polymer's repeating unit). Usually combined with "Draw" in the same question.1 per correctly deduced structure
StateA short factual answer — for this SP, typically the name of the polymer or monomer (e.g. state the name of the polymer formed from chloroethene).1

Quick comparison: monomer vs repeating unit

FeatureMonomerRepeating unit of the polymer
Bond between backbone carbonsC=C double bondC–C single bond
Square brackets around the structure?NoYes
Continuation bonds out of the structure?NoYes (one on each side)
Subscript n?NoYes
Substituents (H, Cl, F, CH₃, …)On the same carbons as in the polymerOn the same carbons as in the monomer
Atom countSame number of atoms as one repeat unitSame number of atoms as the monomer

This table is the single most useful revision summary for this SP — if you can reproduce it from memory and apply each row when drawing, you will pick up the marks reliably.

Key Takeaways
  1. Addition polymerisation joins many small monomers (each with a C=C double bond) into one long chain with no other product formed.
  2. The single chemical change to remember: the monomer's C=C double bond becomes a C–C single bond in the repeating unit of the polymer.
  3. Side groups (H, Cl, F, CH₃) stay attached to the same carbons they were on in the monomer.
  4. The number and type of atoms in the monomer equals the number and type of atoms in the repeating unit — nothing is added, nothing is removed.
  5. A correctly drawn repeating unit needs three features: square brackets, continuation bonds on both sides, and a subscript n outside the bracket.
  6. A correctly drawn monomer needs a clear C=C double bond, with the right substituents on each carbon, and every carbon showing four bonds.
  7. To go monomer → polymer: open the double bond, draw the two carbons single-bonded inside square brackets, add continuation bonds and n.
  8. To go polymer → monomer: take one repeat unit, remove brackets/continuation bonds/n, change the single bond between the backbone carbons to a double bond, keep substituents in the same place.
  9. Be careful with polymer names — poly(tetrafluoroethene) needs the "tetra"; the 2019 Foundation Paper 2 examiner report flagged this as a common omission.
  10. The same examiner report flagged students missing out the subscript n after the brackets when drawing poly(ethene) — always check the n is there.
  11. A fast self-check before moving on: count every H, C, Cl, F on both sides of your translation; if the totals don't match, redraw.
  12. Questions on this SP are almost always presented as a table pairing polymer ↔ monomer structures or names; the command words to expect are Draw, Deduce, and State.
9.3.2.4 Uses of Polymers F+H ▼

Uses of Polymers

Spec point 9.21C: "Explain how the uses of polymers are related to their properties and vice versa: including poly(ethene), poly(propene), poly(chloroethene) (PVC) and poly(tetrafluoroethene) (PTFE)." Foundation + Higher tier · Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only · Paper 2.

This is a "two-way" spec point. You need to be able to do both of these, in either direction:

  • given a property (e.g. flexible and waterproof) → name a polymer and a suitable use, and
  • given a use (e.g. non-stick frying pan coating) → name the polymer and explain which property makes it suitable.

The four named polymers are all addition polymers made from alkene monomers. They share the general property of being unreactive (chemically inert) long-chain molecules, but they differ in flexibility, rigidity, melting point and chemical resistance, and those differences are what decide their uses.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
PolymerA very long-chain molecule built by joining many small monomer units together
Addition polymerA polymer formed when many alkene monomers join together by opening their C=C double bond, with no other product made
MonomerThe small molecule (an alkene for an addition polymer) that joins repeatedly to form a polymer
Repeat unitThe shortest section of a polymer chain that, repeated many times, gives the whole polymer
SaturatedContaining only single C–C bonds (no C=C); the long chain of an addition polymer is saturated
ThermosofteningDescribes a polymer that melts on heating because the weak forces between chains are easily overcome; can be re-shaped repeatedly
Chemically inertDoes not react readily with common reagents (such as acids, alkalis or bromine water); important for food packaging, pipework and lab/cookware uses

*Why polymer properties depend on structure*

The four polymers in this spec point are all long, mostly non-polar carbon-chain molecules. Within each chain the C–C and C–H (and C–Cl or C–F) bonds are strong covalent bonds, so the chains themselves are tough and do not break apart easily. Between the chains, however, the forces are much weaker (intermolecular forces). When chains can slide past each other easily, the polymer is flexible. When the side groups on the chain are larger, more polar, or more able to pack neatly, the chains slide less easily and the polymer becomes stronger, more rigid, and higher melting.

That single idea — strong bonds along the chain, weaker forces between chains, side groups change the packing — explains every property/use pair in the table below.

*Why addition polymers are chemically inert*

The monomer (e.g. ethene) has a reactive C=C double bond and will decolourise bromine water. Once the monomers have joined into a polymer, the double bonds are gone — every C–C bond in the chain is a single bond. The chain is now saturated, so it does not react with bromine water or with most acids and alkalis at room temperature. This inertness is why polymers can safely hold food, water and household chemicals; it is also the reason recycling and disposal are difficult (see 9.3.4.1 Problems with Polymers).

A common misconception that examiners flag is that students predict poly(ethene) will decolourise bromine water "because it came from ethene" — see the Examiner Report Insight below.

Property–Use comparison table for the four named polymers

This is the central table for the spec point. You should be able to reproduce something close to this for any of the four polymers under exam pressure.

PolymerMonomerKey propertiesTypical usesWhy those properties match the use
Poly(ethene) (polythene)Ethene, CH₂=CH₂Flexible; tough; lightweight; waterproof; chemically inert; low cost; easily moulded; thermosofteningPlastic bags; cling film; squeezy detergent bottles; plastic buckets and bowls; water/drink bottlesFlexibility lets a bag stretch without tearing; chemical inertness lets bottles hold detergents and food-safe liquids; waterproof + light + cheap = ideal mass-market packaging
Poly(propene)Propene, CH₂=CHCH₃Tougher and stronger than poly(ethene); higher softening temperature; flexible but rigid enough to keep its shape; resists fatigue when bent repeatedlyCrates and boxes; ropes; carpets; food containers; reusable plastic furniture; hinges in plastic boxes ("living hinges")Higher strength + rigidity holds the shape of a crate or chair; fatigue resistance lets a plastic hinge bend thousands of times without snapping; safe with food because it is inert
Poly(chloroethene), PVC (poly(vinyl chloride))Chloroethene, CH₂=CHClRigid (in unplasticised form); strong; durable; electrically insulating; waterproof; resistant to weathering and many chemicals; can be made flexible by adding plasticisersWindow frames; drainpipes and guttering; water and waste pipes; electrical cable insulation; flooring; (with plasticiser) flexible hoses and clothingRigidity + weather resistance suits outdoor window frames and pipes; electrical insulation + flexibility (plasticised) suits cable sheathing; chemical resistance suits drainage and waste pipes
Poly(tetrafluoroethene), PTFE (Teflon)Tetrafluoroethene, CF₂=CF₂Very low friction (non-stick); very high chemical resistance; high melting point/temperature resistance; tough; electrically insulatingNon-stick coating on frying pans and cookware; coatings on stain-resistant fabrics; bearings; gaskets and seals in chemical plants; insulation on wires used at high temperatureLow friction = food/liquids slide off and do not stick; chemical resistance lets PTFE handle hot oils, acids and alkalis in lab apparatus; high temperature tolerance suits cookware and industrial use

A useful exam tactic: if a question gives you a use, ask yourself which property is doing the work? ("Why is poly(propene) and not poly(ethene) used for crates?" → because poly(propene) is stronger/more rigid and keeps its shape under load.)

Worked example — reasoning from property to polymer

> "State and explain which of the four polymers above would be most suitable for the inside coating of a saucepan." (3 marks)

Step 1 — Identify the key requirements of the use. A saucepan coating must (a) survive hot temperatures, (b) not react with food or oils, (c) let food slide off easily so it does not stick or burn on.

Step 2 — Match requirements to a property profile. You need a polymer with high temperature resistance, chemical inertness (even with hot fats and acidic foods like tomato sauce), and low friction / non-stick behaviour.

Step 3 — Pick the polymer and justify. Poly(tetrafluoroethene) (PTFE) is the best match. It has a high softening temperature, it is extremely chemically inert (the strong C–F bonds make it resist almost all common reagents), and its surface is famously low-friction so food does not stick.

A 3-mark answer would say:

  1. PTFE is suitable;
  2. because it has a high melting/softening point so it survives cooking temperatures and is chemically inert so it does not react with food;
  3. and it has a low-friction (non-stick) surface so food does not stick to the pan.

Worked example — reasoning from polymer to use

> "Poly(propene) is used to make crates that stack on top of each other. Suggest two properties of poly(propene) that make it suitable for this use." (2 marks)

Step 1 — Picture the demands on the crate. A stacked crate must hold its shape under the weight of crates above it, must not crack when knocked or dropped, and is often used outdoors or in food contexts.

Step 2 — Match each demand to a property.

  • "Holds its shape under load" → rigid / strong.
  • "Does not crack when knocked" → tough.

Step 3 — Two-mark answer. Poly(propene) is strong/rigid (so the crate keeps its shape when other crates are stacked on top) and tough (so the crate does not crack or shatter when knocked).

You could equally well credit "chemically inert/safe with food" or "lightweight" if the question framed it as a food crate.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2H — 2019 June Examiner Report: "A common misconception was that poly(ethene) had many double bonds and therefore the reaction with bromine water would occur much quicker with the poly(ethene) than with the ethene." The correct response was that there would be no colour change — the bromine water would remain orange because poly(ethene) is saturated (the C=C bonds of the monomer are used up when the polymer forms). This matters for the uses spec point too: poly(ethene)'s chemical inertness — the very property that lets it package food and household chemicals — exists because the chain is saturated. If you tell an examiner the polymer "still has double bonds", you will lose marks both on the bromine water question and on any "why is it used for…" explanation.

How Uses of Polymers Appears in Exams

Per the current NotebookLM data for this spec point, the SP appears throughout Paper 2 of the Separate Chemistry route (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H). Two question shapes recur:

  • Short property-to-use or use-to-property items — typically 1–2 marks. "State a use of PTFE and give one property that makes it suitable" / "Suggest why poly(propene) and not poly(ethene) is used for…".
  • Comparison-table style items — a partially filled table covering two or three of poly(ethene), poly(propene), PVC and PTFE, with students asked to complete missing properties or uses. The Examiner Reports flag these as appearing alongside other polymer content.

The command words tied to this SP in the current notebook are "Explain", "State" and "Suggest". There is no specific mark tariff or candidate-error statistic in the current notebook beyond the 2019 bromine-water misconception above.

No direct mathematical-skills item was quoted for the uses themselves; the SP is conceptual rather than calculative. The mathematical skill that can attach to it is 5b — translate information between diagrammatic and numerical forms, which applies when a question pairs a polymer's repeat-unit structure to a property or use (for example, deducing from the F atoms on a drawn PTFE repeat unit that the polymer will be very unreactive).

Command Words table

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateGive a property and/or a use as a short factual answer; no reasoning needed1
SuggestApply your knowledge to an unfamiliar context (e.g. a new product) — pick a polymer and link a property to the requirement1–2
ExplainMake a property → use link explicit. "Polymer X is used for Y because it has property Z so that it can…" The "because/so that" wording is what earns the explanation mark2–3

Quick decision-flow for the four named polymers

If you need a fast way to pick the right polymer in the exam:

  1. Does the use need flexibility and low cost (bags, cling film, squeezy bottles)? → poly(ethene).
  2. Does the use need strength/rigidity that holds shape under load (crates, ropes, hinges, carpets)? → poly(propene).
  3. Does the use need rigid, weather-resistant or electrically-insulating plastic (window frames, drainpipes, cable insulation)? → PVC.
  4. Does the use need extreme heat resistance, very low friction, or extreme chemical inertness (non-stick pans, lab seals, stain-resistant fabrics)? → PTFE.

Then justify with one or two of the matching properties from the comparison table.

Key Takeaways
  1. The four named polymers in 9.21C are poly(ethene), poly(propene), poly(chloroethene) (PVC) and poly(tetrafluoroethene) (PTFE) — all addition polymers made from alkene monomers.
  2. Polymers are useful because they combine strong covalent bonds along the chain (toughness) with weaker forces between chains (which control flexibility and softening); side groups on the chain change how chains pack and therefore how the polymer behaves.
  3. Poly(ethene) is flexible, tough, lightweight and chemically inert — used for plastic bags, cling film and squeezy bottles.
  4. Poly(propene) is stronger and more rigid than poly(ethene), with higher softening temperature and good fatigue resistance — used for crates, ropes, carpets and food containers.
  5. PVC is rigid, durable, weather-resistant and an electrical insulator (and can be made flexible with plasticisers) — used for window frames, drainpipes, water/waste pipes and cable insulation.
  6. PTFE has a very low-friction surface, very high chemical resistance and a high softening temperature — used for non-stick coatings on cookware, stain-resistant fabrics, bearings and gaskets in chemical plants.
  7. The question can be set in either direction: property → use, or use → property. Your answer must explicitly link the property to the use ("because… so that…").
  8. Polymers are chemically inert because their chains are saturated — the C=C double bonds of the monomers are used up during polymerisation, so polymers do not decolourise bromine water and do not react readily with acids or alkalis at room temperature.
  9. Examiners (1CH0/2H — June 2019) reported a common misconception that poly(ethene) "still contains many double bonds" and would react with bromine water faster than ethene — the correct answer is no colour change, because poly(ethene) is saturated.
  10. The command words used for this SP in current Edexcel papers are State, Suggest and Explain; "Explain" requires the property-to-use link to be made explicit.
  11. The same inertness that makes these polymers useful is what makes them hard to dispose of and recycle — link forward to 9.3.4.1 Problems with Polymers and 9.3.4.2 Recycling Polymers.
  12. Quick rule of thumb: flexible/cheap = poly(ethene); strong/rigid = poly(propene); rigid + weather/electrical = PVC; non-stick + heat/chemical resistant = PTFE.
9.3.3.1 Condensation Polymers H only ▼

Condensation Polymers

Spec point 9.22C: Explain why polyesters are condensation polymers, how a polyester is formed from a dicarboxylic acid and a diol, and how a molecule of water is formed each time an ester link is formed. **Higher tier only** — Separate Chemistry Paper 2 (1CH0/2H).

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
PolymerA very long molecule built from many small repeating units (monomers) joined together
MonomerA small molecule that joins to other small molecules to form a polymer
Condensation polymerisationA polymerisation reaction in which monomers join together and a small molecule (most commonly water) is released each time a new bond is formed
PolyesterA condensation polymer whose repeat unit is joined by ester linkages (–COO–)
Ester link / ester linkageThe –COO– group formed when a carboxylic acid (–COOH) reacts with an alcohol (–OH), releasing water
Dicarboxylic acidA monomer with two –COOH groups, one at each end of the molecule
DiolA monomer with two –OH (alcohol) groups, one at each end of the molecule
By-productA small molecule released alongside the main product; in condensation polymerisation this is water

Why polyesters are condensation polymers

A polymer is classified by how its monomers join, not by what it is used for. In addition polymerisation (the route used to make poly(ethene), see 9.3.2.1 Formation of Polythene), the C=C double bond in an alkene monomer opens up and the carbon atoms link directly to neighbouring monomers. Nothing is lost — every atom in the monomer ends up in the polymer.

Condensation polymerisation works in a fundamentally different way. The monomers each carry two reactive functional groups, one at each end. When two monomers join, the functional groups react together to form a new covalent linkage, but in doing so a small molecule (here, water) is squeezed out. Because each new linkage in the chain releases a small molecule, the process is called condensation — the same idea as condensing two molecules into one by losing water.

Polyesters fit this definition exactly. A dicarboxylic acid monomer (HOOC–R–COOH) reacts with a diol monomer (HO–R′–OH), and each time the –COOH of one monomer reacts with the –OH of another, a new ester linkage (–COO–) is formed and a molecule of H₂O is released. The polymer chain grows at both ends because every monomer has two reactive groups, so the chain can extend in both directions indefinitely. Because an ester linkage is the repeating bond and water is lost each time it forms, a polyester is a textbook condensation polymer.

How a polyester forms — step by step

To produce a polyester you need two different bifunctional monomers:

  1. A dicarboxylic acid, written generally as HOOC–R–COOH, where R is a hydrocarbon spacer (for example –(CH₂)₄– or a benzene ring)
  2. A diol, written generally as HO–R′–OH, where R′ is again a hydrocarbon spacer

When a –COOH group meets a –OH group, the –OH of the carboxylic acid and the H of the alcohol's –OH group combine. These three atoms leave the system as a molecule of H₂O, and the remaining –C(=O)–O– link bridges the two monomer fragments. This new bond is the ester linkage (–COO–).

Because each monomer has two reactive ends, the new "two-monomer" molecule still has one –COOH on one side and one –OH on the other. It can react again with a fresh monomer, then again, and again. The chain therefore grows from both ends:

`` HO–R′–OH + HOOC–R–COOH + HO–R′–OH + HOOC–R–COOH → …–O–R′–O–CO–R–CO–O–R′–O–CO–R–CO–… + many H₂O ``

The polymer's repeat unit for a generic polyester is:

`` —[ O–R′–O–CO–R–CO ]—n ``

That single repeat unit contains two ester linkages, one at each end of the unit — which is why two water molecules are released for every full repeat unit added to the chain.

Ester linkage formation — diagram described in text

A textbook diagram for this reaction shows two monomers facing each other. On the left, draw the diol with its right-hand –OH group exposed:

`` HO–R′–O—H ``

On the right, draw the dicarboxylic acid with its left-hand –COOH group facing the diol:

`` H—O–CO–R–COOH ``

Circle the –OH from the carboxylic acid and the H from the alcohol's –OH (the highlighted atoms in the centre of the diagram — H from one side, OH from the other). These three atoms — one H from the alcohol side, one O and one H from the acid side — combine and leave as a single molecule of H₂O. The remaining fragments join through the carbon and the remaining oxygen to give the ester linkage:

`` HO–R′–O–CO–R–COOH + H₂O ``

Notice that the dicarboxylic acid still has a –COOH group on its right-hand side, and the diol still has a free –OH on its left-hand side. Both ends remain reactive. Add another diol on the right, and another acid on the left, and the chain doubles in length while two more water molecules are released. This is exactly the process that should be shown clearly in a diagram answer: monomers drawn end-to-end with the H and OH atoms picked out and the resulting –COO– linkage labelled.

Water as the by-product — why this matters

For every ester link that forms, exactly one molecule of water is produced. This is the defining feature of a condensation polymerisation: monomers join, but the product mass is smaller than the sum of the monomer masses because the water is removed.

  • If n dicarboxylic acid monomers react with n diol monomers to give a single polymer chain, the chain contains (2n – 1) ester links, and (2n – 1) molecules of water are released. (The –1 is because the two end groups of the finished chain — one –OH on one end and one –COOH on the other — have not reacted.)
  • For a very long chain (large n), this is effectively 2n water molecules per n repeat units, so on average two waters are released per repeat unit (matching the two ester linkages in the repeat unit shown earlier).

Because water is squeezed out at every step, condensation polymerisation is normally driven by heating and by removing water from the reaction mixture to push the equilibrium towards more polymer. The water by-product also explains the name condensation: each step "condenses" two molecules into one by expelling a small molecule.

Addition vs condensation polymerisation — comparison table

This is the most commonly tested comparison on Paper 2 Higher, so make sure you can pull each row from memory.

FeatureAddition polymerisationCondensation polymerisation
Monomer typeOne type of monomer, containing a C=C double bond (e.g. ethene)Two different monomers, each with two reactive functional groups (e.g. dicarboxylic acid + diol)
Functional groups requiredC=C alkene group–COOH and –OH (for polyesters)
Number of productsOnly one product — the polymerTwo products — the polymer and a small molecule (water)
Small molecule released?No — every atom from the monomer ends up in the polymerYes — one molecule of water per linkage formed
Atom economy100% atom economyLess than 100% — some mass leaves as water
Linkage in chainC–C single bonds along the backboneEster linkages –COO– (for polyesters)
Type of bond opened/formedC=C breaks, new C–C single bonds form–OH and –COOH react; –COO– forms
ExamplePoly(ethene) from etheneA polyester from a dicarboxylic acid + a diol
Tier examined onFoundation + HigherHigher only

Worked example — drawing the polyester repeat unit (Higher)

Question: A dicarboxylic acid HOOC–(CH₂)₄–COOH reacts with the diol HO–(CH₂)₂–OH to form a polyester. Draw the repeat unit and state how many molecules of water are released for each repeat unit.

Step 1 — identify the reactive groups. The acid has two –COOH groups, one on each end. The diol has two –OH groups, one on each end. Both monomers are bifunctional, so condensation polymerisation can occur.

Step 2 — form the first ester link. The –OH from one –COOH group and the H from one –OH of the diol leave as H₂O. The acid's carbonyl carbon now bonds to the diol's oxygen, giving the –COO– linkage.

Step 3 — extend the chain to one full repeat unit. A repeat unit contains one piece of diol and one piece of acid joined together. The repeat unit is:

`` —[ O–(CH₂)₂–O–CO–(CH₂)₄–CO ]—n ``

Step 4 — count the water molecules. The repeat unit contains two ester linkages (one at each end of the bracket), so two molecules of H₂O are released per repeat unit added to the chain. Always check this in an answer by counting the –COO– groups inside your drawn repeat unit.

*Common mistakes to avoid:*

  • Drawing the repeat unit with the ester link written as –CO–O– on one side but –O–CO– on the other; both are acceptable as long as the C=O carbonyl is attached to the carbon that originally came from the acid.
  • Forgetting the brackets and the "n" — without these the structure shows only one repeat, not a polymer.
  • Drawing the monomers separately and labelling that as "polyester" — you must show them joined and the water removed.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2H — November 2021 Examiner Report: "Polymer formation from monomers is generally well understood, although some candidates lack the appropriate literacy skills in explaining the process. Better candidates produced good diagrams… Condensation polymerisation and DNA were well known by candidates." The takeaway is that the chemistry itself is usually known, but clear written explanation and clear diagrams are where marks are gained or lost. If you are asked to explain how a polyester forms, name the functional groups explicitly (–COOH and –OH), name the bond formed (ester linkage / –COO–), and state that water is lost each time a linkage is made.
  • 1CH0/2H — June 2024 Q10(c): "Describe the differences between the type of monomer molecules used to form addition polymers and the type of monomer molecules used to form condensation polymers" (3 marks). This is the canonical comparison-style question. Three marking points typically map to: (i) addition uses one monomer / condensation uses two different monomers, (ii) addition monomers contain a C=C double bond / condensation monomers contain two functional groups (e.g. –COOH and –OH), (iii) addition forms only the polymer / condensation also releases a small molecule (water).

Mathematical Skills

Mathematical skill 5b — translate information between diagrammatic and numerical forms — applies to spec point 9.22C. In practice this means being able to:

  1. Look at structural formulae of two monomers and draw the repeat unit of the polymer they form
  2. Count how many water molecules are released given a stated number of monomer pairs joined
  3. Convert between a drawn repeat unit and a worded description of what has happened ("each ester linkage forms with the loss of one water molecule")

The underlying formula for water released when n acid monomers fully react with n diol monomers in a single chain is:

`` Number of H₂O molecules = 2n − 1 ``

For large n this is effectively 2n, i.e. two water molecules per repeat unit of the polyester chain.

How Condensation Polymers Appears in Exams

  • Paper: Higher tier Paper 2 (1CH0/2H) only. This spec point is not examined on Foundation tier and not examined on Combined Science (1SC0). It is a Separate Chemistry, Higher-only spec point.
  • Question style: Most often appears as a comparison/contrast question pairing condensation polymerisation with addition polymerisation, or as an extended diagram-drawing item where you must show the monomers, the repeat unit and the loss of water.
  • Typical command words: Explain, Describe, Draw.
  • Mark tariff: Comparison-style describe questions are typically 3 marks — see the June 2024 example below.
  • Specific sitting cited in NLM:

- 1CH0/2H — June 2024, Q10(c): "Describe the differences between the type of monomer molecules used to form addition polymers and the type of monomer molecules used to form condensation polymers" — 3 marks.

Command Words table

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
ExplainReasoning that links cause to effect — e.g. why a polyester counts as a condensation polymer2–3
DescribeA short prose answer covering the key differences or steps — e.g. describe the differences between addition and condensation polymerisation3
DrawA clearly labelled diagram of monomers, repeat unit, ester linkage and (where asked) the water by-product2–3
Key Takeaways
  1. Condensation polymerisation joins monomers together while releasing a small molecule (water) each time a new linkage is formed.
  2. A polyester is a condensation polymer joined by ester linkages (–COO–).
  3. Polyesters are made from two different bifunctional monomers: a dicarboxylic acid (HOOC–R–COOH) and a diol (HO–R′–OH).
  4. The reactive groups are the –COOH group of the carboxylic acid and the –OH group of the alcohol; they react together to form the ester linkage.
  5. Every time an ester linkage forms, one molecule of water (H₂O) is released — the –OH from the acid and the H from the alcohol's –OH combine to leave as water.
  6. The polymer repeat unit contains two ester linkages, so on average two water molecules are released per repeat unit of polyester formed.
  7. In an exam diagram, show the monomers end-to-end, circle the atoms lost as water, and draw the –COO– linkage clearly between the remaining fragments.
  8. Addition vs condensation — addition uses one monomer with a C=C bond and has 100% atom economy; condensation uses two different monomers with two functional groups each and releases water.
  9. Condensation polymers and addition polymers can be distinguished by checking whether a small molecule by-product is released — if yes, it is condensation.
  10. The chain keeps growing because every monomer has two reactive ends, so the polymer can extend in both directions.
  11. This spec point is Higher tier only and only examined on Paper 2 (1CH0/2H) of Separate Chemistry; it does not appear in Combined Science.
  12. The most common exam question is a 3-mark describe-the-differences item comparing addition and condensation polymerisation — practise three distinct comparison points to secure full marks.
9.3.4.1 Problems with Polymers F+H ▼

Problems with Polymers

Syllabus point 9.23C: Describe some problems associated with polymers including the availability of starting materials, persistence in landfill sites, gases produced during combustion, and the requirement to sort polymers so they can be melted and reformed.

Polymers — particularly addition polymers like poly(ethene), poly(propene), poly(chloroethene) (PVC) and polystyrene — are everywhere in modern life. They are cheap, durable, light, waterproof and easy to mould. Those same properties, however, are precisely what makes them difficult to deal with at the end of their useful life. This spec point breaks the problem down into four distinct issues. You are expected to be able to describe each one in enough detail to score the mark — and as the examiner reports below make clear, vague answers do not score.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
PolymerA very long molecule built from many small repeating units (monomers) joined together
MonomerA small molecule that reacts with other monomers to form a polymer
Non-biodegradableA material that cannot be broken down by microorganisms (bacteria and fungi) in the environment
BiodegradableA material that can be broken down by microorganisms into simpler, harmless substances
LandfillA site where waste is buried in the ground
CombustionThe reaction of a substance with oxygen, releasing energy (burning)
Crude oilA finite, non-renewable mixture of hydrocarbons formed over millions of years; the source of monomers for most common polymers
Thermosoftening polymerA polymer that softens and melts when heated, so can be reshaped repeatedly (this is the type that can be recycled by melting and reforming)

The four problems — overview

The spec lists four distinct issues; you should be able to discuss each one separately and link it to specific consequences. The table below summarises them; the paragraphs that follow expand on each.

Problem (spec sub-point)Core issueWhy it matters
(a) Availability of starting materialsMonomers come from crude oil, a finite resourceSupply is limited and will eventually run out
(b) Non-biodegradability in landfillMicroorganisms cannot break down most polymersPlastic waste persists in landfill for hundreds of years
(c) Gases produced during combustionBurning polymers releases toxic and acidic gasesHealth risks and contribution to acid rain and climate change
(d) Sorting requirement before melting/reformingDifferent polymers must be separated to be recycledSorting is time-consuming and expensive

(a) Availability of starting materials

The monomers used to make most common polymers — ethene, propene, chloroethene, styrene — are obtained from crude oil. Crude oil is a finite, non-renewable resource: it formed over hundreds of millions of years from the remains of marine organisms, and it cannot be replaced on any human timescale. Every barrel of crude oil that is cracked to produce ethene for poly(ethene) is a barrel that will not be available again.

There are two layers to this problem you should be able to explain:

  1. Crude oil itself is finite. Once it is used up, it is gone. As global demand grows, prices rise and supply becomes less secure.
  2. Crude oil has competing uses. The same oil that supplies polymer monomers is also used to make petrol, diesel, kerosene, lubricants and many other essential products. Using crude oil for disposable plastic packaging means using less of it for fuels, medicines and other higher-value applications.

A good exam answer here links the polymer back to its source — for example, "Polymers like poly(ethene) are made from monomers obtained from crude oil, which is a finite/non-renewable resource that will eventually run out". A weak answer that just says "the materials will run out" without naming crude oil or explaining why it is finite will usually not gain full credit (see Examiner Report Insights below).

(b) Persistence in landfill sites — non-biodegradability

Most common addition polymers are made entirely of strong covalent C–C and C–H bonds. The long chains are unreactive, and microorganisms in the soil cannot break them down. As a result, when polymer waste is buried in landfill it does not rot, decay or decompose like food waste, paper or wood. It persists — often for hundreds of years — taking up valuable land space and slowly accumulating with every new piece of waste added.

The chemistry behind this:

  • Natural materials like cellulose (in paper and wood) have many –OH and ester-type linkages that enzymes from bacteria and fungi can attack
  • Synthetic addition polymers have only C–C and C–H bonds along the backbone, which are not recognised by these enzymes
  • Without enzymes to break the bonds, the polymer simply sits in the ground unchanged

The consequences of non-biodegradable polymers in landfill include:

  • Loss of land — landfill sites fill up faster, and new sites must be created, often on land that could be used for farming, housing or wildlife
  • Visual pollution — plastic litter is unsightly and persists wherever it is dropped
  • Wildlife harm — animals can become entangled in plastic waste or ingest fragments, which they cannot digest
  • Microplastics — over time polymers break down physically (not chemically) into tiny fragments that contaminate soil and water

Again, an examiner-credit answer must qualify the issue — saying "plastics fill up landfill sites because they are non-biodegradable, so microorganisms cannot break them down" will score where "plastics cause pollution" will not.

(c) Gases produced during disposal by combustion

Burning waste polymers is an alternative to landfill — it reduces the volume of waste dramatically and can be used to generate electricity (energy-from-waste). But burning polymers produces a range of harmful gases, and this is one of the most commonly tested sub-points at GCSE.

Carbon dioxide is produced whenever any carbon-containing material is burned in plenty of oxygen:

polymer + O₂ → CO₂ + H₂O

Carbon dioxide is a greenhouse gas and contributes to global warming and climate change.

Carbon monoxide is produced when polymers are burned in a limited supply of oxygen — i.e. by incomplete combustion (see 8.1.4.2 Incomplete Combustion). CO is a colourless, odourless, toxic gas that binds to haemoglobin in the blood and prevents oxygen transport, which can be fatal.

Hydrogen chloride (HCl) is released when chlorine-containing polymers like poly(chloroethene) — PVC — are burned. The chlorine atoms in the polymer chain form HCl gas, which is acidic and contributes to acid rain. The reaction can be summarised as:

PVC + O₂ → CO₂ + H₂O + HCl

Soot (particulates) are tiny solid carbon particles produced by incomplete combustion. Soot causes respiratory problems, blackens buildings, and can carry toxic chemicals deep into the lungs.

Other toxic gases, depending on the polymer, can include nitrogen oxides (NOₓ) — from polymers containing nitrogen such as some nylons or polyurethanes — and various organic compounds. You do not need to know all of these by name at GCSE, but you should be aware that "toxic gases" and "acidic gases" are released, and you should be able to name carbon dioxide, carbon monoxide, and hydrogen chloride (from PVC) as the headline examples.

The vague-vs-specific contrast on this sub-point is critical (see Examiner Report Insights). Compare:

  • 0-mark vague answer: "Burning plastic releases harmful chemicals and pollutes the air."
  • 2-mark qualified answer: "Burning polymers releases carbon dioxide (a greenhouse gas contributing to climate change) and, from PVC, hydrogen chloride which is acidic and forms acid rain. Incomplete combustion also produces toxic carbon monoxide."

The second answer names specific gases and links each to a specific consequence. That is what examiners are looking for.

(d) Requirement to sort polymers before recycling

The most practical alternative to landfill or combustion is to recycle polymers — but only thermosoftening polymers can be recycled by melting and reforming. Even then, the process requires that polymers be sorted into types first.

Why sorting is needed:

  • Different polymers have different melting points — mixing them produces a poor-quality recycled material with unpredictable properties
  • Different polymers have different chemical structures and so different properties (rigidity, transparency, strength); mixing them weakens the recycled product
  • Some polymers (thermosetting polymers) cannot be melted at all, and would contaminate a melt of thermosoftening polymer

Sorting is difficult and expensive because:

  • It is mostly done by hand, which is labour-intensive
  • Polymers often look similar and cannot easily be told apart by eye
  • Recycling symbols (the small numbered triangles printed on packaging — 1 = PET, 2 = HDPE, etc.) help but are not always present or visible
  • The cost of sorting can make recycled polymer more expensive than producing fresh polymer from crude oil

This is why so much polymer waste still ends up in landfill or incineration even where recycling facilities exist. A good answer to this part of the spec should say not just "polymers need sorting" but why sorting is needed (different polymers have different properties / melting points and would not produce a usable mixture if melted together) and what makes sorting difficult (it is time-consuming and expensive).

Examiner Report Insights

The standout examiner-report insight for this spec point is the warning about vague, unqualified answers:

  • 1CH0/2F — 2019 June Examiner Report: "The main issue... was the lack of detail or vagueness in the candidates' responses, mainly in failing to qualify examples. Commonly seen responses which did not score included: causes pollution; harms the environment; releases harmful chemicals/toxins or causes landfill."

The same report emphasises that candidates needed to specifically mention issues such as releasing toxic gases during combustion or being non-biodegradable. In other words: name the gas, name the property, name the consequence. Generic environmental hand-waving — "it's bad for the planet", "it causes pollution", "it releases chemicals" — does not score.

What this means for your exam technique:

  • Always name a specific gas (CO₂, CO, HCl) rather than saying "harmful gases"
  • Always name the property (e.g. non-biodegradable) rather than saying "doesn't break down"
  • Always link the cause to a specific consequence (e.g. HCl → acid rain; CO → toxic / prevents oxygen transport; CO₂ → greenhouse gas / global warming)
  • Always identify the source (e.g. "made from crude oil, which is finite") rather than just "uses up resources"

How Problems with Polymers Appears in Exams

Based on the available notebook data:

  • Position & Type: Found throughout Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H). Disposal issues are highly tested as 2-mark "Describe" or "State" questions where vague answers fail.
  • Command Words: "Describe", "State", "Explain".
  • Tier: This SP is examined at both Foundation and Higher tier; the chemistry content does not change between tiers, but the depth of qualification expected in answers may increase at Higher.

For specific sitting-level appearances beyond the 2019 June Foundation paper noted above, no additional examiner-report data is available for this spec point in the current notebook.

Command Words table

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateA short factual answer; no explanation required, but still must be specific (e.g. "carbon monoxide", not "harmful gas")1
DescribeA short prose answer covering the key features — name the problem and qualify it (e.g. "polymers are non-biodegradable so microorganisms cannot break them down in landfill")2
ExplainReasoning that links the problem to its cause and consequence (e.g. "PVC contains chlorine, so when burned it releases HCl, which is acidic and causes acid rain")2–3

Why qualified examples matter — a worked answer comparison

Below is a worked exam-style comparison to make the examiner's point concrete. The question is the kind that recurs at Foundation tier on Paper 2:

> Describe two problems associated with the disposal of polymers. (2 marks)

*0-mark answer:*

"Polymers cause pollution and harm the environment because they release harmful chemicals."

Why this fails: every clause is vague. "Pollution" — what kind? "Harmful chemicals" — which ones? "Harm the environment" — how? None of these phrases is specific enough to be a "qualified example" in the examiner's language.

*2-mark answer:*

"Polymers are non-biodegradable so they cannot be broken down by microorganisms and persist in landfill for hundreds of years. When burned, they release toxic carbon monoxide and (from PVC) hydrogen chloride, which is acidic and contributes to acid rain."

Why this scores: two distinct problems are named (landfill persistence; combustion gases), each is qualified with a specific mechanism (non-biodegradable / microorganisms cannot break them down; specific named gases), and each is linked to a consequence (persists in landfill; acid rain).

Key Takeaways
  1. Polymers are made from monomers (e.g. ethene, propene, chloroethene) which come from crude oil — a finite, non-renewable resource that will eventually run out and has many competing uses.
  2. Most addition polymers are non-biodegradable: their C–C and C–H backbones cannot be broken down by microorganisms, so plastic waste persists in landfill for hundreds of years.
  3. Landfill space is finite, and non-biodegradable polymer waste accumulates rather than decomposing, taking up land and contributing to visual pollution and wildlife harm.
  4. Burning polymers releases carbon dioxide (a greenhouse gas contributing to climate change), carbon monoxide (a toxic gas from incomplete combustion), and soot/particulates.
  5. Burning PVC (poly(chloroethene)) releases hydrogen chloride (HCl), which is acidic and contributes to acid rain.
  6. Some polymers, when burned, release additional toxic gases (e.g. nitrogen oxides from nitrogen-containing polymers) — but the GCSE-named examples are CO₂, CO and HCl.
  7. Polymers can in principle be recycled by melting and reforming, but only thermosoftening polymers can be reshaped this way.
  8. Recycling requires polymers to be sorted by type first, because different polymers have different melting points and properties — mixing them would produce a low-quality recycled product.
