  9. Sorting is labour-intensive, time-consuming and expensive, which is why much polymer waste still ends up in landfill or is incinerated rather than recycled.
  10. In exam answers, always qualify your examples: name the specific gas (CO, HCl), name the property (non-biodegradable), and link each to a specific consequence (acid rain, toxic, greenhouse effect).
  11. Vague answers like "causes pollution", "harms the environment", "releases harmful chemicals" or "causes landfill" will not score (1CH0/2F — 2019 June examiner report).
  12. The spec sub-divides this point into four distinct issues — (a) starting materials, (b) landfill persistence, (c) combustion gases, (d) sorting — and you should be ready to address any of them by name in a "Describe" or "Explain" question.
9.3.4.2 Recycling Polymers F+H ▼

Recycling Polymers

Spec 9.24C: Evaluate the advantages and disadvantages of recycling polymers, including economic implications, availability of starting materials and environmental impact.

Foundation + Higher tier · Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only · Paper 2.

Recycling polymers is the process of collecting waste plastic, sorting it, cleaning it, and turning it back into useful material rather than sending it to landfill or incineration. The spec command word here is evaluate, which means you must put both sides of the argument (advantages and disadvantages) and then reach a justified overall conclusion. Vague claims like "it's good for the environment" will not score — examiners want specific, qualified statements grounded in economics, starting materials and environmental impact.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
PolymerA long-chain molecule made by joining many small monomer units together
RecyclingProcessing waste material so it can be reused, rather than sent to landfill or incinerated
Mechanical recyclingSorting, washing, shredding and melting waste polymer to remould it into new products of the same or lower grade
Chemical recyclingBreaking the polymer back down into its monomers (or other small molecules) so they can be re-polymerised into new plastic
Crude oilThe non-renewable fossil fuel that provides the hydrocarbon feedstock from which most addition polymers are made
LandfillA site where waste, including non-biodegradable plastic, is buried in the ground
ThermoplasticA polymer that softens and melts when heated, so it can be reshaped — most thermoplastics (PET, HDPE, LDPE, PP) can be mechanically recycled
Thermosetting polymerA polymer with cross-links between chains that does not melt on heating — these generally cannot be melted down and remoulded, so they are very hard to recycle
EvaluateWeigh up the advantages and disadvantages of something and reach a justified overall judgement

How Polymers Are Recycled in Practice

The typical recycling route for everyday plastics (drinks bottles, food trays, packaging) follows four steps:

  1. Collection — kerbside collection or bottle-bank schemes gather waste plastic from households and businesses.
  2. Sorting — different polymers must be separated from each other because they melt at different temperatures and cannot generally be mixed. Sorting is done partly by hand and partly by automated systems that detect the resin identification code (the "1" inside the triangle on PET, "2" for HDPE, and so on) or by infrared scanning.
  3. Cleaning and shredding — sorted plastic is washed to remove labels, food and dirt, then shredded into small flakes.
  4. Re-processing — flakes are melted and either extruded into pellets that are sold to manufacturers (mechanical recycling) or cracked back to monomers (chemical recycling) for re-polymerisation.

A good real-world example is PET (polyethylene terephthalate, used for drinks bottles). PET bottles can be mechanically recycled into new bottles, but the recycled polymer is often used to make polyester fibre for clothing and carpets, because each recycling cycle slightly degrades the polymer chains.

Why Sorting Is So Difficult

The biggest practical problem with recycling polymers is sorting. Many household products contain several polymers laminated or moulded together (e.g. a yoghurt pot with a film lid, or a juice carton with a polymer lining). If two polymers with different melting points are melted together, the result is a low-quality plastic that is brittle, cloudy and useless for high-grade applications. This is one of the main reasons why some plastics are sent to landfill or incineration even when they are technically recyclable — the cost of sorting them properly outweighs the value of the recovered material.

Advantages and Disadvantages — The Three Spec Themes

The spec point names three themes you must consider: economic implications, availability of starting materials, and environmental impact. Build your evaluation around these.

Economic Implications

AdvantagesDisadvantages
Creates jobs in collection, sorting and re-processing industriesSorting plastic into separate polymer types is labour-intensive and expensive
Recycled polymer pellets can be sold to manufacturers as a cheaper alternative to virgin polymerCollection schemes (kerbside, bottle banks, transport to facilities) cost councils and taxpayers money
Reduces the amount of waste councils must pay to send to landfill (landfill tax)When crude oil prices are low, virgin polymer is often cheaper than recycled polymer, making recycling economically unattractive
Reduces a country's reliance on imported crude oil for plastic manufactureCleaning contaminated plastic (food residue, labels, adhesives) adds further cost

Availability of Starting Materials

AdvantagesDisadvantages
Crude oil is a finite, non-renewable resource — recycling makes the existing supply last longerRecycled polymer is often lower quality than virgin polymer, so it cannot always replace it directly
Reduces the volume of crude oil that must be extracted, refined and cracked to make monomersSome polymers (especially thermosetting plastics) cannot be melted and remoulded at all
Each recycling cycle conserves both the hydrocarbon feedstock and the energy that would go into making virgin polymer from scratchPolymer chains shorten slightly during repeated melting, so plastic cannot be recycled indefinitely — eventually it is downcycled or discarded

Environmental Impact

AdvantagesDisadvantages
Reduces the mass of plastic sent to landfill, where most polymers are non-biodegradable and persist for hundreds of yearsCollection lorries, washing facilities and re-processing plants all use energy and produce CO₂
Reduces the amount of plastic incinerated, lowering emissions of CO₂ and (for some polymers) toxic gases such as HCl from PVCCleaning plastic uses large volumes of water, which then needs treating
Reduces plastic pollution in oceans and on land, protecting wildlife from ingestion and entanglementSorting and re-processing still require energy, even if less than making virgin polymer from crude oil
Conserves crude oil, a finite resource whose extraction (drilling, refining, cracking) causes its own pollutionIf recycled plastic is contaminated, batches may be rejected and end up in landfill anyway, wasting the energy already used

Mechanical vs Chemical Recycling

FeatureMechanical recyclingChemical recycling
What happensPolymer is melted and remouldedPolymer is broken back down into monomers or smaller molecules
Quality of productSlightly lower each cycle — chains shortenCan produce virgin-quality polymer, because monomers are re-polymerised fresh
Energy costLowerHigher — needs more heat and often catalysts
Suitable forClean, single-polymer thermoplastics (PET, HDPE)Mixed or contaminated polymer streams; thermosetting polymers in some cases
Common limitationSorting must be very accurateCurrently expensive and not yet widespread

For a Foundation-tier answer, knowing the basic difference (one melts and reshapes, the other breaks down into monomers) is enough. Higher-tier candidates can use the comparison to argue that chemical recycling addresses some of mechanical recycling's downsides (quality loss, contamination) but introduces a new disadvantage (higher energy cost).

Reaching a Justified Conclusion

Because the command word is evaluate, your final sentence or two must reach an overall judgement. There is no single "correct" conclusion — what scores is that you have weighed your points and given a reason. Two acceptable framings:

  • "Overall, recycling polymers is worthwhile because the environmental and resource benefits of conserving crude oil and reducing landfill outweigh the financial cost of sorting and cleaning."
  • "Overall, recycling is only worthwhile when polymers can be effectively sorted, because the energy and cost of sorting mixed or contaminated plastic can outweigh the benefit gained."

Either is creditworthy if backed up by the points you have already made. Avoid sitting on the fence ("there are good and bad points") — that does not answer "evaluate".

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — June 2019 Examiner Report: "The main issue... was the lack of detail or vagueness in the candidates' responses... Commonly seen responses which did not score included: causes pollution; harms the environment; releases harmful chemicals/toxins or causes landfill." This was reported for the broader problems-with-polymers context but applies directly to recycling evaluations: vague environmental claims will not score. You must qualify each point — for example "saves crude oil because it is a finite resource" or "reduces the mass of polymer sent to landfill, where it is non-biodegradable" — rather than writing "good for the environment".

How Recycling Polymers Appears in Exams

This SP is examined on Paper 2 of Separate Chemistry only (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H).

*Question style — "Evaluate"*

Based on the exam-appearance data in the current notebook, recycling polymers appears as a multi-mark question using the command word "Evaluate", requiring candidates to give both advantages and disadvantages plus a justified conclusion. Related command words used in this area include "Describe" (give the main steps of recycling or a stated problem) and "Explain" (give a reason linked to cause and effect, e.g. why a particular polymer is hard to recycle).

For an "Evaluate" question:

  • Plan three to four advantages and three to four disadvantages covering at least two of the three spec themes (economic, starting materials, environmental).
  • Use the wording of the spec themes explicitly — examiners reward students who say "this conserves crude oil, a finite starting material" rather than just "saves oil".
  • Always finish with a one-sentence overall judgement; otherwise you cap your mark.

*No further exam-appearance data (specific paper / sitting / Q-number citations) is available for this spec point in the current notebook.*

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Use for This SP
EvaluateWeigh up advantages and disadvantages and reach a justified judgementMulti-mark, full-question style for 9.24C
DescribeA short prose answer covering main points, no reasoning required"Describe how a polymer is recycled" — the four-step process
ExplainReasoning that links cause to effect"Explain why some polymers are difficult to recycle" — link sorting/thermosetting to outcome

Common Student Errors

  • Writing vague environmental claims ("recycling helps the environment", "plastic is bad") without qualifying them — this is exactly what the June 2019 examiner report criticised.
  • Forgetting to give disadvantages in an "evaluate" question — only listing the good points scores at most half marks.
  • Confusing biodegradable with recyclable. A polymer that is recyclable is one that can be re-processed; a biodegradable polymer is one that breaks down naturally — these are different properties.
  • Saying "recycling produces no CO₂" — collection lorries, washing, melting and re-extrusion all use energy and release CO₂. Recycling reduces emissions compared to making virgin polymer; it does not eliminate them.
  • Not finishing with a conclusion. The mark scheme for "evaluate" questions typically reserves a mark specifically for a justified overall judgement.
  • Suggesting all plastics can be recycled. Thermosetting polymers (e.g. Bakelite, melamine, cured epoxy) cannot generally be melted and remoulded, so mechanical recycling does not work for them.
Key Takeaways
  1. Recycling polymers means collecting, sorting, cleaning and re-processing waste plastic into new products instead of sending it to landfill or incineration.
  2. The spec command word for 9.24C is evaluate — you must give both advantages and disadvantages and reach a justified conclusion.
  3. The three spec themes you must cover are economic implications, availability of starting materials, and environmental impact.
  4. Crude oil is a finite, non-renewable resource — recycling conserves the hydrocarbon feedstock and extends its supply.
  5. Most polymers are non-biodegradable, so landfill plastic persists for hundreds of years — recycling reduces the mass going to landfill.
  6. Sorting is the biggest practical problem: different polymers melt at different temperatures and cannot be mixed without producing low-quality plastic.
  7. Thermosetting polymers cannot be melted and remoulded, so mechanical recycling does not work for them.
  8. Mechanical recycling (melt and remould) is cheaper but quality drops each cycle; chemical recycling (break back to monomers) gives higher-quality polymer but uses more energy.
  9. Recycling still has costs: collection, sorting, cleaning and re-processing all use energy and water and emit CO₂ — but usually less than making virgin polymer from crude oil.
  10. Vague answers do not score — the June 2019 examiner report criticised responses like "causes pollution" or "harms the environment". Always qualify your point (e.g. "reduces landfill mass, because polymers are non-biodegradable").
  11. For an "evaluate" answer, plan 3–4 advantages and 3–4 disadvantages across at least two of the three spec themes, then close with a one-sentence justified overall judgement.
  12. Recycling is not unlimited — polymer chains shorten slightly each time, so plastic is often downcycled (e.g. PET bottles into polyester fibre) before eventually being discarded.
9.4.1.1 Alcohols F+H ▼

Alcohols

Spec points 9.26C and 9.27C — recall the formulae of methanol, ethanol, propan-1-ol and butan-1-ol; draw their displayed structures showing all covalent bonds; recognise that the functional group in alcohols is –OH. Examined on both Foundation and Higher tier; Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only.

Alcohols are the second organic family that Edexcel asks you to know in detail. They follow the same rules you have already met for the alkanes — a homologous series with a single general formula and a single functional group — but with one extra atom that changes everything about how they behave. The spec is restrictive: you only need the first four straight-chain alcohols, and only the 1-ol isomers for propanol and butanol.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
AlcoholAn organic compound containing the hydroxyl functional group –OH attached to a carbon chain
Hydroxyl groupThe –OH functional group; one oxygen atom single-bonded to one hydrogen atom and single-bonded to a carbon atom
Functional groupA specific atom or group of atoms within a molecule that gives the molecule its characteristic chemical properties
Homologous seriesA family of organic compounds with the same functional group and general formula, differing by –CH₂– between consecutive members
General formulaA formula that represents every member of a homologous series; for alcohols this is CₙH₂ₙ₊₁OH (equivalent to CₙH₂ₙ₊₂O)
Displayed formulaA two-dimensional drawing of a molecule showing every atom and every covalent bond as a line
Molecular formulaA formula showing the number of each type of atom in one molecule, e.g. C₂H₆O for ethanol
Structural formulaA condensed representation showing the order in which atoms are joined, e.g. CH₃CH₂OH for ethanol

The first four alcohols

The spec restricts you to these four. Learn the name, the molecular formula, the structural formula, and — most importantly — how to draw the displayed formula showing every covalent bond.

AlcoholnMolecular formulaStructural formulaMr
Methanol1CH₄OCH₃OH32
Ethanol2C₂H₆OCH₃CH₂OH46
Propan-1-ol3C₃H₈OCH₃CH₂CH₂OH60
Butan-1-ol4C₄H₁₀OCH₃CH₂CH₂CH₂OH74

Each consecutive member adds one –CH₂– unit (mass difference of 14), which is the defining feature of any homologous series.

Why the formulae work

Every carbon atom in an alcohol forms four covalent bonds. Every oxygen atom forms two covalent bonds. Every hydrogen atom forms one covalent bond. If you draw the molecule and count bonds, those numbers must match — that is the underlying logic behind the general formula.

For an alcohol with n carbons:

  • n carbons need 4n bonding positions
  • n-1 C–C bonds account for 2(n-1) of those positions
  • The C–O bond accounts for one position on one carbon
  • That leaves 4n – 2(n-1) – 1 = 2n + 1 hydrogens attached to carbon
  • Plus 1 hydrogen attached to oxygen (the –OH)

So the formula is CₙH₂ₙ₊₁OH, which equals CₙH₂ₙ₊₂O if you collect all the hydrogens. Both forms are correct and the spec lists the first form.

The –OH functional group

The defining feature of an alcohol is the hydroxyl group, –OH. This is what makes an alcohol an alcohol — not the carbon chain length. The oxygen sits between the carbon and the hydrogen with two single covalent bonds: one C–O bond and one O–H bond. There is no double bond anywhere in an alcohol molecule; alcohols are saturated.

The hydroxyl group is also the reason alcohols:

  • have much higher boiling points than alkanes of similar mass (the O–H bond allows hydrogen bonding between molecules)
  • mix with water (the O–H group can hydrogen-bond to water molecules)
  • can be oxidised to carboxylic acids (the C–OH carbon is the reactive site)

Boiling point trend

As the carbon chain gets longer, the boiling point rises. Larger molecules have more electrons and more surface contact between neighbouring molecules, so the intermolecular forces are stronger and more energy is needed to separate them into a gas.

AlcoholMrBoiling point / °CState at room temp
Methanol3265liquid
Ethanol4678liquid
Propan-1-ol6097liquid
Butan-1-ol74117liquid

All four are liquids at room temperature. The rising boiling point with Mr is exactly the same trend you see in the alkanes, and the underlying reason is the same — but alcohols also have hydrogen bonding on top of the dispersion forces, which lifts the values significantly higher than the alkanes of comparable mass.

Why only 1-ol isomers?

The spec deliberately restricts you to propan-1-ol and butan-1-ol, not the 2-ol versions. The "1" in the name tells you the –OH is on carbon number 1 (the end of the chain). Propan-2-ol and butan-2-ol exist and are real chemicals, but they are not assessed at GCSE — you only need the straight-chain version with the –OH at the end.

When you draw the structure, the –OH must therefore appear on an end carbon, not a middle one.

Mathematical Skill 5b — Drawing Displayed Structures

Math Skill 5b is translate information between diagrammatic and numerical forms. For this spec point, that means converting a molecular formula or a name into a fully displayed structure with every covalent bond shown as a line.

Procedure

Use these steps every time you are asked to draw an alcohol:

  1. Read the name to identify how many carbons are in the chain (meth = 1, eth = 2, prop = 3, but = 4).
  2. Draw the carbon skeleton as a horizontal chain of C atoms joined by single lines (C–C bonds).
  3. Place the –OH group on carbon 1 (the end carbon) for all four spec alcohols.
  4. Draw the O–H bond as a single line between O and H.
  5. Add hydrogens to every remaining bonding position on each carbon. Each carbon must end up with exactly four lines coming out of it.
  6. Count: the total number of hydrogens should match 2n + 2 (e.g. 8 H for propanol — 7 on carbon, 1 on oxygen).
  7. Check: there are no double bonds, no extra atoms, and the –OH is written as O–H (two atoms, one bond between them) — not as a single "OH" cluster without the bond shown.

Worked example — drawing propan-1-ol

You are asked: "Draw the displayed formula of propan-1-ol, showing all covalent bonds."

Step 1: Three carbons (propan = 3). Draw three Cs joined by single bonds:

`` C — C — C ``

Step 2: Add the hydroxyl group to carbon 1. Show the O–H bond explicitly:

`` C — C — C — O — H ``

Step 3: Fill in the hydrogens. Carbon 1 has 1 bond used (to C2) and 1 bond used (to O), so it needs 2 H. Carbon 2 has 2 bonds used (to C1 and C3), so it needs 2 H. Carbon 3 has 1 bond used (to C2), so it needs 3 H.

`` H H H | | | H— C — C — C — O — H | | | H H H ``

Step 4: Check. Eight hydrogens total (7 on carbon + 1 on oxygen), three carbons, one oxygen — matches the molecular formula C₃H₈O. Every line represents one covalent bond. No double bonds. The –OH is shown as a separate O and H with a line between them.

This is the answer Edexcel expects when the command word is Draw.

Common Drawing Mistakes

These are the errors students make most often when drawing alcohol displayed formulae. They are general subject-knowledge pitfalls — not attributed to any specific examiner report.

  • Writing "OH" as a single cluster without the bond. The mark scheme expects the displayed formula, which means the O–H bond must be drawn as a line. Writing the hydroxyl as just "OH" lumped together usually loses the mark.
  • Forgetting the hydrogen on oxygen. The oxygen needs two bonds. One goes to the carbon; the other must go to a hydrogen. Drawing only C–O loses the mark.
  • Drawing a C=C double bond by accident. Alcohols are saturated. A double bond turns the molecule into something else entirely (an alkene or an aldehyde/ketone if the double bond is on the C–O). Always single bonds throughout.
  • Putting the –OH in the middle of the chain. For propanol and butanol the spec is explicit: only the 1-ol. The –OH belongs on an end carbon.
  • Miscounting hydrogens on a carbon. Every carbon must have exactly four lines coming out of it. Three or five is wrong. Methanol's central carbon, for example, has 3 H + 1 bond to O = 4 bonds.
  • Confusing molecular formula with displayed formula. The command Draw demands the picture with all bonds shown. Writing C₂H₆O does not answer a "draw the displayed formula" question.
  • Writing the molecular formula as CH₃OH and calling that a displayed formula. CH₃OH is a structural formula. It is not displayed unless every C–H, C–C, C–O and O–H bond is shown as a line.

How Alcohols Appear in Exams

Alcohols typically appear in the middle-to-late section of Paper 2 (1CH0/2F or 1CH0/2H). The recurring task is drawing the displayed structure of one of the first four alcohols, showing every covalent bond and the –OH group correctly. Recall questions on the functional group (–OH) and on the general formula also appear.

Specific citation

  • 1CH0/2H June 2024 Q10(b)(i) — "Give the name of the carbon-containing product formed when the alcohol in Figure 15 undergoes dehydration" (1 mark). This is the closest related question reference available for this SP; it is a naming task about a dehydration product rather than a drawing task, but it sits inside the alcohols sub-topic.

Command words you will meet

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
DrawA full displayed formula with every covalent bond shown as a line1–2
StateA short factual answer — usually the functional group, general formula, or name1
GiveA short factual answer — typically a name, formula, or product1

For Draw questions, the marking point is usually whether every bond is shown (including the O–H) and whether the structure is chemically correct (no double bonds, four bonds per carbon, –OH on the right carbon).

For State and Give questions on alcohols, common short answers include "–OH" (functional group), "CₙH₂ₙ₊₁OH" (general formula), and the names of the first four alcohols.

Key Takeaways
  1. Alcohols are a homologous series with the general formula CₙH₂ₙ₊₁OH (equivalent to CₙH₂ₙ₊₂O).
  2. The functional group in alcohols is –OH (the hydroxyl group); this is spec point 9.27C.
  3. The four alcohols you must know are methanol, ethanol, propan-1-ol and butan-1-ol — only the 1-ol isomers are assessed.
  4. Each consecutive alcohol differs from the previous one by –CH₂– (Mr difference of 14), which is the defining property of a homologous series.
  5. Every carbon makes 4 bonds, every oxygen makes 2 bonds, every hydrogen makes 1 bond — this rule lets you check any displayed structure.
  6. The –OH group must be drawn as a separate O and H connected by a single line, not as a single "OH" cluster — the displayed formula requires every bond to be shown.
  7. Alcohols are saturated: there are no double bonds anywhere in the molecule.
  8. All four spec alcohols are liquids at room temperature; boiling point increases with chain length (65 °C → 78 °C → 97 °C → 117 °C).
  9. The –OH on carbon 1 rule applies to propan-1-ol and butan-1-ol — placing it in the middle of the chain is a wrong-isomer error.
  10. Math Skill 5b — translating between names/formulae and displayed structures — is the core skill assessed for this spec point.
  11. On Paper 2, the recurring task is to Draw the displayed structure of one of the first four alcohols, showing every covalent bond.
  12. The closest related question reference available is 1CH0/2H June 2024 Q10(b)(i) — a 1-mark naming task about the carbon-containing product of dehydration.
9.4.2.1 Core Practical: Heat of Combustion of Alcohols F+H ▼

Core Practical: Heat of Combustion of Alcohols

Spec point 9.28C: Investigate the temperature rise produced in a known mass of water by the combustion of the alcohols ethanol, propanol, butanol and pentanol — Foundation + Higher, Paper 2, Separate Chemistry only.

This is a Core Practical, so the method, equipment, measurements, data handling and sources of error are all assessable. You will be expected to describe the apparatus, identify variables, process raw data into a meaningful result, plot or interpret graphs of the trend, and evaluate why the result is always lower than the true value.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
CombustionA reaction in which a substance burns in oxygen, releasing heat energy
Exothermic reactionA reaction that transfers energy from the chemicals to the surroundings, causing a temperature rise
Heat of combustionThe energy released when a substance burns completely in oxygen
Spirit burnerA small container holding a liquid fuel with a wick, used to burn alcohols in a controlled way
Temperature rise (ΔT)The difference between the final and initial temperature of the water, ΔT = T<sub>final</sub> − T<sub>initial</sub>
Mass lossThe mass of fuel burnt, calculated as m<sub>before</sub> − m<sub>after</sub> of the spirit burner and contents
CalorimeterThe vessel (usually a copper or glass beaker) holding the water that absorbs the heat from the burning fuel
Independent variableThe variable you change deliberately — here, the alcohol used (ethanol, propanol, butanol, pentanol)
Dependent variableThe variable you measure — here, the temperature rise of the water and/or the mass of fuel burnt
Control variableA variable kept constant so the comparison is fair — for example, the mass of water, the distance from burner to beaker, and the temperature rise reached

The Aim of the Practical

You are investigating how the energy released changes as you move along a homologous series — ethanol (2 carbons), propanol (3), butanol (4), pentanol (5). The general pattern is that the longer the carbon chain, the more energy released per mole, and the more energy released per gram, because more C–C and C–H bonds are broken and more C=O and O–H bonds are formed.

The practical is comparative — you do not need to calculate the actual heat of combustion in kJ/mol. You compare either:

  • The mass of alcohol needed to produce the same fixed temperature rise (e.g. 30 °C) in a fixed mass of water, or
  • The temperature rise produced when a fixed mass of each alcohol is burnt under the same conditions.

The first approach (constant ΔT, measure mass loss) is the version most often examined.

Equipment

  • Spirit burner containing the alcohol (one burner per alcohol)
  • Copper calorimeter or thin-walled metal beaker
  • Clamp and stand to hold the calorimeter above the burner
  • Heatproof mat
  • Thermometer (0–110 °C, or temperature probe)
  • Measuring cylinder (100 cm³)
  • Balance, reading to at least 0.01 g
  • Stirring rod
  • Lid for the calorimeter (with a hole for the thermometer) to reduce heat loss
  • Draught shield (cardboard or metal screen) to reduce heat loss to the surroundings

Method

  1. Use a measuring cylinder to add a fixed volume of water (typically 100 cm³, equivalent to 100 g) into the calorimeter.
  2. Record the initial temperature of the water with the thermometer (T<sub>initial</sub>).
  3. Place the spirit burner containing the first alcohol on the balance and record its mass (m<sub>before</sub>), with the cap removed.
  4. Position the spirit burner under the calorimeter so the wick is a fixed distance below the base of the calorimeter (e.g. 5 cm). Keep this distance the same for every alcohol — this is a control variable.
  5. Light the wick and stir the water gently with the stirring rod.
  6. When the water temperature has risen by exactly 30 °C (so T<sub>final</sub> = T<sub>initial</sub> + 30 °C), put the cap back on the burner to extinguish the flame immediately.
  7. Continue stirring and record the highest temperature reached (this allows for the slight rise after extinguishing).
  8. Reweigh the spirit burner with the cap on (m<sub>after</sub>).
  9. Calculate the mass of alcohol burnt: m<sub>burnt</sub> = m<sub>before</sub> − m<sub>after</sub>.
  10. Repeat steps 1–9 with fresh water for each of the other three alcohols (propanol, butanol, pentanol).
  11. Repeat the whole experiment to get concordant results and calculate a mean mass burnt for each alcohol.

Variables

Variable typeWhat it is in this practical
IndependentThe alcohol used (ethanol, propanol, butanol, pentanol)
DependentThe mass of alcohol burnt to achieve the 30 °C temperature rise
ControlMass/volume of water in the calorimeter; starting temperature of the water; distance from wick to calorimeter base; same calorimeter; same target temperature rise; same draught conditions

Safety and Hazards

  • Alcohols are highly flammable — keep stoppers on burners when not in use and keep them away from open flames when refilling.
  • Tie back long hair and remove loose clothing or scarves.
  • Use a heatproof mat under the apparatus.
  • Wear safety goggles throughout.
  • Cap the burner immediately to extinguish the flame — never blow it out, as this can spread vapour.
  • Allow the equipment to cool before handling — the calorimeter and clamp become very hot.
  • Methanol (sometimes included in extended versions of this practical) is toxic by inhalation and should be handled with extra care; the spec list above does not include it.

Worked Example Data Table

A typical results table for this practical is set out like this. Foundation candidates are often given a partially completed version; Higher candidates may be asked to design the table themselves.

AlcoholNumber of C atomsMass of burner before (g)Mass of burner after (g)Mass burnt (g)Initial water temp (°C)Final water temp (°C)Temperature rise (°C)
Ethanol2165.20163.102.1020.050.030.0
Propanol3158.40156.801.6020.050.030.0
Butanol4172.55171.251.3020.050.030.0
Pentanol5169.80168.701.1020.050.030.0

Worked Calculations

Mass burnt is calculated using a simple subtraction:

`` mass burnt = m_before − m_after ``

For ethanol: 165.20 − 163.10 = 2.10 g

Temperature rise is also a subtraction:

`` ΔT = T_final − T_initial ``

For ethanol: 50.0 − 20.0 = 30.0 °C

Interpreting the trend: the mass burnt decreases as the carbon chain gets longer. This means you need less of the longer-chain alcohol to produce the same temperature rise, so longer-chain alcohols release more energy per gram. The trend reflects the fact that each extra –CH₂– group adds more bonds whose combustion releases additional energy.

Plotting the Graph

A standard follow-up question is to plot mass burnt against number of carbon atoms in the alcohol:

  • x-axis: number of carbon atoms (2, 3, 4, 5) — the independent variable
  • y-axis: mass of alcohol burnt (g) — the dependent variable
  • Plot the four points and draw a smooth line or curve of best fit.
  • The graph should show a downward trend — as carbon number increases, mass burnt decreases.

You may also be asked to plot temperature rise (if the experiment used a fixed mass of alcohol instead) — in that case the trend reverses: temperature rise increases with carbon number.

Sources of Error and Improvements

Source of errorWhy it mattersImprovement
Heat loss to the surroundingsMost of the energy from the flame does not actually heat the water — it heats the air and the calorimeter wallsUse a lid, a draught shield, and a polished metal calorimeter to reduce conduction, convection and radiation losses
Incomplete combustionSpirit burners produce a yellow flame with soot (carbon) which means some energy is "lost" as unreacted carbon rather than released as heatEnsure a good air supply; use a bomb calorimeter (industrial method) for accurate results
Evaporation of alcohol from the wickThe burner continues losing mass even after the flame is out, so the recorded mass burnt is higher than the mass that actually burntReplace the cap immediately after extinguishing; record mass quickly
Inaccurate temperature readingThe thermometer takes time to respond and may not be at the bulk water temperatureStir continuously and read the maximum temperature after extinguishing
Distance from wick to calorimeterIf the distance varies between alcohols, some flames reach the calorimeter more effectively than othersClamp the calorimeter at a fixed measured height; use the same burner shape where possible

Because so much energy is lost, experimental values for the heat of combustion are always lower than the data-book values — and the percentage error is typically 50–80%. The pattern across the series is still meaningful, but the absolute energy values are not.

Examiner Report Insights

*1CH0/2F — June 2019 Examiner Report:** On the Core Practical for the combustion of alcohols: *"It was evident that many candidates had failed to read the question properly since many answered by stating 'stop heating the water once it had reached 30 °C', as opposed to a temperature increase of 30 °C. Many candidates confused the mass of the burner with the mass of ethanol."

These two specific errors come up in almost every sitting of this Core Practical and are worth understanding in detail:

*Error 1 — "30 °C as final temperature" vs "30 °C temperature rise"*

The question asks you to heat the water until the temperature has risen by 30 °C, not until the thermometer reads 30 °C. If the water starts at 20 °C, you stop at 50 °C, not at 30 °C. Reading "30 °C" as the final temperature instead of as the rise gives you a much smaller temperature change and ruins the comparison between alcohols. When you describe the method in an extended-response question, write something like "stop heating once the temperature has risen by 30 °C" — not "stop heating once the temperature reaches 30 °C".

*Error 2 — Mass of burner vs mass of alcohol*

The balance does not weigh the alcohol directly — it weighs the whole spirit burner, including the metal body, wick and lid, plus the alcohol inside. The mass of alcohol burnt is the difference between the mass of the burner before and after:

`` mass of alcohol burnt = mass of burner before − mass of burner after ``

If you write something like "the mass of alcohol was 165.20 g" (the whole burner reading), you have given the wrong quantity. Always subtract — and always state clearly that you are using the change in burner mass.

How Heat of Combustion of Alcohols Appears in Exams

This Core Practical appears in the middle-to-late section of Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H). It very frequently shows up in two formats:

  • Data-interpretation graph question — you are given a graph of mass burnt vs carbon number (or temperature rise vs carbon number) and asked to describe the trend, read off values, calculate mass loss or temperature rise from a table, or extrapolate.
  • Extended 6-mark method question — you are asked to describe the full experimental procedure, often referring to a labelled figure of the apparatus.

The command words seen on this Core Practical include "Describe", "Draw", "Plot", "Calculate" and "Explain".

Example: 1CH0/2F June 2021 Q7(b)(ii) — a 6-mark extended-method question: "Describe an experiment, using the equipment in Figure 16, that could be used to obtain results like those shown in Figure 15" (measuring mass loss of alcohol burners). For this style of question, you need to mention: a fixed volume/mass of water in the calorimeter; recording initial water temperature; weighing the burner before; stopping at a fixed temperature rise; reweighing the burner; calculating the mass burnt; and repeating with each alcohol while keeping the water mass, distance from wick, and target temperature rise constant.

Mathematical Skills

The skills assessed on this practical include arithmetic computation, interpreting charts, and plotting variables. You will be expected to:

  1. Subtract to find mass burnt: m<sub>burnt</sub> = m<sub>before</sub> − m<sub>after</sub>
  2. Subtract to find temperature rise: ΔT = T<sub>final</sub> − T<sub>initial</sub>
  3. Plot mass burnt (y) against number of carbon atoms (x), or temperature rise (y) against number of carbon atoms (x)
  4. Interpret the trend from the plotted graph or a results table
  5. Read off values to one decimal place from a graph or table

While the equation Q = m × c × ΔT (energy transferred to water) is not on this spec point, it underpins the practical's purpose — the larger the temperature rise (or the less alcohol needed for the same rise), the more energy released per gram of fuel.

Command Words Table

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
DescribeA step-by-step account of the method or an account of the trend in the data2–6
DrawA labelled diagram of the apparatus, or axes for a graph2–4
PlotAdd points (and a line/curve of best fit) to a pre-drawn grid using the data given2–3
CalculateWork out a numerical answer with appropriate units, showing working1–3
ExplainLink an observation (e.g. "the result is too low") to a cause (e.g. "heat lost to surroundings")2–4
Key Takeaways
  1. The Core Practical compares the energy released by ethanol, propanol, butanol and pentanol by burning each in turn and measuring either the mass burnt for a fixed temperature rise, or the temperature rise for a fixed mass of fuel.
  2. The independent variable is the alcohol used; the dependent variable is the mass burnt (or temperature rise); key control variables are mass of water, starting temperature, distance from wick to calorimeter, and target temperature rise.
  3. Mass burnt is the difference between the mass of the spirit burner before and after, not the total mass of the burner — confusing these is a top examiner-reported error.
  4. The target is a temperature rise of 30 °C above the starting temperature — not a final reading of 30 °C on the thermometer; this is the other top examiner-reported error.
  5. Apparatus essentials: spirit burner, copper calorimeter, clamp and stand, thermometer (or probe), balance to 0.01 g, measuring cylinder, lid and draught shield.
  6. Safety: alcohols are highly flammable — cap the burner to extinguish, never blow out; wear goggles; use a heatproof mat; allow apparatus to cool before handling.
  7. The general trend is that mass burnt decreases as the carbon chain lengthens — longer-chain alcohols release more energy per gram because more bonds are broken and formed during combustion.
  8. To plot the graph: number of carbon atoms on the x-axis, mass burnt (or temperature rise) on the y-axis; draw a smooth curve of best fit.
  9. Experimental values are always lower than data-book values because of heat loss to surroundings, incomplete combustion, evaporation from the wick and warming of the calorimeter rather than the water.
  10. Improvements: lid on the calorimeter, draught shield, polished metal calorimeter, fixed wick-to-base distance, immediate capping of the burner to stop evaporation losses.
  11. On 1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H Paper 2, this practical appears most commonly as a 6-mark extended-method question or a data-interpretation graph question, with command words Describe, Draw, Plot, Calculate and Explain.
  12. When describing the method in an extended-response question, explicitly state that you stop heating when the temperature has risen by 30 °C and that the mass of fuel burnt is found by subtracting the final from the initial mass of the burner (not just the alcohol).
9.4.3.1 Carboxylic Acids F+H ▼

Carboxylic Acids

Spec 9.29C: Recall the formulae of molecules of the carboxylic acids, methanoic, ethanoic, propanoic and butanoic acids, and draw the structures of these molecules, showing all covalent bonds. Spec 9.30C: Recall that the functional group in carboxylic acids is –COOH. (Foundation + Higher tier, Separate Chemistry only.)

Carboxylic acids are the third major homologous series you meet at GCSE, after alkanes/alkenes and alcohols. They are the organic molecules behind vinegar (ethanoic acid), the smell of rancid butter (butanoic acid), the sting of ant and nettle venom (methanoic acid), and the sourness of fermented milk (propanoic acid is found in some cheeses, and the related lactic acid in yogurt). Recognising them, drawing them with every covalent bond shown, and quoting the right molecular formula is what 9.29C and 9.30C test.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
Carboxylic acidAn organic compound whose functional group is –COOH (a carboxyl group); general formula CₙH₂ₙ₊₁COOH
Functional groupThe atom or group of atoms in a molecule that gives it its characteristic chemical reactions; for carboxylic acids this is –COOH
Carboxyl group (–COOH)A carbon atom double-bonded to one oxygen (C=O) and single-bonded to a hydroxyl group (–O–H)
Homologous seriesA family of organic compounds that share the same functional group, have the same general formula, and differ from each other by –CH₂–
Displayed (structural) formulaA 2-D structure that shows every atom and every covalent bond as a line
Molecular formulaA formula that gives the number of each kind of atom in a molecule, e.g. CH₃COOH

The four named carboxylic acids you must know

Spec 9.29C names exactly four members: methanoic, ethanoic, propanoic and butanoic acid. These are the first four members of the homologous series. The pattern in the name is identical to the alkane/alcohol pattern: the meth-/eth-/prop-/but- stem tells you the total number of carbon atoms (including the carbon in –COOH), and the –oic acid ending tells you it is a carboxylic acid.

NameMolecular formulaCondensed formulaMrCommon source
Methanoic acidHCOOHHCOOH46Ant/nettle stings (formic acid)
Ethanoic acidCH₃COOHCH₃COOH60Vinegar
Propanoic acidC₂H₅COOHCH₃CH₂COOH74Some cheeses; food preservative
Butanoic acidC₃H₇COOHCH₃CH₂CH₂COOH88Rancid butter

Notice three patterns at once:

  • Mr increases by 14 each step (one extra –CH₂– is +12 + 2 = +14). This is the textbook signature of a homologous series.
  • The general formula CₙH₂ₙ₊₁COOH only works for n = 1, 2, 3, … i.e. ethanoic acid onwards. Methanoic acid is the n = 0 exception — there is no alkyl group, so the formula reduces to HCOOH. A common student error is to write methanoic acid as "CH₃COOH" by mistake — that is ethanoic acid. Methanoic acid only has one carbon atom in total, and that carbon is the one in the –COOH group.
  • Every molecule ends in –COOH, which is the functional group required by 9.30C.

The –COOH functional group — drawing it correctly (the critical skill)

This is the single most common place students lose marks on this spec point, and an Edexcel examiner report calls it out directly. You must show:

  1. A central carbon atom (the carboxyl carbon)
  2. That carbon double-bonded to one oxygen (C=O) — this oxygen is not bonded to anything else
  3. That same carbon single-bonded to a second oxygen, which is then single-bonded to a hydrogen (–O–H)
  4. That same carbon single-bonded to either an H (methanoic acid) or an alkyl group –CH₃, –C₂H₅, –C₃H₇ (ethanoic, propanoic, butanoic)

So the carboxyl carbon makes four bonds in total: one C=O (counts as two of the four), one C–O, and one C–C (or C–H for methanoic acid). Oxygen and hydrogen also satisfy their normal bonding: the carbonyl oxygen has 2 bonds (both to the C, the C=O), the hydroxyl oxygen has 2 bonds (one to C, one to H), and every H has 1 bond.

*Correct vs incorrect drawing of –COOH*

The Edexcel 1CH0/2F — June 2019 Examiner Report flags exactly this error: "Very few candidates were able to draw the carboxylic acid group correctly... the carboxylic acid functional group has been incorrectly drawn with a double bond between the carbon and hydroxyl group, as opposed to between the carbon the oxygen atom." In other words, candidates put the C=O double bond in the wrong place — they drew C=O–H (a double bond to the OH oxygen) instead of C(=O)–O–H (a double bond to the lone carbonyl oxygen, single bond to the –OH).

*Correct (what gets the mark):*

`` O ‖ …—C—O—H ``

The double bond (drawn as ‖ or =) is between the carboxyl carbon and the lone oxygen on the side. The other oxygen is single-bonded to C and single-bonded to H. That second oxygen has two single bonds, not one.

*Incorrect (what loses the mark, per June 2019):*

`` …—C=O—H ← WRONG: double bond on the wrong oxygen | ``

or

`` …—C—O=H ← WRONG: oxygen cannot double-bond to hydrogen ``

Hydrogen only ever forms one covalent bond — it has only one electron to share. So H=O is impossible. Oxygen needs two bonds to complete its octet, so in –COOH one oxygen achieves this with a single double bond to C (the carbonyl oxygen, C=O), and the other achieves it with one single bond to C and one to H (the hydroxyl oxygen, –O–H).

If you remember "the double bond is on the lonely oxygen, never on the OH oxygen" you will pick up this drawing mark every time.

Displayed structures of the four named acids

Showing all covalent bonds (including C–H bonds — do not leave any "implied" bonds in a displayed-structure answer) is the wording of spec 9.29C. Below, every line represents one shared pair of electrons.

Methanoic acid (HCOOH) — 1 carbon, Mr = 46

`` O ‖ H — C — O — H ``

The single carbon is bonded to: one H (left), one =O (top, double bond), and one –O–H (right). Four bonds in total. No –CH₂– or –CH₃ group at all.

Ethanoic acid (CH₃COOH) — 2 carbons, Mr = 60

`` H O | ‖ H — C ——— C — O — H | H ``

The left-hand carbon (the methyl carbon) makes 4 single bonds: 3 to H, 1 to the carboxyl carbon. The right-hand carbon (the carboxyl carbon) makes 4 bonds: 1 to the methyl carbon, 1 double bond C=O (top), 1 single bond to O which then bonds to H.

Propanoic acid (CH₃CH₂COOH or C₂H₅COOH) — 3 carbons, Mr = 74

`` H H O | | ‖ H — C — C ——— C — O — H | | H H ``

Three carbons in a chain. The carboxyl carbon (right) carries the –COOH. The middle and end carbons are saturated –CH₂– and –CH₃ groups.

Butanoic acid (CH₃CH₂CH₂COOH or C₃H₇COOH) — 4 carbons, Mr = 88

`` H H H O | | | ‖ H — C — C — C ——— C — O — H | | | H H H ``

Four carbons in a chain, all single-bonded apart from the C=O on the carboxyl carbon. Every carbon has 4 bonds; every hydrogen has 1 bond; the carbonyl oxygen has 2 bonds (both to C); the hydroxyl oxygen has 2 bonds (one to C, one to H).

Why the carboxyl group has these specific bonds

Carbon needs four bonds to be stable (full outer shell of 8 electrons). Oxygen needs two, and hydrogen needs one. In –COOH:

  • The carbonyl C=O uses 2 of the carbon's 4 bonds and both of one oxygen's 2 bonds. That oxygen is now satisfied with no spare bonds — it cannot pick up a hydrogen. That is why it is wrong to draw H attached to the carbonyl oxygen.
  • The hydroxyl –O–H uses 1 of the carbon's remaining 2 bonds. That oxygen has 1 bond to C and 1 bond to H — 2 bonds total — so it is also satisfied.
  • The carbon's final bond goes to a hydrogen (methanoic acid) or to the next carbon in the chain (ethanoic, propanoic, butanoic).

Putting the double bond on the wrong oxygen would force one of the oxygens to have 3 bonds (impossible at GCSE) and would leave the other oxygen short. The arrangement is fixed by the bonding rules — it is not a stylistic choice.

Foundation vs Higher — what changes

The spec point is identical for F and H tier. Both tiers need to:

  • recall the molecular formulae of methanoic, ethanoic, propanoic, butanoic acids
  • recognise –COOH as the carboxyl/functional group
  • draw the displayed structures showing every covalent bond

The Higher tier may push the same content into slightly longer questions or pair it with a "predict the next member" or "predict products" task — but the underlying skill (correct –COOH drawing) is the same.

Worked example: identifying a carboxylic acid from a formula

Q. A compound has the molecular formula C₃H₆O₂. State whether this could be a carboxylic acid, and if so, name it and draw its displayed structure.

*Step 1 — does it fit the general formula?*

General formula for a carboxylic acid is CₙH₂ₙ₊₁COOH, i.e. CₙH₂ₙO₂ overall (since the COOH adds 1 C, 2 O, and 1 H to CₙH₂ₙ₊₁ minus one H makes the alkyl side). For propanoic acid, n = 3: C₃H₆O₂ ✓.

*Step 2 — count carbons.*

3 carbons → prop- → propanoic acid.

*Step 3 — draw it with every covalent bond.*

`` H H O | | ‖ H — C — C ——— C — O — H | | H H ``

Check: 3 C atoms ✓, 6 H atoms ✓, 2 O atoms ✓, every C has 4 bonds, both O atoms have 2 bonds, the C=O double bond is on the lone carbonyl oxygen — not on the –OH oxygen ✓.

Mathematical Skill 5b — translating between diagrammatic and numerical forms

Edexcel's Mathematical Skill 5b ("translate information between diagrammatic and numerical forms") underpins this spec point: you have to convert between a written name, a molecular formula, and a displayed structure showing every covalent bond.

A reliable procedure:

  1. Read the name. The first part (meth/eth/prop/but) tells you the carbon count: 1 / 2 / 3 / 4.
  2. Draw a chain of that many carbon atoms in a row.
  3. Mark the right-hand carbon as the carboxyl carbon and attach the –COOH group: =O on top, –O–H on the side.
  4. Fill in –H atoms on every other carbon so each carbon has exactly 4 bonds.
  5. Double-check: every C has 4 bonds, every H has 1 bond, every O has 2 bonds, the C=O sits on the lone (terminal) oxygen.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — June 2019 Examiner Report: On the task of drawing the structure of ethanoic acid, the report states "Very few candidates were able to draw the carboxylic acid group correctly... the carboxylic acid functional group has been incorrectly drawn with a double bond between the carbon and hydroxyl group, as opposed to between the carbon the oxygen atom." The takeaway: candidates drew C=O–H instead of C(=O)–O–H. If you put the C=O double bond on the wrong oxygen — the one carrying the H — you will lose the structure mark.

How Carboxylic Acids Appears in Exams

  • Paper / position: Middle-to-late Paper 2 (1CH0/2F or 1CH0/2H). This is Separate-only content (Section 9), so it does not appear on the Combined Science papers.
  • Standard task: Draw the displayed structure of a named carboxylic acid (most often ethanoic acid), showing all covalent bonds, per the June 2019 example flagged above.
  • Paired with: Sometimes paired with a homologous-series trends question (recall the general formula, recognise the –COOH group, identify the next member of the series).
  • Command words seen: "Draw" (for the displayed structure), "State" (for the functional group or a formula), "Name" (for identifying which acid corresponds to a given structure or formula).

Command Words table

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
DrawA displayed structure showing every covalent bond, with the –COOH group correctly arranged (C=O on the lone oxygen, –O–H on the other)1–2
StateA short factual answer — e.g. "the functional group is –COOH" or "the formula is CH₃COOH"1
NameThe correct name of a carboxylic acid given its formula or structure (e.g. "propanoic acid")1
Key Takeaways
  1. The four named carboxylic acids you must recall are methanoic (HCOOH), ethanoic (CH₃COOH), propanoic (C₂H₅COOH) and butanoic (C₃H₇COOH) acid.
  2. The functional group of every carboxylic acid is –COOH (the carboxyl group), per spec 9.30C.
  3. The –COOH group is a carbon double-bonded to one oxygen (C=O on the lone, terminal oxygen) and single-bonded to an –O–H group; the carbon also makes one further single bond to H (methanoic) or to an alkyl chain.
  4. Hydrogen forms only one covalent bond — it can never be double-bonded to oxygen, so any structure showing H=O is wrong.
  5. The most common Edexcel mistake (1CH0/2F June 2019 Examiner Report) is putting the C=O double bond on the wrong oxygen — the one carrying the H — instead of on the lone carbonyl oxygen.
  6. Methanoic acid is the n = 0 exception with only one carbon (HCOOH); it does not have a –CH₃ group. Do not confuse it with ethanoic acid.
  7. Carboxylic acids form a homologous series with general formula CₙH₂ₙ₊₁COOH (or CₙH₂ₙO₂ overall for n ≥ 1); successive members differ by –CH₂– and Mr increases by 14.
  8. A displayed structure must show every covalent bond — including all C–H bonds and the O–H bond — not just the carbon skeleton.
  9. Common command words on this SP are "Draw", "State" and "Name"; the standard exam task is drawing the displayed structure of a named carboxylic acid on Paper 2.
  10. This SP is Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only — it does not appear on 1SC0 Combined Science papers.
9.4.3.2 Making Ethanoic Acid F+H ▼

Making Ethanoic Acid

Spec point 9.31C: "Recall that ethanol can be oxidised to produce ethanoic acid and extend this to other alcohols (reagents not required)." Examined at Foundation and Higher tier, Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only, Paper 2.

This spec point is short on the syllabus but high on connections. It links three different parts of your course together: the alcohols homologous series (9.4.1), the carboxylic acids homologous series (9.4.3.1), and the idea of oxidation as adding oxygen to a starting material. The key fact is that ethanol can be oxidised to ethanoic acid, and the same pattern can be extended to any primary alcohol in the series. You are not required to recall any specific reagents — only the reactants, the products, and the type of reaction.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
OxidationA reaction in which oxygen is added to a substance (or hydrogen is removed)
AlcoholAn organic compound containing the −OH (hydroxyl) functional group
Carboxylic acidAn organic compound containing the −COOH (carboxyl) functional group
EthanolThe two-carbon alcohol with formula CH₃CH₂OH (or C₂H₅OH)
Ethanoic acidThe two-carbon carboxylic acid with formula CH₃COOH
Homologous seriesA family of compounds with the same functional group and general formula, differing by a −CH₂− unit
VinegarA dilute aqueous solution of ethanoic acid, typically 4–8% by volume

Why ethanol oxidises to ethanoic acid

Ethanol contains the −OH group on the end carbon of a two-carbon chain. When ethanol is oxidised, the carbon that holds the −OH group gains another bond to oxygen. The end result is that the alcohol functional group (−CH₂OH) is converted into a carboxylic acid functional group (−COOH). The carbon skeleton is unchanged — both ethanol and ethanoic acid have two carbons in their chain — so the product is ethanoic acid, not propanoic or methanoic acid.

You can summarise the reaction in words:

`` ethanol + oxygen → ethanoic acid + water ``

Or with structural formulae:

`` CH₃CH₂OH + [O] → CH₃COOH + H₂O ``

The [O] notation means "an oxidising agent" without specifying which one — this matches the spec, which says reagents not required. The reaction can be carried out in the lab using an oxidising agent (such as an acidified solution containing dichromate ions), but you do not need to name it for this spec point.

The carbon-number link — why the names match up

Students very often miss the most useful pattern in this topic: the alcohol and the carboxylic acid have the same number of carbon atoms. The oxidation only changes the functional group at the end of the chain; it does not add or remove any carbon atoms. So the carbon-number stem of the name stays the same.

Number of carbonsPrimary alcoholCarboxylic acid product
1 (meth-)Methanol, CH₃OHMethanoic acid, HCOOH
2 (eth-)Ethanol, CH₃CH₂OHEthanoic acid, CH₃COOH
3 (prop-)Propanol, CH₃CH₂CH₂OHPropanoic acid, CH₃CH₂COOH
4 (but-)Butanol, CH₃(CH₂)₃OHButanoic acid, CH₃(CH₂)₂COOH

The naming rule is simple: the alcohol ends in -anol, and its oxidation product ends in -anoic acid, but the stem (meth-, eth-, prop-, but-) is identical. If you can name the alcohol, you can name the carboxylic acid it forms.

Comparing the two functional groups

The structural change in the molecule is local — only the right-hand end of the chain changes.

FeatureAlcohol (ethanol)Carboxylic acid (ethanoic acid)
Functional group−OH (hydroxyl)−COOH (carboxyl)
Structural formulaCH₃CH₂OHCH₃COOH
General formula (homologous series)CₙH₂ₙ₊₁OHCₙH₂ₙ₊₁COOH (where n is the number of C's in the alkyl part, so total C count = n + 1)
Number of oxygens12
Effect on universal indicator in waterStays neutral (~pH 7)Turns orange/red (weakly acidic, pH ~2–3)
SmellSweet/alcoholicSharp, vinegar-like

Notice the oxygen count: the carboxylic acid has one more oxygen atom than the alcohol it came from. That extra oxygen has come from the oxidising agent — which is why we describe the reaction as oxidation.

Extending the pattern to other alcohols

The spec explicitly tells you to extend the rule to other alcohols. The general statement is:

> A primary alcohol can be oxidised to the carboxylic acid with the same number of carbon atoms.

Worked examples:

*Methanol → Methanoic acid*

`` CH₃OH + [O] → HCOOH + H₂O ``

Both molecules have one carbon. Methanol is the simplest alcohol; methanoic acid is the simplest carboxylic acid (also called formic acid — found in ant and nettle stings).

*Propan-1-ol → Propanoic acid*

`` CH₃CH₂CH₂OH + [O] → CH₃CH₂COOH + H₂O ``

Both molecules have three carbons. The −OH on the end carbon is converted into a −COOH; the rest of the chain is untouched.

*Butan-1-ol → Butanoic acid*

`` CH₃CH₂CH₂CH₂OH + [O] → CH₃CH₂CH₂COOH + H₂O ``

Both molecules have four carbons. Butanoic acid is responsible for the smell of rancid butter.

If a question gives you any alcohol from the series (methanol up to butanol) and asks for the carboxylic acid produced on oxidation, the procedure is always the same: keep the carbon count the same, swap the -anol ending for -anoic acid.

Biological oxidation — making vinegar

Ethanoic acid is the chemical name for the acid in vinegar, and vinegar is made industrially and traditionally by leaving an ethanol-containing liquid (such as wine, cider or beer) exposed to air. The oxidation is carried out by microbes — bacteria of the genus Acetobacter — which use enzymes to convert ethanol to ethanoic acid in the presence of oxygen from the air.

The net chemistry is identical to the lab reaction:

`` ethanol + oxygen → ethanoic acid + water CH₃CH₂OH + O₂ → CH₃COOH + H₂O ``

This is the second biological transformation in the wine/vinegar story:

  1. Sugars are fermented by yeast (anaerobic, no oxygen) to make ethanol and CO₂ → see 9.4.4.1 Fermentation
  2. Ethanol is oxidised by bacteria (aerobic, needs oxygen) to make ethanoic acid

That is why a bottle of wine left open turns sour — once the oxygen reaches the ethanol, the bacteria do the rest. Sealed bottles store wine because they keep oxygen out and stop step 2 from happening.

Why the product is acidic

Ethanol is a neutral molecule, but ethanoic acid is a weak acid. The reason is the −COOH group. In aqueous solution, the −COOH group can lose its hydrogen as a proton (H⁺), forming the ethanoate ion CH₃COO⁻:

`` CH₃COOH (aq) ⇌ CH₃COO⁻ (aq) + H⁺ (aq) ``

The −OH group in ethanol does not ionise in water in the same way, which is why ethanol is essentially pH-neutral. The structural change from −OH to −COOH is what gives the molecule its acidic behaviour, and that is the chemical reason vinegar tastes sour.

For more on the properties of the carboxylic acid family, see 9.4.3.1 Carboxylic Acids.

Examiner Report Insights

No examiner-report data available for this spec point in the current notebook.

The wider Topic 9.4 narrative in the notebook covers oxidation of alcohols as a homologous-series example, and a related examiner comment on carboxylic-acid trends from 1CH0/2H November 2021 is associated with the neighbouring spec point 9.4.3.3 Predicting Products rather than with 9.31C directly.

How Making Ethanoic Acid Appears in Exams

Based on the exam-appearance data in the current notebook:

  • Paper(s): 1CH0/2F (Foundation) and 1CH0/2H (Higher), Separate Chemistry only — this content does not appear on the Combined Science (1SC0) papers
  • Position: Middle-to-late Paper 2
  • Question types: Short equation-writing or product-identification items
  • Context: Sometimes embedded in a longer question about identifying a fermented or oxidised product (often a vinegar/wine context)
  • Command words used: "State", "Name", "Give"

Typical things you might be asked:

  • Name the product when ethanol is oxidised
  • Write a word equation for the oxidation of ethanol
  • Name the carboxylic acid formed when a named alcohol (e.g. propanol or butanol) is oxidised
  • Identify the type of reaction taking place when wine turns into vinegar
  • State why a bottle of wine left open to air goes sour

Because the spec says reagents not required, you will not be asked to name the oxidising agent — but you may still be asked to give a word equation or a balanced symbol equation using the [O] notation.

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateA short factual answer; no explanation1
NameGive the chemical name of a substance, ion, or species1
GiveProvide a short, specific piece of information1

These are all low-tariff command words. Questions on this spec point are usually worth 1 mark each, occasionally 2 if you are asked for the product and a word/symbol equation.

A worked exam-style example

> "Wine is made by fermenting grape juice. If a bottle of wine is left open to the air, it slowly turns sour and develops the taste of vinegar. > > (a) Name the alcohol present in wine. (1) > (b) Name the acid responsible for the sour taste of vinegar. (1) > (c) Write a word equation for the reaction that produces this acid from the alcohol in part (a). (2)"

*Suggested answers:*

  • (a) Ethanol
  • (b) Ethanoic acid
  • (c) ethanol + oxygen → ethanoic acid + water

Notice that the carbon count is the same in (a) and (b): both molecules are two-carbon (eth-). That is the easiest pattern to memorise and the most reliable way to avoid losing marks.

Common student errors to avoid

  • Writing the wrong product name. A frequent slip is writing "ethanol acid" or "ethanoate" instead of "ethanoic acid". The correct full name is ethanoic acid.
  • Changing the carbon count. Some students answer "propanoic acid" when asked for the oxidation product of ethanol. The carbon count does not change in this reaction.
  • Forgetting water as a co-product. The full word equation includes water on the right-hand side — leaving it out can cost a mark in a 2-mark equation question.
  • Naming a reagent when none is required. The spec explicitly says reagents are not required. You will not be penalised for not naming the oxidising agent, but you also should not invent one.
  • Confusing oxidation with fermentation. Fermentation is anaerobic (yeast, no oxygen, makes ethanol from sugar). Oxidation of ethanol is aerobic (bacteria or chemical oxidising agent, requires oxygen, makes ethanoic acid from ethanol). They are two different reactions in two different steps.
Key Takeaways
  1. Ethanol is oxidised to ethanoic acid; the word equation is ethanol + oxygen → ethanoic acid + water.
  2. The symbol equation is CH₃CH₂OH + [O] → CH₃COOH + H₂O — [O] represents an unspecified oxidising agent (you do not need to recall reagents).
  3. The reaction is an oxidation because oxygen is added (the carboxylic acid has one more oxygen atom than the alcohol).
  4. The functional group changes from −OH (alcohol) to −COOH (carboxylic acid), but the rest of the carbon chain is unchanged.
  5. The number of carbon atoms is the same in the alcohol and its oxidation product: ethanol (C2) → ethanoic acid (C2); propanol (C3) → propanoic acid (C3); butanol (C4) → butanoic acid (C4); methanol (C1) → methanoic acid (C1).
  6. The naming pattern is: drop the -anol ending of the alcohol and replace it with -anoic acid for the oxidation product, keeping the same stem (meth-, eth-, prop-, but-).
  7. Vinegar is a dilute aqueous solution of ethanoic acid, made by allowing the ethanol in wine, cider or beer to be oxidised by bacteria in the air.
  8. Wine turns sour when exposed to air because Acetobacter bacteria oxidise the ethanol in the wine to ethanoic acid — the same overall reaction as the lab oxidation.
  9. Ethanoic acid is a weak acid because the −COOH group can release a proton (H⁺) in aqueous solution, forming ethanoate (CH₃COO⁻); ethanol does not do this and is neutral.
  10. Common exam command words for this spec point are "Name", "State" and "Give" — usually 1-mark items on Paper 2.
  11. This spec point is Separate Chemistry only (1CH0) and is examined on Paper 2 at both Foundation and Higher tier.
  12. Do not change the carbon count when answering: the oxidation product of an n-carbon primary alcohol is always the n-carbon carboxylic acid.
9.4.3.3 Predicting Products F+H ▼

Predicting Products

Spec 9.32C — Recall members of a given homologous series have similar reactions because their molecules contain the same functional group and use this to predict the products of other members of these series. Foundation + Higher tier, Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only, Paper 2.

This spec point ties together everything you have learned about organic chemistry into one core idea: if you know how one member of a homologous series reacts, you can predict how every other member reacts, because they all share the same functional group. Examiners do not want you to memorise an endless list of equations — they want you to spot the pattern and apply it.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
Homologous seriesA family of organic compounds with the same general formula and the same functional group, differing by a –CH₂– unit between consecutive members
Functional groupThe atom or group of atoms in a molecule that determines its characteristic chemical reactions
General formulaAn algebraic formula that gives the molecular formula of any member of a homologous series (e.g. CₙH₂ₙ₊₁COOH for carboxylic acids, CₙH₂ₙ₊₁OH for alcohols)
Relative formula mass (Mr)The sum of the relative atomic masses of all the atoms in the formula unit
EsterAn organic compound formed when a carboxylic acid reacts with an alcohol; contains the –COO– linkage
Sodium salt (of a carboxylic acid)The ionic compound formed when a carboxylic acid reacts with sodium (or sodium hydroxide / sodium carbonate); contains the –COO⁻Na⁺ group

*Why members of a homologous series react the same way*

A homologous series is held together by one shared feature — the functional group. The carbon chain attached to the group can be one carbon long (methanoic acid, HCOOH), two carbons long (ethanoic acid, CH₃COOH), three carbons long (propanoic acid, C₂H₅COOH) and so on, but the reactive part of every molecule is always the same –COOH. Because chemistry happens at the functional group, the same reagents attack each member in the same way and produce the same type of product. The only thing that changes from one member to the next is the length of the carbon chain hanging off the functional group — and that changes the size of the product, not the kind of reaction.

This is the central insight you are being asked to use. If methanoic acid plus sodium gives sodium methanoate plus hydrogen, then propanoic acid plus sodium must give sodium propanoate plus hydrogen. You are not learning a new reaction — you are reapplying one you already know.

The –CH₂– Increment and Mr by 14

Consecutive members of a homologous series differ by exactly one –CH₂– unit. That unit has a relative formula mass of:

`` Mr(-CH₂-) = 12 + (2 × 1) = 14 ``

So every time you move up the series by one carbon, the Mr of the molecule increases by 14. This is the numerical fingerprint of a homologous series and is the easiest way to check that a predicted product is plausible.

Carboxylic acidFormulaMr
Methanoic acidHCOOH46
Ethanoic acidCH₃COOH60
Propanoic acidC₂H₅COOH74
Butanoic acidC₃H₇COOH88

Each row goes up by 14. The same pattern holds for the alcohols (methanol Mr 32, ethanol Mr 46, propan-1-ol Mr 60, butan-1-ol Mr 74) and the alkanes, alkenes and every other homologous series on the spec.

Predicting Products — Worked Examples

Example 1 — Carboxylic acid + reactive metal

You are told that ethanoic acid + magnesium → magnesium ethanoate + hydrogen:

`` 2 CH₃COOH + Mg → (CH₃COO)₂Mg + H₂ ``

Predict the products of propanoic acid + magnesium.

The functional group is still –COOH. The reaction must be the same type — acid + reactive metal → salt + hydrogen. Replace "ethanoate" with "propanoate":

`` 2 C₂H₅COOH + Mg → (C₂H₅COO)₂Mg + H₂ ``

Products: magnesium propanoate and hydrogen.

Example 2 — Carboxylic acid + sodium

Sodium reacts with the –COOH group exactly the same way for every carboxylic acid:

`` 2 CH₃COOH + 2 Na → 2 CH₃COONa + H₂ (ethanoic acid → sodium ethanoate) ``

Predict the products of propanoic acid + sodium:

`` 2 C₂H₅COOH + 2 Na → 2 C₂H₅COONa + H₂ ``

Products: sodium propanoate and hydrogen. The salt is named by swapping the "-ic acid" ending for "-oate" and putting the metal name in front.

Example 3 — Carboxylic acid + sodium carbonate

The –COOH group reacts with carbonates to give a salt, water and carbon dioxide:

`` 2 CH₃COOH + Na₂CO₃ → 2 CH₃COONa + H₂O + CO₂ ``

Predict the products of butanoic acid + sodium carbonate:

`` 2 C₃H₇COOH + Na₂CO₃ → 2 C₃H₇COONa + H₂O + CO₂ ``

Products: sodium butanoate, water and carbon dioxide. The effervescence test (limewater turning milky) works for every member of the series.

Example 4 — Esterification (carboxylic acid + alcohol)

The standard reaction in the spec is ethanoic acid + ethanol → ethyl ethanoate + water, with concentrated sulfuric acid as the catalyst:

`` CH₃COOH + C₂H₅OH → CH₃COOC₂H₅ + H₂O ``

Predict the product of methanol + ethanoic acid:

`` CH₃COOH + CH₃OH → CH₃COOCH₃ + H₂O ``

Product: methyl ethanoate and water. The naming rule is "alcohol part first (as the –yl group), acid part second (as the –oate group)". So:

  • Methanol + ethanoic acid → methyl ethanoate
  • Ethanol + propanoic acid → ethyl propanoate
  • Propan-1-ol + ethanoic acid → propyl ethanoate

Every esterification produces water as the second product, regardless of which acid and alcohol you start with.

Example 5 — Combustion (any organic homologous series)

Complete combustion of any hydrocarbon, alcohol or carboxylic acid in excess oxygen always gives carbon dioxide and water. So if you know:

`` CH₃COOH + 2 O₂ → 2 CO₂ + 2 H₂O (ethanoic acid) ``

Then propanoic acid must give:

`` C₂H₅COOH + 7/2 O₂ → 3 CO₂ + 3 H₂O ``

The products are the same types of molecule — only the number of CO₂ and H₂O changes with chain length.

Trends in Physical Properties

Although the spec point is about predicting chemical products, the November 2021 examiner report (see below) explicitly links this skill to predicting trends in physical properties along the series. The pattern you need to know:

PropertyTrend going up the homologous seriesReason
Relative formula mass (Mr)Increases by 14 per stepEach step adds –CH₂– (Mr = 14)
Boiling pointIncreasesLonger chains have more electrons → stronger intermolecular forces, so more energy is needed to separate the molecules
DensityIncreases (carboxylic acids and alcohols approach the density of water and then exceed it)More mass per unit volume as chain length grows
Solubility in waterDecreases with chain lengthThe non-polar hydrocarbon chain becomes the dominant feature; the polar functional group is "outweighed"
ViscosityIncreasesStronger intermolecular forces resist flow
FlammabilityDecreasesLarger molecules are harder to vaporise into the flame

When a question asks you to predict the boiling point of a missing member, you can either extrapolate linearly from the data given or simply state that it will be higher than the member below and lower than the member above.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2H — November 2021 Examiner Report: On the carboxylic-acid trends question, examiners noted: "Most able candidates mentioned the formation of esters and the production of sodium salts… a good number recognised the significance some or all of -COOH, carboxylic acid and formula mass increasing by 14… The best candidates identified the common functional group and hence similar chemical reactions, and then identified the patterns in the boiling point and in the density." The clear takeaway: the highest-scoring answers named the functional group, named the product types (esters and sodium salts), and connected the +14 Mr pattern to the predicted trends. Weaker answers stopped at "they react similarly" without naming what the products actually are.

No further examiner-report data available for this spec point in the current notebook.

How Predicting Products Appears in Exams

Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H)

  • Position and type: Middle-to-late Paper 2. Multi-mark questions ask candidates to predict the product when a different alcohol or carboxylic acid undergoes the same reaction. These are often paired with trends in physical properties (Mr, boiling point, density), so the question rewards students who can hold the chemistry and the maths together.
  • Foundation tier focus: Usually a "name the product" or "complete the word equation" item, with a stem that gives one worked reaction and asks for the next member's product by analogy.
  • Higher tier focus: Expect to give the full balanced symbol equation for the predicted reaction, plus an explanation that explicitly names the functional group as the reason the reaction type is the same.

No further exam-appearance data for this spec point in the current notebook.

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
PredictState a product, formula or trend that is consistent with a given pattern; no full justification required1–2
ExplainGive the reason the prediction works — must mention shared functional group, similar reactions, or the +14 Mr pattern2–4
DescribeSet out a trend (e.g. boiling point increases up the series) or a reaction in prose; data or named products expected2–3

*How to answer a "predict and explain" question*

  1. Name the functional group shared by all members (e.g. –COOH).
  2. State that members of a homologous series react in the same way because they share that functional group.
  3. Give the predicted product(s) by analogy with the worked example in the stem.
  4. If the question also asks about Mr, boiling point or density, use the +14 increment to support your prediction.

This four-step structure mirrors what the November 2021 report praised in the strongest answers.

Key Takeaways
  1. Members of a homologous series share the same functional group, the same general formula, and differ by a –CH₂– unit between consecutive members.
  2. Because they share a functional group, every member undergoes the same types of reaction — only the size of the product changes.
  3. The relative formula mass increases by 14 each step up the series (the mass of –CH₂–); this is the fastest plausibility check on a predicted formula.
  4. Carboxylic acid + reactive metal → salt + hydrogen; the salt is named by changing "-ic acid" to "-oate" (e.g. propanoic acid + sodium → sodium propanoate + hydrogen).
  5. Carboxylic acid + carbonate → salt + water + carbon dioxide; works for every carboxylic acid in the series.
  6. Carboxylic acid + alcohol → ester + water; the ester name has the alcohol part first ("-yl") and the acid part second ("-oate"), e.g. methanol + ethanoic acid → methyl ethanoate + water.
  7. Complete combustion of any organic molecule in excess oxygen always gives carbon dioxide and water; balance for chain length only.
  8. Going up a homologous series: boiling point, density and viscosity increase; solubility in water and flammability decrease.
  9. The November 2021 Higher Paper 2 examiner report (1CH0/2H) singled out candidates who named the –COOH group, named esters and sodium salts as the product types, and linked the +14 Mr pattern to trends in boiling point and density.
  10. When answering "Predict and explain" questions, always name the functional group first, then give the product, then justify with the shared-reaction rule.
  11. Do not invent new reactions for higher members — you only need to extend the reaction already in the stem to a longer chain.
  12. Predictions for physical properties of a missing member can be made by interpolation (between the members above and below) or by extrapolation using the +14 Mr step.
9.4.4.1 Fermentation F+H ▼

Fermentation

Spec 9.33C: Describe the production of ethanol by fermentation of carbohydrates in aqueous solution, using yeast to provide enzymes. This is a Separate Chemistry only spec point, examined on Paper 2 at both Foundation and Higher tier.

Fermentation is the industrial and traditional method for producing ethanol (the alcohol in beer, wine and spirits) from sugars. It is one of two routes to ethanol you need to know for Edexcel GCSE — the other being the hydration of ethene from crude oil. Fermentation is a batch process, uses a renewable feedstock (sugar from crops), and runs at low temperatures with very little energy input. The whole spec point comes down to four things: the reactant (a carbohydrate, usually glucose, in aqueous solution), the biological catalyst (yeast, which provides enzymes), the conditions (warm temperature around 30 °C, anaerobic), and the products (ethanol and carbon dioxide).

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
FermentationThe breakdown of sugars by yeast enzymes to produce ethanol and carbon dioxide
YeastA single-celled fungus that produces the enzymes needed to convert sugars to ethanol
EnzymeA biological catalyst (a protein) that speeds up a specific reaction in a living organism
CarbohydrateA class of compounds including sugars (e.g. glucose) and starches that contain carbon, hydrogen and oxygen
Aqueous solutionA solution where water is the solvent — required so that yeast cells, enzymes and sugar can mix and react
AnaerobicIn the absence of oxygen — the condition under which yeast converts sugar to ethanol rather than to carbon dioxide and water
