DenaturedWhen a protein (such as an enzyme) loses its 3D shape and stops working, usually because of heat or extreme pH

The fermentation equation

The word equation you must be able to write:

*glucose → ethanol + carbon dioxide*

The symbol equation:

C₆H₁₂O₆ (aq) → 2C₂H₅OH (aq) + 2CO₂ (g)

A few things to notice about this equation:

  • The reactant is glucose, a carbohydrate with the formula C₆H₁₂O₆.
  • Each glucose molecule produces two ethanol molecules and two carbon dioxide molecules.
  • Ethanol is written as C₂H₅OH (or CH₃CH₂OH), showing the −OH functional group that makes it an alcohol.
  • State symbols matter: the glucose and ethanol are aqueous because the reaction takes place in solution; the CO₂ leaves the mixture as a gas (you can see it bubbling out, which is why bread dough rises and beer is fizzy).
  • There is no oxygen on the reactant side. Fermentation is an anaerobic process — adding oxygen would let the yeast respire fully and produce CO₂ and water instead of ethanol.

The role of yeast — and the yeast → enzyme → denaturation chain

This is the single most important conceptual link in the spec point, and examiners have flagged that candidates routinely miss part of it. You need to be able to set out a chain of reasoning that goes:

  1. Yeast is added to the sugar solution.
  2. The yeast cells contain (and release) enzymes — these are the actual catalysts for the reaction.
  3. Enzymes are proteins, and proteins lose their shape (denature) when heated too strongly.
  4. At around 30 °C the enzymes work at a sensible rate; at 80 °C they would denature and stop working completely.
  5. So if you ran fermentation at 80 °C the enzymes inside the yeast would be destroyed, the reaction would stop, and no ethanol would be made.

When a question asks "why is fermentation carried out at 30 °C rather than 80 °C", the high-mark answer must contain both halves: that yeast provides enzymes, and that enzymes denature at high temperatures. Stating only that "enzymes denature at 80 °C" is what loses marks — the examiner wants the link to yeast made explicit.

Equally, you cannot run fermentation cold either. Below about 20–25 °C the enzymes are working too slowly and the reaction takes far too long. At 0 °C the enzymes are effectively inactive (which is why you store bread yeast in the fridge to keep it dormant). The sweet spot at around 30 °C is the temperature at which the yeast's enzymes are most active without being damaged.

Why each condition matters

ConditionWhat it isWhy it is needed
Yeast addedA single-celled fungus, added as a powder or live cultureYeast cells release the enzymes that catalyse the conversion of sugar to ethanol; without yeast there is no reaction
Temperature ~ 30 °CWarm, but well below boilingHot enough for the enzymes (and yeast cells) to be active, but cool enough that the enzymes are not denatured
Aqueous solutionSugar dissolved in waterYeast cells need to be suspended in water to take up sugar; the enzymes also work in solution
AnaerobicNo oxygen (a sealed vessel, often with a one-way valve)In the absence of oxygen yeast carries out fermentation, producing ethanol; in the presence of oxygen the yeast respires fully and produces CO₂ and water instead of ethanol
TimeDays to weeksThe reaction is slow because enzymes are working at body-temperature-ish; the mixture is left until fermentation is complete

A common Foundation-tier mistake is to say the mixture is heated "to make the reaction go faster". That is true in principle for most reactions (collision theory) but it is not what you do here, because raising the temperature too far would destroy the catalyst. The control of temperature in fermentation is therefore a balance between rate and enzyme stability — not just maximising rate.

Why fermentation eventually stops

Fermentation does not run forever. There are two reasons it stops:

  1. The sugar runs out. Once all the glucose has been converted, there is no more reactant.
  2. The ethanol concentration gets too high. Yeast cells die when the ethanol concentration in the mixture reaches around 15% — the ethanol itself denatures the proteins inside the yeast cells. This is why naturally fermented drinks (wine, beer) never get stronger than roughly 15% ABV. To make spirits (vodka, whisky), the fermentation mixture must be distilled afterwards — see 9.4.4.2 Purifying the Product.

If the fermentation vessel is left open to the air after the sugar has run out, the ethanol can be oxidised by airborne bacteria (or by atmospheric oxygen with a microbial catalyst) to ethanoic acid — this is how wine turns into vinegar. That oxidation is covered in 9.4.3.2 Making Ethanoic Acid.

Fermentation vs. hydration of ethene — quick comparison

Although this revision note is specifically about fermentation, the most common exam framing is to compare the two industrial routes to ethanol. The basics of that comparison:

FeatureFermentationHydration of ethene
Raw materialSugar / carbohydrate (renewable, from crops)Ethene from cracking crude oil (non-renewable)
Type of processBatchContinuous
Temperature~ 30 °C (low)~ 300 °C (high)
PressureAtmosphericHigh (~ 60–70 atm)
CatalystYeast enzymesPhosphoric acid
RateSlow (days/weeks)Fast
Purity of ethanolImpure aqueous mixture — needs distillationPure ethanol produced directly
Atom economyLower — CO₂ is a waste product100% (only ethanol made)

You do not need every cell of this table for SP 9.4.4.1 itself, but understanding it helps explain why fermentation is preferred in some contexts (renewable feedstock, low energy input, simple equipment) and not in others (purity, speed, scale).

A worked through-line of an "explain" answer (the high-tariff version)

Imagine the question is the kind that has been used at this tier: "Explain why fermentation is carried out at a temperature of 30 °C rather than 80 °C." A 2-mark answer that hits the spec-point reasoning would be:

> "Yeast provides the enzymes that catalyse fermentation (1). At 80 °C these enzymes would be denatured and so the reaction would stop / no ethanol would be made (1). 30 °C is warm enough for the enzymes to work at a reasonable rate without being denatured."

The two scoring points are: (a) yeast → enzymes link, and (b) denaturation at 80 °C. Answers that have only the second of those — i.e. "the enzymes denature at 80 °C" with no mention of where the enzymes come from — are the ones examiners have specifically said miss marks.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2F — June 2019 Examiner Report (on explaining why fermentation is carried out at 30 °C rather than 80 °C): "Few candidates were able to correctly link the idea of enzymes and yeast and to discuss enzymes denaturing. Most responses identified that enzymes are denatured at 80 °C but not that yeast provided these enzymes." The point being made is exactly the chain set out above — yeast → enzyme → denaturation. Candidates picked up half of that chain (the denaturation bit) but did not say where the enzymes come from. To pick up full marks on this style of question you have to spell out both halves.
  • 1CH0/2H — November 2021 Examiner Report: "While most candidates mentioned yeast, the idea of warming the mixture to a limited extent was less well stated..." Here the issue is the reverse: students mention yeast but do not pin down why 30 °C specifically — i.e. they do not explain that the temperature has to be warm enough for the enzymes to work but cool enough that they are not denatured. Stating only "yeast is needed" without the temperature reasoning is not enough at Higher tier.

The pattern across both reports is consistent: marks are lost not because candidates do not know the topic, but because the link between yeast, enzymes and temperature is incomplete. When you revise this SP, practise writing the chain out as a single sentence — that is the form that picks up both marks.

How Fermentation Appears in Exams

Fermentation questions sit in the middle-to-late part of Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H), within the Separate Chemistry 2 content on alcohols. The stems almost always set up the conditions (temperature, anaerobic environment, aqueous solution, yeast) and then probe one of three things:

  • Describe-style questions: state the conditions, identify the products, or write the word equation.
  • Explain-style questions: link a condition (especially temperature) to its underlying reason — i.e. enzymes denaturing.
  • State-style questions: short factual recall — the name of the catalyst, the product, the role of yeast.

A specific example: 1CH0/2F June 2019 Q10(a) asked "Explain why the reaction is carried out at a temperature of 30 °C rather than at a temperature of 80 °C" and was worth 2 marks. The two marking points were (i) the yeast → enzyme link, and (ii) the denaturation idea. The 2019 examiner report (cited above) makes clear that most candidates only got the second of those.

The recurring stem details — yeast, ~ 30 °C, aqueous, anaerobic — are reused across sittings, so it is worth practising them until they are automatic.

Command Words table

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateA short factual answer; no explanation (e.g. "name the product", "give the temperature")1
DescribeA short prose answer covering the main features (conditions, equation, what happens)2–3
ExplainReasoning that links cause to effect — e.g. why 30 °C, why anaerobic, why yeast is needed2–3

Source: command words observed in NLM exam-appearance data for this SP ("Explain", "Describe", "State").

Key Takeaways
  1. Fermentation converts sugars (carbohydrates) into ethanol and carbon dioxide using yeast in aqueous solution under anaerobic conditions at around 30 °C.
  2. The word equation is: glucose → ethanol + carbon dioxide. The symbol equation is: C₆H₁₂O₆ (aq) → 2C₂H₅OH (aq) + 2CO₂ (g).
  3. Yeast is a single-celled fungus that provides the enzymes which catalyse the reaction — yeast is not the catalyst directly, the enzymes inside the yeast are.
  4. Enzymes are proteins, and proteins denature (lose their shape and function) when heated too strongly — so high temperatures stop the reaction completely.
  5. Around 30 °C is the working compromise: warm enough for the enzymes to be active, cool enough that they are not denatured. At 80 °C the enzymes would denature and no ethanol would be made.
  6. Fermentation is carried out in aqueous solution so that the yeast cells and enzymes can move freely and access the dissolved sugar.
  7. Fermentation is anaerobic — the absence of oxygen is what makes the yeast produce ethanol rather than respiring fully to CO₂ and water.
  8. Fermentation stops when either the sugar is used up or the ethanol concentration becomes high enough (around 15%) to kill the yeast.
  9. Compared to the hydration of ethene, fermentation uses a renewable feedstock, runs at low temperature, is a batch process, and produces an impure aqueous mixture of ethanol that must be distilled to be concentrated — see 9.4.4.2 Purifying the Product.
  10. The high-mark "explain why 30 °C not 80 °C" answer must contain two ideas: (a) yeast provides the enzymes, and (b) those enzymes would denature at 80 °C.
  11. Common exam-losing mistakes: mentioning denaturation without linking it to yeast; mentioning yeast without explaining why 30 °C specifically; saying the mixture is heated "to speed up the reaction" without noting the upper temperature limit.
  12. Fermentation is a Separate Chemistry only topic (1CH0) and is examined on Paper 2 at both Foundation and Higher tier.
9.4.4.2 Purifying the Product F+H ▼

Purifying the Product

Spec point 9.34C — Explain how to obtain a concentrated solution of ethanol by fractional distillation of the fermentation mixture. Examined on Foundation and Higher tier, Separate Chemistry only, Paper 2.

Fermentation in 9.4.4.1 Fermentation gives you a dilute aqueous solution of ethanol — typically around 10–15% ethanol in water by volume, plus dead yeast cells and dissolved sugars. To make this solution useful as a fuel, a solvent, or as an industrial feedstock, you need to concentrate the ethanol. The technique used is fractional distillation, which separates two liquids that have different boiling points but are otherwise fully mixed (miscible).

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
Fractional distillationA separation technique that separates two or more miscible liquids using their different boiling points
MiscibleTwo liquids that mix completely in any proportion to form a single layer
Boiling pointThe temperature at which a pure liquid changes into vapour at atmospheric pressure
Fractionating columnA vertical glass column, usually packed with glass beads or rods, that sits between the heated flask and the condenser; it creates a vertical temperature gradient
CondenserA water-cooled glass tube in which vapour is cooled back into liquid
ThermometerThe instrument used to monitor the temperature of the vapour at the top of the fractionating column
Fermentation mixtureThe dilute ethanol–water mixture (typically 10–15% ethanol) produced by yeast acting on a sugar solution

Why fractional distillation works

Pure ethanol boils at 78 °C and pure water boils at 100 °C. That 22 °C gap is what makes the separation possible. When you heat the fermentation mixture, the more volatile component — ethanol — turns to vapour first. If you can collect the vapour produced at a temperature close to 78 °C, and stop the water from joining it, you end up with a much more concentrated ethanol product than you started with.

A simple distillation alone would not give a clean separation, because at any temperature between 78 °C and 100 °C, water is also evaporating to some extent. The fractionating column is the piece of apparatus that solves this problem. It sits vertically between the heated flask and the condenser, and is packed with glass beads (or short lengths of glass rod) that provide a large surface area.

As the mixed ethanol/water vapour travels up the column:

  • The vapour cools as it rises (the column is at room temperature at the top and much hotter at the bottom)
  • Water vapour, which has the higher boiling point, condenses first and trickles back down the column into the flask
  • Ethanol vapour, which has the lower boiling point, continues up the column
  • Only when the vapour reaches a part of the column that is at or above 78 °C does ethanol-rich vapour pass over the top into the condenser

The repeated evaporation and condensation on the beads inside the column is sometimes called "many simple distillations stacked on top of each other" — each cycle enriches the rising vapour in the more volatile component (ethanol).

The apparatus — step by step

You should be able to draw and label this apparatus, and describe how you would use it.

ComponentPurpose
Round-bottom flaskHolds the fermentation mixture; sits in a heating mantle or on a tripod with gauze and Bunsen
Anti-bumping granulesPromote smooth boiling and prevent the liquid from suddenly boiling over
Fractionating columnPacked with glass beads; creates the vertical temperature gradient that separates ethanol from water
ThermometerPlaced at the top of the fractionating column, with its bulb level with the side-arm to the condenser, so it reads the temperature of the vapour leaving the column
CondenserA water-cooled glass tube angled downwards; water flows in at the lower end and out at the upper end (counter-current)
Collection vesselA clean conical flask or beaker that catches the condensed liquid (the distillate)

*The procedure:*

  1. Place the fermentation mixture and a few anti-bumping granules in the round-bottom flask.
  2. Fit the fractionating column on top of the flask, with the thermometer at the top of the column and the condenser leading off to one side.
  3. Turn on the cold-water flow through the condenser jacket (in at the bottom, out at the top).
  4. Heat the flask gently. The mixture starts to boil; mixed vapour rises into the fractionating column.
  5. Watch the thermometer reading. As ethanol vapour reaches the top of the column, the temperature reads close to 78 °C.
  6. The ethanol vapour passes into the condenser, where it cools and condenses back into liquid ethanol, which drips into the collection vessel.
  7. While the thermometer reads close to 78 °C, you are collecting a concentrated ethanol fraction.
  8. When the thermometer reading begins to climb sharply towards 100 °C, water vapour is starting to pass over too — stop collecting, or change the collection vessel.

Why the location of the fractionating column matters

This is the single most common point that examiners report candidates getting wrong (see Examiner Report Insights below). The fractionating column must be vertical, between the flask and the condenser, with the thermometer at the top of the column. If a student draws or describes the column in the wrong place — for example, horizontal, or after the condenser, or with the thermometer at the bottom — they have not described fractional distillation at all.

The column has to be vertical because the separation relies on gravity: water vapour that condenses inside the column has to drain back down into the flask under its own weight. If the column is horizontal, condensed water just sits there, no temperature gradient is established, and ethanol and water leave together.

The thermometer has to be at the top of the column (with the bulb level with the side-arm leading to the condenser) because that is where the vapour about to enter the condenser passes the thermometer. A thermometer reading of 78 °C at that point confirms it is ethanol vapour that is being collected. A thermometer placed lower down — for example, in the flask itself — would read the temperature of the boiling mixture, which tells you nothing useful about what is leaving the apparatus.

Why "pre-boiling" and "ice-pack on the condenser" are wrong

Examiners have reported that weaker candidates suggest a range of incorrect "improvements" instead of describing the actual apparatus. Two of the most common — both of which appear in the November 2021 examiner report — are worth picking apart, because the reasoning shows up at the heart of how fractional distillation works.

"Pre-boiling the dilute ethanol": Some candidates suggest that boiling the fermentation mixture first will get rid of the water, leaving concentrated ethanol behind. This is the wrong way round. Ethanol has the lower boiling point (78 °C), so ethanol boils off before water. Pre-boiling would lose ethanol and concentrate the water — the opposite of what you want. The only thing pre-boiling could possibly achieve is to drive off the most volatile component, which is the very component you are trying to keep.

"Putting an ice-pack around the condenser": Other candidates suggest cooling the condenser more aggressively, as if a colder condenser would somehow separate ethanol from water more effectively. The condenser is already cooled by running cold water; making it colder still does not improve the separation. Separation happens in the fractionating column, not the condenser. By the time the vapour reaches the condenser, the ethanol/water separation has already either succeeded (if the column was set up correctly) or failed (if it was not). All the condenser does is turn vapour back into liquid. Adding ice would only condense whatever vapour reaches it, including water vapour if the column has not done its job.

The general lesson: you cannot "fix" a poorly described fractional distillation by adding cooling at the wrong end of the apparatus. The separation has to happen in the column, by the temperature gradient. If a student's answer reaches for tricks at the condenser end, that is a sign they have not understood where the actual separation happens.

Comparison — simple vs fractional distillation

FeatureSimple distillationFractional distillation
What it separatesA pure liquid from a dissolved solid (e.g. water from salt water)Two or more miscible liquids with different boiling points
ColumnNone — direct from flask to condenserFractionating column packed with glass beads, between flask and condenser
Temperature gradientNoneHot at bottom, cool at top
Used for ethanol/water?No — would give a mixed product, not concentrated ethanolYes — separates ethanol (78 °C) from water (100 °C)

How concentrated can you make the ethanol?

Fractional distillation can produce a solution of about 96% ethanol in water. You cannot get pure (100%) ethanol by distillation alone, because at this composition the ethanol and water form what is called an azeotrope — a mixture that boils as if it were a single substance. This detail is not required by the spec, but it is worth knowing that "concentrated solution of ethanol" in the spec wording acknowledges that the product is concentrated, not pure.

Examiner Report Insights

*1CH0/2H — November 2021 Examiner Report:** *"A majority of candidates realised that fractional distillation was required; very few were able to describe the location of the fractionating column. Weaker candidates suggested all manner of remedies such as pre-boiling the dilute ethanol or putting an ice pack around the condenser."

What this tells you for the exam:

  • Identifying that "fractional distillation" is the right technique is not enough for full marks on its own — most candidates get that part. Marks are won by describing the apparatus correctly.
  • The position of the fractionating column is the discriminator. Be specific: vertical, on top of the flask, with the condenser leading off the top and a thermometer at the top of the column.
  • Avoid the trap of suggesting modifications to the wrong piece of apparatus (the condenser). The condenser's job is to condense vapour, not to separate it.
  • If you are asked to draw the apparatus, label the column, the thermometer position, and the direction of water flow through the condenser.

How "Purifying the Product" Appears in Exams

This spec point is part of Section 9 (Separate Chemistry 2) and is only assessed on Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H).

  • Position and type: Typically appears in the middle-to-late portion of Paper 2, as an extended-method or 4–6 mark "describe how" item that asks candidates to set up fractional distillation and explain how it concentrates the ethanol from the fermentation mixture. May appear together with 9.4.4.1 Fermentation as a single multi-part question on producing and purifying ethanol from a sugar source.
  • Command words: Explain, Describe, Draw are the typical command words tied to this SP.
  • What you need to be able to do: name the technique (fractional distillation); describe or draw the apparatus including the position of the fractionating column; identify the thermometer position and what reading confirms ethanol is being collected; use the boiling-point difference (78 °C ethanol vs 100 °C water) to explain why the technique works.

Command Words Table

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
DescribeA step-by-step prose answer covering how the apparatus is set up and what happens at each stage3–4
ExplainReasoning that links the boiling-point difference to the separation, and the column position to the temperature gradient4–6
DrawA labelled diagram of the apparatus showing flask, column, thermometer position, condenser with water flow, and collection vessel2–4
Key Takeaways
  1. Fermentation gives only a dilute ethanol/water mixture (around 10–15% ethanol); to obtain a concentrated ethanol solution you use fractional distillation.
  2. The separation works because ethanol boils at 78 °C and water boils at 100 °C — a 22 °C gap.
  3. The apparatus is: round-bottom flask containing the fermentation mixture (with anti-bumping granules) → fractionating column packed with glass beads → thermometer at the top of the column → condenser → collection vessel.
  4. The fractionating column must be vertical, between the flask and the condenser. Its position is the most common thing candidates get wrong.
  5. The column creates a temperature gradient — hot at the bottom, cooler at the top. Water vapour condenses inside the column and runs back down; ethanol vapour passes through and out to the condenser.
  6. The thermometer at the top of the column confirms ethanol is being collected: a steady reading near 78 °C means ethanol vapour is leaving the column.
  7. The condenser cools the ethanol vapour back into liquid; cold water flows through the jacket counter-current to the vapour.
  8. You stop collecting when the thermometer reading rises sharply towards 100 °C — that is water vapour starting to come over.
  9. "Pre-boiling the dilute ethanol" does not help: ethanol has the lower boiling point, so pre-boiling would lose ethanol, not water.
  10. "Putting an ice-pack around the condenser" does not help: separation happens in the column, not the condenser. Extra cooling at the condenser just condenses whatever vapour reaches it.
  11. Fractional distillation can concentrate ethanol up to about 96%, but not 100% — beyond this, ethanol and water form a mixture that boils as a single substance.
  12. This spec point is examined on Paper 2 only (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H), Separate Chemistry only — it is not part of Combined Science (1SC0).
9.5.1.1 Size of Nanoparticles F+H ▼

Size of Nanoparticles

Spec point 9.35C: Compare the size of nanoparticles with the sizes of atoms and molecules. Foundation + Higher tier · Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only · Paper 2.

Nanoparticles sit in a length-scale window between individual atoms and the everyday "fine powder" particles you can see under an optical microscope. The whole reason nanochemistry is treated as a separate topic — and the reason it has its own block of marks in Paper 2 — is that particle size itself controls behaviour at this scale. Before you can argue about catalysis, sun-cream, or possible health risks, you have to be fluent in the numbers: what counts as a nanoparticle, how big is an atom, and how do those sizes compare in standard form.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
NanoparticleA particle with at least one dimension between 1 nm and 100 nm (1 × 10⁻⁹ m to 1 × 10⁻⁷ m)
Nanometre (nm)A unit of length equal to 1 × 10⁻⁹ m (one billionth of a metre)
NanoscienceThe study of structures and materials on the nanometre scale, and how their properties differ from the same substance in bulk
Coarse particle (dust)A particle with a diameter between roughly 2.5 × 10⁻⁶ m (2 500 nm) and 1 × 10⁻⁴ m (100 000 nm); visible as dust
Fine particle (PM2.5)A particle with a diameter between 1 × 10⁻⁷ m (100 nm) and 2.5 × 10⁻⁶ m (2 500 nm)
Bulk materialThe same substance in everyday large-scale form, typically with particles much larger than 1 × 10⁻⁶ m
Order of magnitudeA factor of 10; "two orders of magnitude bigger" means 100× bigger
Standard formA number written as A × 10ⁿ where 1 ≤ A < 10 and n is an integer

What the spec actually asks for

Spec point 9.35C is a comparison statement. The examinable skill is being able to put nanoparticles, atoms, and molecules in size order, give realistic numerical values for each in standard form, and convert fluently between nanometres and metres. Foundation papers usually test this as a "state" or "compare" recall step; Higher papers usually fold the size into a calculation, most often the surface-area-to-volume ratio question that follows.

The size scale, from atom to bulk material

Sizes in chemistry span many orders of magnitude, and only standard form keeps the numbers manageable. The table below is the one to learn. The diameters quoted are typical, indicative values — real atoms vary across the periodic table and real "nanoparticles" cover a range — but the orders of magnitude are the load-bearing knowledge.

ObjectTypical diameter (m)Typical diameter (nm)Order of magnitude (m)
Atom (e.g. carbon, oxygen)~1 × 10⁻¹⁰~0.110⁻¹⁰
Small molecule (e.g. H₂O, CO₂)~3 × 10⁻¹⁰ to 1 × 10⁻⁹~0.3 to 110⁻¹⁰ to 10⁻⁹
Nanoparticle1 × 10⁻⁹ to 1 × 10⁻⁷1 to 10010⁻⁹ to 10⁻⁷
Fine particle (PM2.5)1 × 10⁻⁷ to 2.5 × 10⁻⁶100 to 2 50010⁻⁷ to 10⁻⁶
Coarse particle (dust)2.5 × 10⁻⁶ to 1 × 10⁻⁴2 500 to 100 00010⁻⁶ to 10⁻⁴
Bulk material grain> 1 × 10⁻⁶ (often much larger)> 1 00010⁻⁶ and above

Reading the table across, an atom is roughly one ten-thousand-millionth of a metre across; a nanoparticle is ten to one thousand times bigger than a single atom; a fine particle is another factor of one hundred bigger again; a grain of bulk powder is bigger still. The size labels in chemistry are not arbitrary — they map onto the powers of ten in the right-hand column.

Why the nanoparticle window is set at 1 nm – 100 nm

The lower bound (1 nm) is essentially the size of a small molecule, so anything smaller than 1 nm is by convention treated as a molecule or cluster rather than a "particle". The upper bound (100 nm) is the cut-off where the unusual nanoscale properties — very high surface-area-to-volume ratio, quantum and optical effects, very high reactivity — start to fade and the material begins to behave like a fine powder. The 1 nm – 100 nm window is therefore the size range where chemistry changes character because of size alone, even though the substance is the same.

A useful mental picture: a 100 nm nanoparticle is about 1 000 atoms across its diameter (because 100 nm ÷ 0.1 nm per atom = 1 000). A 1 nm nanoparticle is about 10 atoms across — barely bigger than a small molecule. The whole nanoparticle range therefore spans particles from ten atoms across up to roughly a thousand atoms across.

Converting between nanometres and metres

Two conversions come up in almost every nanoparticle question. You must be confident with both.

*Worked example 1 — nm to m (standard form)*

A zinc oxide nanoparticle in sun cream has a diameter of 30 nm. Express this diameter in metres in standard form.

`` 1 nm = 1 × 10⁻⁹ m 30 nm = 30 × 10⁻⁹ m = 3.0 × 10⁻⁸ m ``

The key step is recognising that "30 ×" must be rewritten as "3.0 ×" to get the leading number into the 1 ≤ A < 10 range, which adds one to the index of 10.

*Worked example 2 — m to nm*

A silver nanoparticle has a diameter of 7.5 × 10⁻⁸ m. What is this in nanometres?

`` 1 m = 1 × 10⁹ nm 7.5 × 10⁻⁸ m = 7.5 × 10⁻⁸ × 10⁹ nm = 7.5 × 10¹ nm = 75 nm ``

A 75 nm particle is comfortably inside the 1 nm – 100 nm nanoparticle window.

*Worked example 3 — comparing two sizes (orders of magnitude)*

How many times bigger is a 50 nm nanoparticle than a typical 0.1 nm atom?

`` ratio = 50 nm / 0.1 nm = 500 ``

So the nanoparticle is 500 times bigger than a single atom across one diameter, or "two and a bit orders of magnitude" larger. This is the type of comparison statement that picks up the "compare" mark on Foundation papers.

*Worked example 4 — nanoparticle vs bulk grain*

A talc grain in baby powder might be 50 μm (50 × 10⁻⁶ m) across. How many times bigger is that than a 50 nm nanoparticle of the same material?

`` 50 μm = 50 × 10⁻⁶ m = 5.0 × 10⁻⁵ m 50 nm = 50 × 10⁻⁹ m = 5.0 × 10⁻⁸ m ratio = (5.0 × 10⁻⁵) / (5.0 × 10⁻⁸) = 1 × 10³ = 1 000 ``

The bulk grain is 1 000 times the diameter of the nanoparticle — three orders of magnitude bigger. Volume scales as diameter cubed, so the bulk grain is roughly 1 000³ = 1 × 10⁹ times the volume of the nanoparticle, which is why the same mass of material in nanoparticle form has so much more exposed surface area.

How nanoparticles compare to molecules

Small molecules like H₂O, CO₂, and CH₄ sit at the very bottom edge of the nanoparticle scale — about 0.3 to 1 nm across. A 1 nm nanoparticle is therefore not much bigger than a single small molecule, which is part of why the lower bound of "nano" is set at 1 nm. Large biological molecules (proteins, DNA double helix diameter) creep into the lower end of the nanoparticle range — a haemoglobin molecule is about 5 nm across — but for the purposes of 1CH0 the comparison the spec wants is the simple one:

  • a water molecule ≈ 1 × 10⁻¹⁰ m
  • a small nanoparticle ≈ 1 × 10⁻⁹ m (ten times bigger than a molecule)
  • a large nanoparticle ≈ 1 × 10⁻⁷ m (one thousand times bigger than a molecule)

If a comparison question asks you to put atoms, molecules, and nanoparticles in size order with example values, write:

> Atoms (~1 × 10⁻¹⁰ m) ≤ small molecules (~1 × 10⁻⁹ m) < nanoparticles (1 × 10⁻⁹ m to 1 × 10⁻⁷ m) < fine particles (> 1 × 10⁻⁷ m).

Examiner Report Insights

No examiner-report quote is targeted specifically at the size comparison spec point (9.35C). The wider Topic 9.5 reports focus on properties, uses, and risks of nanoparticles rather than on the numerical size comparison itself. Worth noting from NLM: examiners frequently set this material at the front of Paper 2 (Q1 or Q2), so candidates meeting the size question are typically working on a fresh paper and should treat the standard-form arithmetic as marks worth banking early.

How Size of Nanoparticles appears in exams

Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H) — this is a Separate Chemistry-only SP, so it never appears on the Combined Science 1SC0 papers.

  • Position: almost exclusively at the very beginning of Paper 2, as Q1 or Q2. The nanoparticle block is consistently placed early in the paper.
  • Style on Foundation: short "state" or "compare" questions asking for a size value (or a size range) of a nanoparticle, often with a multiple-choice or fill-the-blank option. Standard-form conversion may be scaffolded.
  • Style on Higher: more often a lead-in to a multi-step calculation. NLM notes that a 3-mark surface-area-to-volume-ratio calculation (covered in spec point 9.36C) frequently follows immediately after the size question, and the standard-form conversion you do for size is reused in that calculation.
  • Common command words (from NLM): "Calculate", "State", "Compare".

If you see "Calculate" on this SP, expect to convert between nm and m in standard form. If you see "Compare", give two size values (one for the nanoparticle, one for the atom or molecule) and make an explicit comparison statement — e.g. "the nanoparticle is approximately 100 times larger than the water molecule".

Mathematical Skills

NLM tags this SP against three GCSE maths skills:

  • 1b — Recognise and use expressions in decimal form
  • 1d — Recognise and use expressions in standard form
  • 2h — Use an appropriate number of significant figures, and use orders of magnitude

The procedure you must be fluent in:

`` nm → m : value (nm) × 10⁻⁹ = value (m) m → nm: value (m) × 10⁹ = value (nm) ``

Step-by-step for any conversion question:

  1. Identify the unit you have been given and the unit you need.
  2. Multiply by 10⁻⁹ (nm → m) or 10⁹ (m → nm).
  3. Combine indices of 10 using the rule 10ᵃ × 10ᵇ = 10ᵃ⁺ᵇ.
  4. Rewrite the leading number so it sits in the range 1 ≤ A < 10, adjusting the index accordingly.
  5. Quote your answer to the same number of significant figures as the data in the question (usually 2 or 3 sig figs at GCSE).

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateA short factual size value or range, no working1
CompareTwo size values and an explicit comparative statement1–2
CalculateA numerical answer with working shown; standard form required where appropriate2–3
Key Takeaways
  1. A nanoparticle has at least one dimension between 1 nm and 100 nm, i.e. between 1 × 10⁻⁹ m and 1 × 10⁻⁷ m.
  2. 1 nm = 1 × 10⁻⁹ m; convert nm to m by multiplying by 10⁻⁹, and m to nm by multiplying by 10⁹.
  3. A typical atom is ~1 × 10⁻¹⁰ m across — about one tenth of a nanometre.
  4. A small molecule (e.g. H₂O) is ~1 × 10⁻¹⁰ to 1 × 10⁻⁹ m across — at most about 1 nm.
  5. Nanoparticles are roughly 10 to 1 000 times bigger than a single atom across their diameter.
  6. The top of the nanoparticle range (100 nm) is about 1 000 atoms across; the bottom (1 nm) is about 10 atoms across.
  7. Above 100 nm you move into fine particles (PM2.5, up to ~2 500 nm) and coarse particles (dust, up to ~100 000 nm), which behave like bulk material rather than nano.
  8. Bulk material grains are typically larger than 1 × 10⁻⁶ m (1 000 nm and up) — three or more orders of magnitude bigger than a nanoparticle.
  9. Standard form is essential: rewrite "30 × 10⁻⁹" as "3.0 × 10⁻⁸" so the leading number is in the range 1 ≤ A < 10.
  10. The spec point appears on Paper 2 only and is Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only — it is not on Combined Science 1SC0 papers.
  11. The size question is almost always at the start of Paper 2 (Q1 or Q2) and often feeds into a surface-area-to-volume-ratio calculation that follows.
  12. For "Compare" questions, always quote two size values and state the relationship between them (e.g. "100 times larger", "two orders of magnitude bigger").
9.5.1.2 Properties of Nanoparticles F+H ▼

Properties of Nanoparticles

Spec point 9.36C: Describe how the properties of nanoparticulate materials are related to their uses including surface area to volume ratio of the particles they contain, including sunscreens. Foundation + Higher tier, Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only, Paper 2.

Nanoparticles behave very differently from the same substance in bulk. The reason almost always comes back to one geometric fact: as particles get smaller, the proportion of their atoms that sit on the surface rises sharply. That single idea — measured by the surface area to volume ratio (SA:V) — explains why nanoparticulate titanium dioxide is used in sunscreens, why nanoscale silver kills bacteria, and why nanoparticles often make highly effective catalysts.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
NanoparticleA particle with at least one dimension between approximately 1 and 100 nm (1 nm = 1 × 10⁻⁹ m)
Surface areaThe total area of the outer faces of a particle, measured in nm², m², or cm²
VolumeThe space occupied by the particle, measured in nm³, m³, or cm³
Surface area to volume ratio (SA:V)The surface area of a particle divided by its volume; often expressed as a simplified ratio such as 1:10
Nanoparticulate materialA material made from, or containing, nanoparticles, whose properties differ from the bulk form of the same substance

Why surface area to volume ratio matters

Every chemical reaction or physical interaction with the outside world (absorbing UV, killing bacteria, catalysing a reaction) happens at the surface of a particle. Atoms buried in the middle of a bulk lump do nothing useful — only the surface atoms can interact with the surroundings.

If you take a fixed mass of a substance and break it into smaller and smaller pieces, the total volume stays the same, but the total surface area grows enormously. So the fraction of atoms exposed at the surface rises, and the substance behaves more reactively, more strongly as a catalyst, or more effectively as a UV absorber.

*Why SA:V increases as size decreases (cube example):*

For a cube with side length l:

  • Surface area = 6 × l² (six faces, each of area l²)
  • Volume = l³
  • SA:V = 6l² / l³ = 6 / l

The key point is the l in the denominator. As l shrinks, 6 / l gets larger. Halve the side length and you double the SA:V; cut it to a tenth and SA:V increases tenfold. This is why moving from millimetre-scale particles to nanometre-scale particles increases SA:V by a factor of roughly a million.

Consequences of high SA:V

Because nanoparticles have so much surface relative to their volume, they tend to:

  • React faster — more atoms are exposed and able to take part in collisions with reactant particles
  • Make more effective catalysts — catalysis happens on the catalyst surface, and a higher SA:V means more active sites per gram of catalyst (see 7.1.4.1 Catalysts)
  • Absorb and scatter electromagnetic radiation differently — including absorbing ultraviolet (UV) light while remaining transparent to visible light
  • Have different optical and electrical behaviour — nanoparticles often appear different colours from the bulk solid (gold nanoparticles can look red, for example)
  • Be more biologically active — small enough to enter cells and cross biological barriers, which is both useful (drug delivery) and a source of risk (see 9.5.1.3 Risks)

Uses of nanoparticulate materials

Titanium dioxide (TiO₂) in sunscreens. Bulk titanium dioxide is a white pigment used in paint. Nanoparticulate TiO₂ has the same chemistry but a hugely greater SA:V. It absorbs UV light from the sun strongly while staying transparent to visible light, so it can be put into a sunscreen that protects skin from UV without leaving a thick white layer. This is the headline example demanded by the spec, and it is the one Edexcel has asked about explicitly.

It is not enough in an exam to say that nanoparticulate TiO₂ "absorbs light" or "absorbs sunlight". The marking point is specifically that it absorbs UV light from the sun (see Examiner Report Insights below).

Silver nanoparticles as antibacterials. Bulk silver is a relatively unreactive shiny metal. Silver nanoparticles, with their very high SA:V, are reactive enough to disrupt bacterial cells and are added to wound dressings, deodorants, and some clothing fabrics.

Catalysts. Many industrial catalysts are used in nanoparticulate form because the rate of a reaction at a solid catalyst depends on the surface area available. A given mass of catalyst converted into nanoparticles can have many times the activity of the same mass as a bulk lump.

Drug delivery. Nanoparticles can be designed to carry drug molecules inside the body, release them at the target site, and pass through barriers that larger particles cannot. Their small size and high SA:V make this practical.

Sensors and electronics. The unusual optical and electrical behaviour of nanoparticles is used in some sensors, displays, and self-cleaning surfaces.

In every case, the rule is the same: the property that makes the nanoparticle useful is a consequence of its tiny size and high SA:V, not a different chemistry from the bulk substance.

Worked example 1 — surface area of an 80 nm cube (matches 1CH0/2F June 2024 Q1(c))

A nanoparticle is modelled as a cube of side length l = 80 nm. Calculate its surface area.

Formula: Surface area = 6 × l²

*Working:*

  • l² = 80 × 80 = 6 400 nm²
  • Surface area = 6 × 6 400 = 38 400 nm²

You can express this in standard form as 3.84 × 10⁴ nm². For the 2-mark Edexcel calculation, the answer 38 400 nm² (with units) is sufficient, with one mark for the method (6 × 80²) and one for the correct value.

Worked example 2 — SA:V ratio of a 60 nm cube (matches 1CH0/2H June 2022 Q1(c))

A nanoparticle is modelled as a cube of side length l = 60 nm. Show that the surface area to volume ratio of this cube is 1:10.

*Step 1 — Surface area:*

  • l² = 60 × 60 = 3 600 nm²
  • Surface area = 6 × l² = 6 × 3 600 = 21 600 nm²

*Step 2 — Volume:*

  • Volume = l³ = 60 × 60 × 60 = 216 000 nm³

*Step 3 — Ratio (surface area : volume):*

  • 21 600 : 216 000

Step 4 — Simplify. Divide both sides by the smaller value (21 600):

  • 21 600 / 21 600 = 1
  • 216 000 / 21 600 = 10

So SA:V = 1 : 10, as required.

A useful sanity-check: from the general result SA:V = 6 / l, we get 6 / 60 = 0.1 = 1/10, which gives the same 1:10 ratio. This shortcut is worth knowing — but in a "show that" question you should still write out the surface area, the volume, and the simplification of the ratio, because each step is likely to carry a mark.

Comparison — bulk vs nanoparticulate behaviour

PropertyBulk materialNanoparticulate form
Particle sizeLarger than ~100 nm; usually visible or microscopic1–100 nm in at least one dimension
SA:V ratioLowVery high (roughly 10⁶ times higher than for a 1 mm particle of the same shape)
ReactivityLowerOften much higher
Catalytic activity per gramLowerHigher, because more atoms are at the surface as active sites
Optical behaviourOften opaque (e.g. white TiO₂)Can be transparent to visible light while still absorbing UV (TiO₂ sunscreens)
Biological behaviourStays outside cells in most casesSmall enough to enter cells; useful for drug delivery, but a possible risk

Mathematical Skills

The two main calculations Edexcel uses for this spec point come from the Use ratios; calculate surface areas and volumes of cubes strand. The formulae you need are:

`` Surface area of a cube = 6 × l² Volume of a cube = l³ SA:V ratio = 6l² : l³ → simplify to 6 : l → divide both sides to get 1 : (l/6) ``

*Procedure for an SA:V "show that" question:*

  1. Calculate the surface area using 6 × l².
  2. Calculate the volume using l³.
  3. Write the ratio surface area : volume.
  4. Divide both sides by the surface area to get 1 : something (or by the volume to get something : 1, whichever matches the form the question asks for).
  5. Check your answer against the target ratio stated in the question.

If side lengths are given in nm, express your final values with the correct unit (nm² for area, nm³ for volume). The ratio itself is dimensionless, so no units are written after it.

Standard form is helpful for very small or very large numbers — for example a 1 nm cube has surface area 6 nm² = 6 × 10⁻¹⁸ m² and volume 1 × 10⁻²⁷ m³. You will not normally be asked to convert nm to m for this calculation, but you should recognise that 1 nm = 1 × 10⁻⁹ m if a question requires it.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2H — 2019 June Examiner Report: When asked why nanoparticles of titanium dioxide are used in sunscreens, "in most cases, candidates scored just 1 mark as they started to describe two reasons and did not give linked detail … candidates were not specific enough with their answers and just stated that sunlight or light is absorbed rather than the UV light from the sun."

The two clear messages from this report are:

  1. Be specific. It is not enough to say "absorbs light" or "absorbs sunlight". The mark requires you to say it absorbs UV light from the sun (or simply ultraviolet light). UV is the part of the spectrum that damages skin, and that is exactly why TiO₂ nanoparticles are used in sunscreen.
  2. Give linked detail. If you start a reason, finish it. A linked answer is something like: "Nanoparticulate TiO₂ has a very high surface area to volume ratio, so it absorbs UV light from the sun effectively while remaining transparent to visible light, which protects the skin without looking white." That is one fully developed reason — examiners credit linked detail over two half-finished reasons.

How Properties of Nanoparticles Appears in Exams

This spec point is one of the most predictable openers in Paper 2. NLM data shows it appearing almost exclusively at the very beginning of Paper 2 (Q1 or Q2) in two main forms:

  • Short 1–2 mark explanations of why a nanoparticulate material has a particular property or use (e.g. sunscreens, catalysts, antibacterial coatings).
  • Multi-step 3-mark calculations of surface area, volume, or SA:V ratio for a cube of given side length.

The command words you can expect are Calculate, Explain, and State.

Specific sittings from NLM:

  • 1CH0/2F — 2024 June, Q1(c): "Calculate the surface area of this nanoparticle", given an 80 nm cube (2 marks). The method is 6 × l² as worked through above.
  • 1CH0/2H — 2022 June, Q1(c): A 60 nm cube is given, and candidates are asked to "Show that the surface area : volume ratio for this cube is 1:10" (3 marks). The three marks are for surface area, volume, and the simplification step.

The Foundation tier paper tends to favour the surface-area calculation alone. The Higher tier paper tends to push into full SA:V ratio questions with the "show that" framing, where every line of working has to appear because the answer is already given.

Command Words table

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateA short factual answer (e.g. "TiO₂") with no explanation1
CalculateA numerical answer with working shown; include the correct unit2–3
ExplainReasoning that links cause to effect — for example, link high SA:V to a particular property and then to a use2–3
Show thatAll steps of the calculation must appear because the final answer is given; no working = no marks2–3
DescribeA short prose answer covering the key features (e.g. how SA:V changes with size)1–3
Key Takeaways
  1. A nanoparticle has at least one dimension between 1 and 100 nm.
  2. Nanoparticles have very high surface area to volume (SA:V) ratios compared with the bulk form of the same substance.
  3. For a cube of side length l: surface area = 6l², volume = l³, so SA:V = 6/l. As l gets smaller, SA:V gets larger.
  4. A higher SA:V means more atoms are exposed at the surface, which is why nanoparticles tend to be more reactive, more effective catalysts, and more biologically active.
  5. Titanium dioxide nanoparticles are used in sunscreens because they absorb UV light from the sun while staying transparent to visible light — say "UV light", not just "light" or "sunlight" (1CH0/2H — 2019 June examiner report).
  6. Silver nanoparticles are used as antibacterials; nanoparticulate catalysts are used in industry; nanoparticles are also being developed for drug delivery and in sensors and electronics.
  7. The chemistry of a nanoparticulate substance is the same as the bulk substance — it is the size and SA:V that change the useful properties, not the elements involved.
  8. Calculation skills required by Edexcel for this spec point: surface area of a cube (6 × l²), volume of a cube (l³), and simplifying a surface area : volume ratio.
  9. For an 80 nm cube: surface area = 6 × 80² = 38 400 nm² (1CH0/2F — 2024 June Q1(c) style).
  10. For a 60 nm cube: surface area = 21 600 nm², volume = 216 000 nm³, SA:V = 21 600 : 216 000 = 1 : 10 (1CH0/2H — 2022 June Q1(c) style).
  11. "Show that" questions only score full marks if every step of the working is written down, because the final answer is already in the question stem.
  12. Expect this spec point at the very beginning of Paper 2 — Q1 or Q2 — as either a short explanation question or a 3-mark calculation.
9.5.1.3 Risks F+H ▼

Risks

Syllabus point 9.37C: Explain the possible risks associated with some nanoparticulate materials. Foundation + Higher tier · Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only · Paper 2.

Nanoparticles are valued because their tiny size and huge surface-area-to-volume ratio give them properties that bulk materials do not have — they can be highly reactive, can pass through structures that bulk particles cannot, and can carry useful cargo such as drug molecules. The same properties that make them useful, however, are also what make them potentially risky. This spec point is not asking you to recall a list of catastrophic accidents; it is asking you to explain why we are cautious about using materials whose long-term behaviour in the body and in the environment is not yet fully understood.

This is a qualitative spec point — there are no calculations. Marks are won and lost almost entirely on whether you can link a specific risk to a specific consequence, and whether you can say something more precise than "they are bad for you".

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
NanoparticleA particle with at least one dimension on the nanometre scale (typically 1–100 nm), small enough to behave very differently from the bulk form of the same material
Surface-area-to-volume ratioThe ratio of a particle's outer surface area to its volume; this rises sharply as particle size falls, making nanoparticles unusually reactive
NanotechnologyThe use of structures and devices built at the nanometre scale, including nanoparticulate materials in cosmetics, sunscreens, medicines and electronics
RiskThe chance that a hazard causes harm; in this spec point, risk refers to the chance that nanoparticles damage health or the environment
CatalyseTo speed up a chemical reaction without being used up; nanoparticles may catalyse unwanted reactions inside cells

Why nanoparticles raise new safety questions

The starting point for every answer here is that nanoparticles behave differently from the bulk material because they are extremely small and have a very large surface-area-to-volume ratio. That same surface area is where reactions happen, where particles bind to cell membranes, and where they may interact with biological molecules. When you write about risks, you are essentially explaining that the useful properties of nanoparticles — they are tiny enough to slip through barriers, and they are reactive enough to do new chemistry — are also the risky properties, because we cannot fully predict what they will do inside a living body or in the wider environment.

A second reason for caution is the novelty of the technology. Nanoparticulate materials in cosmetics, sunscreens and medicines have not been in widespread use for long, so we do not yet have decades of data on long-term effects. Examiners reward this point explicitly (see Examiner Report Insights below).

Specific risks you should be able to name

The spec wants you to be able to explain — not just list — the possible risks. The risks below are all sensible chemistry points that follow from what nanoparticles are and how they behave. For full marks at GCSE, each risk needs to be linked to a consequence: "they enter the body" is not enough; you need "they enter the body and could catalyse reactions or damage cells".

  • Penetration through the skin. Nanoparticles are small enough to pass through pores in the skin that block ordinary bulk particles. This matters for cosmetics and sunscreens, where nanoparticles of titanium dioxide and zinc oxide sit on the skin in large numbers.
  • Passage into the bloodstream. Once nanoparticles have crossed the skin, the lining of the lungs (if inhaled) or the gut (if ingested), they can enter the bloodstream and be carried to organs that bulk particles never reach, such as the brain or the liver.
  • Catalysis of unexpected reactions in the body. Because nanoparticles have an unusually high surface-area-to-volume ratio, they can act as catalysts. Inside cells, they may speed up reactions that the body does not want to happen, producing harmful products or damaging biological molecules.
  • Accumulation in cells and tissues. Nanoparticles that are not broken down or excreted can build up over time. Accumulation in cells may disrupt normal cell function or trigger inflammation.
  • Inhalation into the lungs. Nanoparticles released as dust or aerosols can be breathed deep into the lungs, past the defences that filter out larger particles. From there they may pass into the bloodstream or stay lodged in lung tissue.
  • Environmental release. Nanoparticles washed off the skin or released from products during disposal can enter rivers, soils and aquatic food chains, where their long-term effects on wildlife are not yet fully known.
  • Novelty of the technology. Nanotechnology is relatively new, so we do not yet know the long-term consequences of widespread exposure — there is no decades-long dataset.

How to turn a risk into a full-mark answer

This is the single most important skill for 9.37C. Examiner reports show that candidates routinely lose marks by stating a risk without explaining its consequence. The pattern below is what the mark scheme rewards.

Vague answer (typically 0 marks)One-mark answerTwo-mark answer (risk + consequence)
"They are bad for you""They can penetrate the skin""Nanoparticles can penetrate the skin and pass into the bloodstream, where they may catalyse unwanted reactions in cells"
"They harm the body""They can enter the body""Nanoparticles can be inhaled into the lungs and then enter the bloodstream, accumulating in organs over time"
"They cause health problems""Nanotechnology is new""Nanotechnology is new, so the long-term effects on health are not yet known and unexpected problems may emerge later"
"They are dangerous""They get into cells""Nanoparticles can enter cells and disrupt or catalyse reactions inside them, damaging the cell"

Notice that every two-mark answer has the same shape: [specific risk] + [linking word: "and", "so", "which means"] + [specific consequence]. Practise writing answers in this shape until it is automatic.

Why "harms the body" scores zero

Examiners are explicit that answers like "harms the body" or "causes health problems" gain no marks. The reason is that the question command word is explain, and a phrase like "harms the body" is a re-statement of the question, not an explanation. The examiner needs to see (i) what the nanoparticle physically does (enters the skin, enters cells, accumulates) and (ii) why that matters (catalyses unwanted reactions, damages cells, has unknown long-term effects). Either half alone is incomplete; together they earn the marks.

Worked example — building a 2-mark "Explain" answer

> Explain one possible risk of using nanoparticles in sunscreen. (2 marks)

Step 1 — Pick a specific, chemistry-grounded risk that links to the product. Sunscreen sits on the skin, so penetration through the skin is the natural choice.

Step 2 — Write the risk in concrete terms: "Nanoparticles in sunscreen are small enough to penetrate the skin."

Step 3 — Add the consequence: "…and can pass into the bloodstream, where they may catalyse unwanted reactions in cells."

Step 4 — Read it back and check it is one sentence with a clear cause-and-effect link.

Model answer: "Nanoparticles in sunscreen are small enough to penetrate the skin and pass into the bloodstream, where they may catalyse unwanted reactions in cells." (2 marks: 1 for the risk, 1 for the linked consequence.)

Worked example — a "novelty" answer

> Suggest why some people are concerned about the use of nanoparticles in cosmetics. (2 marks)

A strong novelty-based answer:

"Nanotechnology is a new area of science, so we do not yet know the long-term effects of nanoparticles on health, and unexpected problems may only emerge after many years of use." (1 mark for "new / long-term effects not yet known", 1 mark for the consequence "unexpected problems may emerge later".)

This second worked example matters because it shows that you do not always have to talk about skin or bloodstream — the novelty argument is just as valid, and examiners specifically reward it.

Examiner Report Insights

  • 1CH0/2H — June 2019 Examiner Report: "Where candidates did not score, it was often as their answers were vague, for example that it might harm the body or cause health problems... Where candidates did score full marks, it was often for recognising that nanotechnology is new and so therefore we don't yet know that consequences... or that the nanoparticles might pass into the body and could change or catalyse reactions in the body." This is the single clearest steer from Edexcel on what they want: vague = no marks; novelty argument OR a body-penetration-plus-consequence argument = full marks.
  • 1CH0/2H — June 2022 Examiner Report: "many candidates just made a simple point about a risk associated with nanoparticles such as 'they can penetrate through the skin' without further consequence and so could score 1 mark." The pattern from 2019 was repeated in 2022 — half-formed answers scored half marks. To get the second mark, you must add the consequence ("and then pass into the bloodstream", "and may catalyse reactions in cells", etc.).

The two reports together make the assessment pattern obvious: this is a risk + consequence spec point, not a memory-recall list. You can choose any sensible risk, but you must always pair it with what that risk leads to.

How Risks Appears in Exams

This SP appears on Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H) only, because Section 9 (Separate Chemistry 2) is examined exclusively on Paper 2.

  • Position and type: Often near the beginning of Paper 2 (Q1 or Q2), as a short opener.
  • Mark tariff: Typically a 2-mark "Explain" item.
  • Common stems: "Explain one possible risk of using nanoparticles in [cosmetics / sunscreens / medicines]" or "Suggest why some people are concerned about the use of nanoparticles."
  • What gains marks: A specific risk plus its consequence — for example, penetration through the skin and then entry into the bloodstream / catalysis of unexpected reactions in cells / accumulation in cells. The "novelty of nanotechnology — long-term effects unknown" argument also scores full marks when paired with its consequence.
  • What loses marks: Vague statements such as "harms the body", "is bad for you", or "causes health problems"; or a single half-statement such as "they can penetrate the skin" with nothing after it (this scores 1 of 2 marks).

The command word "State" can also appear with this content (often a 1-mark item naming a single risk), but the more demanding "Explain" items are where the 2-mark risk-plus-consequence structure is needed.

Command Words table

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateA single named risk; no explanation needed1
ExplainA specific risk linked to its consequence — cause and effect2
SuggestA plausible risk-and-consequence the candidate has worked out, not necessarily one they have memorised2
Key Takeaways
  1. Nanoparticles raise new safety concerns because their tiny size and huge surface-area-to-volume ratio let them behave very differently from the bulk material.
  2. The same properties that make nanoparticles useful (small enough to pass through barriers, reactive enough to do new chemistry) are what make them potentially risky.
  3. Specific risks you should be able to name: penetration through the skin, passage into the bloodstream, catalysis of unexpected reactions inside the body, accumulation in cells, inhalation into the lungs, environmental release, and the novelty of the technology.
  4. For full marks on an "Explain" item you must link a specific risk to a specific consequence — for example, "penetrate the skin and pass into the bloodstream where they may catalyse reactions in cells".
  5. Vague answers like "harms the body" or "is bad for you" score zero — examiner reports are explicit about this.
  6. A half-formed answer such as "they can penetrate the skin" with no consequence scores only 1 of 2 marks.
  7. The novelty argument is also a valid full-marks route: nanotechnology is new, so long-term effects on health are not yet known and unexpected problems may emerge later.
  8. This SP is examined only on Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H), often near the start of the paper (Q1 or Q2) as a 2-mark "Explain" item.
  9. The SP is Separate Chemistry only (1CH0) — it is not on Combined Science (1SC0).
  10. There is no calculation in this SP — marks are won purely on the precision and structure of your written answer.
  11. Practise the shape [specific risk] + [linking word] + [specific consequence] until it is automatic; almost every mark scheme on this spec point follows it.
  12. Choose your example to fit the context in the question — sunscreens and cosmetics fit "skin penetration"; medicines and inhaled products fit "bloodstream / lungs"; widespread consumer products fit "novelty / long-term effects unknown".
9.5.2.1 Ceramics, Polymers, Composites & Metals F+H ▼

Ceramics, Polymers, Composites & Metals

Spec point 9.38C: "Compare, using data, the physical properties of glass and clay ceramics, polymers, composites and metals." Foundation + Higher tier, Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only, Paper 2.

The whole point of this spec point is the comparison. You are not expected to memorise long lists of values, but you must be able to read a data table of properties, spot which material has the highest or lowest value of each property, link the property back to the underlying structure and bonding, and then choose a suitable material for a given job. That last step — material selection — is what examiners reward, and it almost always carries the mark in this section of the paper.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
CeramicA hard, brittle, non-metallic and non-polymeric solid formed by heating a material (clay or sand-based) until particles fuse together. Includes clay ceramics and glass ceramics.
Clay ceramicA ceramic made by shaping wet clay and then firing it in a kiln at high temperature (around 1000–1200 °C). Examples: pottery, porcelain, bricks.
Glass ceramicA ceramic made by melting silicon dioxide-based mixtures and cooling them so that the structure is largely non-crystalline (amorphous). Soda-lime glass and borosilicate glass are the two main examples.
Soda-lime glassGlass made by heating a mixture of sand (SiO₂), sodium carbonate and limestone (calcium carbonate). Used for windows and bottles because it is cheap and easy to shape.
Borosilicate glassGlass made by melting sand with boron trioxide. Has a higher melting point than soda-lime glass and a lower thermal expansion, so it resists thermal shock — used in oven-proof labware (e.g. Pyrex).
PolymerA long-chain molecule made from many small repeating monomer units joined by covalent bonds. The bulk solid is held together by intermolecular forces between chains.
Thermosoftening polymerA polymer with no covalent cross-links between chains; softens and melts on heating so it can be re-shaped (e.g. poly(ethene), poly(propene)).
Thermosetting polymerA polymer with extensive covalent cross-links between chains; does not soften on heating because the cross-links hold the structure rigid (e.g. Bakelite, melamine, epoxy resins).
CompositeA material made from two or more different materials combined together: a matrix (the bulk/binder) that surrounds reinforcement (the fibres or particles that strengthen it). The composite has properties that neither component has on its own.
Matrix (in a composite)The continuous phase that surrounds the reinforcement and transfers load to it (e.g. epoxy resin in fibreglass, cement paste in concrete).
Reinforcement (in a composite)The fibres or particles dispersed in the matrix that give the composite its strength or stiffness (e.g. glass fibres, carbon fibres, gravel and sand in concrete).
MetalAn element (or alloy) made of positive ions in a sea of delocalised electrons. The delocalised electrons give the characteristic metallic properties: conductivity, malleability, ductility, lustre.
AlloyA mixture of a metal with at least one other element (often another metal). Generally harder than the pure metal because different-sized atoms distort the regular lattice and stop layers sliding.
DensityMass per unit volume (units: g cm⁻³ or kg m⁻³). Tells you how heavy a given volume of the material will be.
HardnessResistance to scratching, indentation or wear. Hard materials do not deform plastically when something is pressed into them.
BrittlenessA measure of how easily a material breaks without deforming first. Brittle materials shatter; the opposite is tough.
MalleabilityAbility to be hammered or rolled into thin sheets without breaking.
DuctilityAbility to be drawn out into a wire.
Thermal conductivityRate at which heat passes through a material. High in metals; very low in ceramics and polymers (which are therefore good thermal insulators).
Electrical conductivityAbility to carry an electric current. High in metals (delocalised electrons); essentially zero in ceramics, polymers and most composites.
DurabilityAbility to resist wear, weathering and chemical attack over a long lifetime. Ceramics and metals are generally durable; polymers can degrade in sunlight; iron and steel rust.

The five material classes — structure and bonding origin of their properties

Clay ceramics are made by firing wet clay (a fine-grained aluminosilicate). During firing, water is driven off and the particles partially fuse, leaving a hard, rigid structure of strong ionic-covalent bonds with a porous, often crystalline microstructure. Strong directional bonds resist any rearrangement of atoms, so clay ceramics are hard, very stiff, electrically insulating, thermally insulating and have very high melting points — but they are also brittle, because there are no free electrons and no slip planes; once a crack starts, the bonds along the crack tip simply break instead of bending.

Glass ceramics are also based on a network of strong silicon–oxygen covalent bonds, but the structure is amorphous (no long-range crystalline order) because the melt has been cooled too quickly for the SiO₄ tetrahedra to arrange into a regular lattice. This gives glass its transparency, smoothness and chemical inertness, and a high melting point — but, like clay ceramics, glass is brittle because the rigid covalent network cannot deform.

Polymers are long covalent chains held to each other by intermolecular forces (in thermosofteners) or by additional covalent cross-links (in thermosets). Because the forces between chains are weak compared with ionic, covalent network or metallic bonding, polymers have low density, low melting points, are flexible, are electrical insulators and are thermal insulators. Thermosofteners can be melted and re-shaped; thermosets cannot — they char and decompose instead, because the cross-links are full covalent bonds that have to break.

Composites combine two materials so that the strengths of one compensate for the weaknesses of the other. The matrix surrounds the reinforcement and transfers load into it. Fibreglass is glass fibres (very strong in tension, but brittle on their own) embedded in a polymer resin matrix (light and tough, but not very stiff on its own); the composite is light, stiff and strong, and does not shatter. Carbon fibre reinforced polymer (CFRP) uses carbon fibres in a polymer matrix and is even stiffer and lighter — used in aircraft and high-end bicycles. Concrete is sand and gravel (the reinforcement) in a hardened cement paste (the matrix); strong in compression but weak in tension, which is why structural concrete is further reinforced with steel bars. Wood is a natural composite of cellulose fibres in a lignin matrix.

Metals consist of positive metal ions in a lattice through which delocalised electrons move freely. The delocalised electrons are what give metals their high electrical and thermal conductivity (electrons carry charge and kinetic energy). The non-directional metallic bonding also lets layers of ions slip past each other when a force is applied, which is why metals are malleable and ductile rather than brittle. Most metals also have high density (closely packed heavy ions) and high melting points (strong metallic bonding), though there are exceptions — sodium and potassium are soft and low-melting, aluminium is light.

Centrepiece comparison table — the five material classes

The table below is the central piece of revision for this spec point. Values are typical ranges that you might see in an exam data table.

PropertyClay ceramics (e.g. porcelain)Glass ceramics (e.g. soda-lime glass)Polymers (e.g. poly(ethene))Composites (e.g. fibreglass / concrete)Metals (e.g. steel, copper, aluminium)
Density (g cm⁻³)High (~2.3–3.9)Medium (~2.4–2.5)Low (~0.9–1.4)Variable — typically low–medium (~1.5–2.4 for fibreglass; ~2.4 for concrete)High (~2.7 Al, ~7.9 Fe, ~8.9 Cu)
Melting point (°C)Very high (~1200–1800)High (~600–1500)Low (~120–250)Limited by matrix; typically lower than ceramics/metalsHigh (~660 Al, ~1085 Cu, ~1538 Fe)
HardnessVery hard, resistant to scratchingHard but scratchableSoft, easily scratchedHard surface, but depends on matrix and reinforcementHard (especially alloys); pure metals softer
Brittleness / toughnessBrittle — shatters without warningBrittle — shattersTough/flexible — bends rather than breaksTough — combination of matrix + fibres resists crack growthTough — malleable and ductile, deforms before breaking
Electrical conductivityInsulatorInsulatorInsulatorInsulator (unless reinforced with carbon fibres / metal mesh)Excellent conductor
Thermal conductivityLow (insulator)Low (insulator)Very low (insulator)Low–mediumHigh
Chemical durability / corrosion resistanceExcellent — chemically inertExcellent — chemically inertGood, but can degrade in UV / harsh solventsGood — matrix protects reinforcementVariable — Fe rusts, Al passivates with an oxide layer, Au inert
Workability / shapingShaped wet, then fired (irreversible)Shaped while molten, sets on coolingEasily moulded, extruded; thermosofteners can be re-shapedCast around the reinforcement, then curedCast, rolled, drawn, machined; can be re-melted and reused
CostLow (clay is cheap and abundant)Low–mediumLow (mass-produced from oil-based monomers)Medium–high (fibreglass), high (CFRP), low (concrete)Medium (steel, aluminium) to very high (Ti, precious metals)
Typical usesBricks, tiles, pottery, kitchenware, electrical insulators on pylonsWindows, bottles, oven-proof labware, optical fibresPlastic bags, bottles, pipes, electrical cable insulation, kitchenwareAircraft wings & bodywork, boat hulls, bicycle frames (CFRP); buildings, bridges (concrete); roofing (fibreglass)Wires (Cu), structural beams (steel), aircraft (Al alloys), cars, food cans
Why it has these propertiesStrong ionic-covalent network; no free electrons; no slip planesAmorphous covalent SiO₂ network; no free electronsLong chains held by weak intermolecular forcesTwo materials combine to give hybrid behaviour: matrix transfers load, fibres carry itSea of delocalised electrons; non-directional metallic bonding lets layers slip

Worked example — picking a material from a property table

A typical exam stem gives you a table like the one above and asks you to choose a material for a specific job. Work through the requirements one at a time.

*Example.** *Suggest, with a reason, the most suitable material from the table below for the outer coating of an underground electrical cable.

MaterialDensity / g cm⁻³Melting point / °CElectrical conductivityHardnessFlexibility
A — clay ceramic2.51500poorhighbrittle
B — soda-lime glass2.41000poormediumbrittle
C — poly(ethene)0.9130poorlowvery flexible
D — copper8.91085excellentmediummalleable

Required properties of a coating for an underground electrical cable:

  1. Electrical insulator — otherwise the cable would short to the soil (rules out D, copper).
  2. Flexible — the cable must bend without cracking when laid (rules out A and B, which are brittle).
  3. Durable, water-resistant and chemically inert — buried in damp soil for decades.

Answer: C — poly(ethene). It is an electrical insulator, flexible (so it bends with the cable instead of shattering), low density (so the cable is light to handle) and chemically inert to water and soil. The single mark in this kind of question is awarded for naming the material AND justifying it against the data in the table — naming alone usually scores zero.

Why composites are different

Composites are the only class on the spec where the properties are deliberately engineered by combining two materials. The reason this works is that the matrix and the reinforcement do different jobs:

  • Matrix (resin, cement, metal) — surrounds the fibres, holds them in place, transfers any applied force into the fibres, and stops a crack jumping from one fibre to the next.
  • Reinforcement (glass fibres, carbon fibres, gravel + sand) — carries the load along its long axis, where it is very strong; the matrix stops it buckling when compressed.

The result is a material that is strong like the fibres but tough like the matrix, often at low density. This is why aircraft wings are CFRP rather than aluminium, and why reinforced concrete (steel bars in concrete) is the default structural material — the steel carries tensile loads the concrete cannot.

Examiner Report Insights

No examiner-report data available for this spec point in the current notebook. (The wider Topic 9.5 examiner-report data targets nanoparticle properties and risks rather than the bulk-material comparison in 9.38C.)

How Ceramics, Polymers, Composites & Metals Appears in Exams

This spec point is Paper 2 only (Separate Chemistry, 1CH0 only) and is examined at both Foundation and Higher tier.

  • Position and type: Near the start of Paper 2. The question is almost always presented as a data table comparing properties (density, hardness, melting point, electrical conductivity, durability) across the five material classes — ceramics, glass, polymers, composites and metals.
  • Command words: Typically "Compare", "State" and "Describe". "Compare" requires you to use words like higher than, lower than, more than, less than — never just give one value on its own.
  • Specific example: 1CH0/2F June 2021 Q1(b) — provides a table of properties for ceramics, glass, metal, and polymers and asks candidates to name a suitable material for a cable coating (1 mark).

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
StateA short factual answer (a name or value from the table); no explanation1
CompareUse comparison language (higher than, lower than, more than, less than) to relate two or more materials' properties using the data given2
DescribeA short prose answer covering the main properties or differences1–3
SuggestApply your knowledge to an unfamiliar context — e.g. choose a material for a job, justifying with data from the table1–2

Exam technique tips

  • Always quote a number or relative term from the table when you justify a choice — "copper has the highest electrical conductivity (X S m⁻¹), so it is the best choice for the wire" beats "copper conducts electricity well".
  • For Compare, give both sides of the comparison: "Material A has a higher density than material B" — not just "A has a high density".
  • Watch the command word: Suggest questions allow more freedom (any sensible material from the table with a valid reason), whereas State questions usually want a one-word answer.
  • Do not confuse brittle with weak. Ceramics are brittle but actually very strong in compression — they just snap suddenly in tension.
  • Composites usually appear as fibreglass or concrete in Foundation papers; CFRP and reinforced concrete are more likely at Higher tier.
Key Takeaways
  1. The five material classes on this spec point are clay ceramics, glass ceramics, polymers, composites and metals, and you must be able to compare their physical properties using a data table.
  2. Clay ceramics (e.g. porcelain, bricks) are hard, brittle, electrical and thermal insulators with very high melting points, made by firing clay in a kiln.
  3. Glass ceramics (e.g. soda-lime glass) are amorphous SiO₂-based solids — hard, brittle, transparent, and chemically inert, made by melting sand with sodium carbonate and limestone.
  4. Polymers are long-chain molecules with low density, low melting points, flexibility and electrical/thermal insulation; thermosofteners can be re-melted, thermosets cannot because of covalent cross-links.
  5. Composites combine a matrix (binder) with a reinforcement (fibres or particles) to give properties neither component has on its own — e.g. fibreglass (glass fibres in resin), CFRP, concrete (sand + gravel in cement), wood (cellulose fibres in lignin).
  6. Metals are made of positive ions in a sea of delocalised electrons, giving them high electrical and thermal conductivity, malleability, ductility, high density and high melting points.
  7. Density is lowest in polymers, medium in ceramics and composites, and highest in metals — the underlying reason is how heavy the atoms are and how closely they are packed.
  8. Electrical conductivity is high in metals (delocalised electrons) and essentially zero in ceramics, glass, polymers and most composites.
  9. Hardness is highest in clay ceramics and alloys; brittleness is highest in ceramics and glass; metals and polymers deform rather than shatter.
  10. To choose a material for a job: list the properties the job requires, then use the table to eliminate any material that fails on a property, then name the survivor and justify with values from the table.
  11. Composites work because the matrix and reinforcement do different jobs — the fibres carry the load, the matrix transfers force into them and stops cracks spreading.
