  12. In Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H), this spec point is examined with "Compare", "State" and "Describe" command words around a data table, often as a single-mark material-selection question (e.g. 1CH0/2F June 2021 Q1(b) — cable coating).
9.5.3.1 Effective Use of Materials F+H ▼

Effective Use of Materials

Spec point 9.39C: Explain why the properties of a material make it suitable for a given use and use data to select materials appropriate for specific uses. Foundation + Higher, Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only, Paper 2.

This is a data-handling spec point dressed up as a chemistry question. You will almost always be given a properties table — density, melting point, electrical or thermal conductivity, hardness, tensile strength, corrosion resistance, cost — and asked to pick a material for a stated use, then justify the choice using the numbers. The chemistry knowledge you need is mostly behind the scenes: it tells you which property column actually matters for the job in the question.

Key Definitions

TermDefinition
PropertyA measurable characteristic of a material (e.g. density, melting point, electrical conductivity, hardness, tensile strength)
DensityMass per unit volume of a material, usually quoted in g/cm³ or kg/m³
Melting pointThe temperature at which a solid material turns into a liquid; high melting points are needed for high-temperature uses
Electrical conductivityA measure of how easily electric current passes through a material; metals are high, polymers and ceramics are low
Thermal conductivityA measure of how easily heat passes through a material; needed high for cookware bases, low for insulation
HardnessResistance of a material to scratching, denting or wear
Tensile strengthThe maximum stretching force a material can withstand before it breaks
Corrosion resistanceThe ability of a material to resist chemical attack by air, water or other substances
Effective useChoosing a material whose property profile matches all the demands of the application, not just one

The selection logic — what examiners want to see

Picking a material is a two-step argument. First, decide which properties the job demands. Second, scan the data table and choose the material whose numbers best satisfy that demand profile. A full-mark justification almost always quotes specific numerical values from the data, not just the word "high" or "low".

The standard rubric an examiner uses goes like this:

  1. State a property the use requires (e.g. "the coating must be a good electrical insulator").
  2. Quote the data column that captures that property.
  3. Compare the candidate material's value to the alternatives in the table.
  4. Repeat for any second or third property that matters (e.g. flexibility, cost, corrosion resistance).
  5. Conclude with the named material.

A one-property answer ("aluminium is best because it is light") usually scores 1 mark. A two- or three-property answer that quotes numbers and rules out the alternatives ("aluminium is best because its density of 2.7 g/cm³ is the lowest in the table, and its melting point of 660 °C is high enough for the operating range, and it does not corrode like iron at 7.9 g/cm³") scores the full tariff.

Reading a properties data table

A typical Edexcel data table for this spec point looks like this:

MaterialDensity (g/cm³)Melting point (°C)Electrical conductivityHardnessCost (£/kg)
Copper8.961085very highmedium7.00
Aluminium2.70660highlow–medium2.00
Steel7.851450mediumhigh0.80
PVC (polymer)1.40100 (softens)very low (insulator)low1.20
Ceramic (alumina)3.952050very low (insulator)very high5.00

You will not be expected to memorise these numbers — they will be in the question. What you do need is the skill to interpret each column. A "very low" electrical conductivity means the material is an insulator, which is good for a plug case but bad for a wire. A high melting point is essential for a saucepan base but irrelevant for a window frame. A low density is essential for an aircraft body but unhelpful for a pendulum weight.

Common student errors

  • Picking on one property only. Choosing aluminium just because it is light, when the question is about a saucepan handle (which needs low thermal conductivity, so a polymer is better).
  • Confusing electrical conductivity direction. Saying a polymer is suitable for a wire because it is an electrical insulator — exactly backwards.
  • Ignoring cost or corrosion. Two materials may both meet the technical spec; the deciding factor is the cheaper or more corrosion-resistant one.
  • Vague language. Answers like "copper has good conductivity" instead of "copper has the highest electrical conductivity at X S/m, higher than aluminium at Y S/m".
  • Failing to rule out the wrong choices. Examiners reward answers that explicitly state why the alternatives are unsuitable, not just why the chosen one is.

Worked Example 1 — Cable coating (the canonical case)

This is the question pattern that appeared as 1CH0/2F June 2021 Q1(b) — "name a suitable material for a cable coating" (1 mark), with a properties table provided.

Question: Use the table below to choose a suitable material to coat an outdoor electrical cable.

MaterialDensity (g/cm³)Electrical conductivityFlexibilityCorrosion resistanceCost (£/kg)
Copper8.96very highlowmedium7.00
Aluminium2.70highmediummedium2.00
PVC1.40very low (insulator)highhigh1.20
Steel7.85mediumlowlow (rusts)0.80

*Property demand profile for a cable coating:*

  1. Must be an electrical insulator — the coating must not conduct, or it will short the cable and shock the user.
  2. Must be flexible — the cable must bend without the coating cracking.
  3. Must resist corrosion — the cable is outdoors in rain and air.
  4. Lower density is helpful (lighter cables to install) but not critical.
  5. Low cost is helpful.

*Working:*

  • Copper, aluminium and steel are all metals with non-zero electrical conductivity, so they would all short the cable. They are ruled out on property 1 alone.
  • PVC has "very low" conductivity — it is an insulator, which is what we need.
  • PVC is also flexible (high), corrosion resistant (high) and cheap (£1.20/kg).

Answer: PVC. It is an electrical insulator (conductivity very low) so it stops the current escaping the wire, it is flexible so the cable can bend without cracking, and it resists corrosion outdoors. The metals in the table all conduct electricity and would be dangerous as a coating.

Worked Example 2 — Water pipe under a road

Question: Choose a material for a water pipe that runs underground beneath a road.

MaterialDensity (g/cm³)HardnessCorrosion resistance (in damp soil)Cost (£/m)Flexibility
Copper8.96mediumhigh12.00low
Steel7.85highlow (rusts)4.00very low
PVC1.40lowvery high2.00high
Lead11.34lowmedium9.00medium

*Property demand profile:*

  1. Must resist corrosion — buried in damp soil for decades.
  2. Must withstand the weight of road traffic — needs reasonable hardness/strength, OR a small enough diameter that the soil around it carries the load.
  3. Should be cheap — pipes are bought by the kilometre.
  4. Lead is ruled out on toxicity grounds (lead leaches into drinking water), even though the data table does not show that.

*Working:*

  • Steel rusts in damp soil — fails corrosion test.
  • Copper resists corrosion and is hard enough but is six times more expensive than PVC.
  • PVC has "very high" corrosion resistance at £2.00/m. Its low hardness is offset by being buried in compacted soil that carries the road load, and modern pipe-laying uses PVC for exactly this reason.

Answer: PVC. Its corrosion resistance is very high, so it will not degrade in damp soil; it is the cheapest option at £2.00/m; and the surrounding soil supports it against traffic loading. Copper also resists corrosion but is six times more expensive and so not justified for an underground bulk pipe. Steel rusts and lead is toxic to drinking water.

Worked Example 3 — Cookware base

Question: Choose a material for the base of a frying pan.

MaterialDensity (g/cm³)Melting point (°C)Thermal conductivityHardnessCost (£/kg)
Aluminium2.70660very highmedium2.00
Copper8.961085highestmedium7.00
Stainless steel7.851450mediumhigh3.00
PVC1.40100 (softens)very lowlow1.20
Ceramic3.952050very lowvery high5.00

*Property demand profile:*

  1. Must have a high melting point — far above cooking temperatures (~250 °C).
  2. Must have high thermal conductivity — to spread heat evenly across the base and cook food without hot-spots.
  3. Must be hard enough to resist scratching from utensils.
  4. Reasonable cost.

*Working:*

  • PVC softens at 100 °C — it would melt on the hob. Ruled out.
  • Ceramic has a very high melting point but very low thermal conductivity, so heat would not spread evenly. Ruled out for the base (it is used for the plate you serve onto, not the cooking surface).
  • Copper has the highest thermal conductivity but is expensive at £7.00/kg.
  • Aluminium has very high thermal conductivity, a melting point of 660 °C (well above cooking range), and costs only £2.00/kg.
  • Stainless steel has medium thermal conductivity but the highest hardness — many real pans use a stainless-steel outer layer with an aluminium or copper core to combine both.

Answer: Aluminium. Its thermal conductivity is very high (so heat spreads evenly across the base), its melting point of 660 °C is far above cooking temperatures, and at £2.00/kg it is the cheapest of the technically suitable options. Copper conducts slightly better but is more than three times more expensive. PVC melts on the hob and ceramic does not spread heat evenly.

Worked Example 4 — Aerospace panel (Higher-tier flavour)

Question: Choose a material for an external panel of an aircraft.

MaterialDensity (g/cm³)Tensile strength (MPa)Corrosion resistanceCost (£/kg)
Aluminium alloy2.80400high3.00
Steel7.85500low0.80
Carbon-fibre composite1.60700very high25.00
Titanium4.50900very high30.00

*Property demand profile:*

  1. Low density — every kilogram saved on an aircraft reduces fuel burn.
  2. High tensile strength — must withstand flight loads.
  3. Corrosion resistance — exposed to rain, humidity, salt at altitude.
  4. Cost matters but is secondary to weight on a passenger aircraft.

*Working:*

  • Steel has the cheapest cost but its density of 7.85 g/cm³ is nearly three times that of aluminium alloy, and it corrodes. Ruled out for external panels.
  • Titanium has the highest strength and best corrosion resistance but at £30/kg is ten times the cost of aluminium alloy.
  • Carbon-fibre composite has the lowest density (1.60 g/cm³) and the second-highest strength (700 MPa) and is corrosion-resistant — but at £25/kg it is reserved for high-stress structural parts.
  • Aluminium alloy is the workhorse: density 2.80 g/cm³, tensile strength 400 MPa, corrosion resistant, £3.00/kg.

Answer: Aluminium alloy. Its low density of 2.80 g/cm³ keeps the aircraft light and fuel-efficient, its tensile strength of 400 MPa is sufficient for non-structural skin panels, and it resists corrosion at altitude. Carbon-fibre composite is lighter and stronger but eight times more expensive and is used only on the highest-stress parts. Steel is too dense and rusts.

How property demand maps to common uses — quick reference

UseKey properties demandedLikely material from a data table
Electrical wire (core)High electrical conductivity, ductileCopper, sometimes aluminium
Electrical cable coatingElectrical insulator, flexible, weatherproofPolymer (PVC)
Water pipe (underground)Corrosion resistant, cheapPolymer (PVC); sometimes copper
Saucepan baseHigh melting point, high thermal conductivityAluminium, copper, stainless steel
Saucepan handleHigh melting point, low thermal conductivityPolymer (heat-resistant); wood
Window frameCorrosion resistant, light, rigidAluminium; uPVC
Aircraft body panelLow density, high tensile strength, corrosion resistantAluminium alloy; composite
Surgical implantCorrosion resistant in body fluids, non-toxic, strongTitanium; stainless steel
Drill bit / cutting toolVery high hardness, high melting pointCeramic; tungsten carbide
Lightweight bike frameLow density, high strength, stiffAluminium alloy; composite

Cost and environmental factors

Examiners often slip a cost column into the table. Once two or more materials meet the technical demands, cost is the tie-breaker. The chosen material does not have to be the cheapest in the table — it has to be the cheapest of the ones that work. A surgical implant cannot be made from steel just because steel is £0.80/kg; the implant has to work first, so corrosion resistance ranks above cost.

Environmental factors (recyclability, toxicity in use, end-of-life disposal) are sometimes referenced in higher-tier questions. They link to 4.1.6.1 Life Cycle Assessment — picking aluminium over steel saves fuel during decades of aircraft service, even though aluminium production itself is energy-intensive.

Examiner Report Insights

No examiner-report data available for this spec point in the current notebook.

(The wider Topic 9.5 examiner reports concentrate on nanoparticle properties and risks — see the 9.5.1.2 and 9.5.1.3 notes — rather than on the material-selection logic of 9.39C.)

Mathematical Skills

No specific maths skill code is cited for 9.39C, but reading and comparing numerical data in a properties table is the recurring quantitative element on this spec point. The numerical skills you actually use are:

  • Comparing magnitudes of figures across rows of a table (which column entry is the largest?)
  • Comparing magnitudes with different units (g/cm³ versus kg/m³ — convert if needed)
  • Spotting orders of magnitude (a conductivity of 10⁷ S/m versus 10⁻¹⁵ S/m is a 22-order-of-magnitude gap — "very high" versus "very low")
  • Calculating cost per unit length or per unit mass when two columns combine (£/kg × kg used)

How Effective Use of Materials Appears in Exams

Paper 2 (1CH0/2F and 1CH0/2H): This spec point is Paper 2 only, Separate Chemistry only, and it appears near the start of the paper — typically as a low-tariff (1–2 mark) follow-up to a properties data table presented under spec point 9.38C.

  • 1CH0/2F June 2021 Q1(b) — "name a suitable material for a cable coating" (1 mark), given a properties table. The expected answer is a polymer with low electrical conductivity (insulator).
  • Typical command words used: Suggest, Explain, Justify (and on Foundation, sometimes just Name).
  • Higher-tier versions add a second mark for justification using data from the table — the difference between a 1-mark "name" answer and a 2-mark "explain using the data" answer.

Command Words

Command WordWhat It RequiresTypical Mark Tariff
Name / StateA one-word material choice from the data1
SuggestA reasonable choice plus a brief reason1–2
ExplainA choice plus property-by-property reasoning2–3
JustifyA choice supported by specific numerical values from the data table2–4
Key Takeaways
  1. The "effective use of materials" question is a data-handling task: you are given a properties table and must pick the material whose property profile matches the demands of a stated use.
  2. The two-step logic is: (a) decide which properties the job demands; (b) compare the data columns and choose the material that satisfies all the demands, not just one.
  3. Full-mark answers quote specific numerical values from the data table, not just words like "high" or "low".
  4. Common properties used: density, melting point, electrical conductivity, thermal conductivity, hardness, tensile strength, corrosion resistance, cost.
  5. Cable coatings need an electrical insulator (polymer like PVC), not a conductor — this is the canonical 1CH0/2F June 2021 Q1(b) example.
  6. Water pipes, window frames and outdoor uses prioritise corrosion resistance plus low cost — polymers (PVC) usually win.
  7. Saucepan bases need high thermal conductivity (metals); saucepan handles need low thermal conductivity (polymers). The same kitchen item uses two opposite property profiles.
  8. Aerospace panels prioritise low density plus high strength — aluminium alloys and carbon-fibre composites; steel is too heavy, titanium too expensive for non-critical parts.
  9. Once two materials both meet the technical demands, cost is the tie-breaker — but only after the technical properties are satisfied.
  10. Common errors: picking on one property only, confusing "good conductor" with "good insulator", failing to rule out the wrong materials, using vague language without numbers.
  11. The command word matters: Name is 1 mark; Suggest is 1–2; Explain or Justify is 2–4 and needs explicit data-quoted reasoning.
  12. This spec point is Paper 2 only and Separate Chemistry (1CH0) only — it does not appear on Combined Science (1SC0) or on Paper 1.
